GEOMETRIC REFLECTIVE BOUNDARY CONDITIONS FOR ASYMPTOTICALLY ANTI-DE SITTER SPACES Thanks: ludovic.souetre@sorbonne-universite.fr


Abstract
This article solves the initial boundary value problem for the vacuum Einstein equations with a negative cosmological constant in dimension 4 4 , giving rise to asymptotically Anti-de Sitter spaces. We introduce a new family of geometric reflective boundary conditions, which can be regarded as the homogeneous Robin boundary conditions, involving both the conformal class and the stress-energy tensor of the timelike conformal boundary. This family includes as a special case the homogeneous Neumann boundary condition, consisting of setting the boundary stress-energy tensor to zero. It also agrees, in a limit case, with the homogeneous Dirichlet boundary condition, where one fixes a locally conformally flat conformal class on the boundary, already covered in Friedrich’s pioneering work [ 38 ] . The proof of local existence and uniqueness for this family of boundary conditions relies notably on Friedrich’s framework and his extended conformal Einstein equations. These are rewritten in a tensorial formalism, rather than a spinorial one, as it facilitates the comparison with the Fefferman-Graham expansion of asymptotically Anti-de Sitter metrics. Similarly to Friedrich’s proof, our geometric boundary conditions are eventually inferred from gauge-dependent boundary conditions by means of an auxiliary system on the conformal boundary. In addition, our analysis also comprises new necessary and sufficient conditions for the unphysical fields associated to the initial data to be smooth up to the conformal boundary. Finally, the paper contains examples of asymptotically Anti-de Sitter spaces, with a focus on their conformal boundary data. This provides valuable insight into the possible shapes of boundary stress-energy tensors.



1
Introduction

1.1
The vacuum Einstein equations with a negative cosmological constant

The vacuum Einstein equations with a cosmological constant
\Lambda\in\mathbb{R}
for a
(n+1)
-dimensional Lorentzian manifold
(\mathcal{M},g)
write as

\Ric(g)=\frac{2\Lambda}{n-1}g\,,

(VE)

where
\Ric(g)
is the Ricci tensor of the Lorentzian metric
g
. The sign of the cosmological constant
\Lambda
determines not only the sign of the scalar curvature
R
and thus local geometric properties, but also the nature of the potential conformal boundary at infinity. The simplest solutions of (
VE
) are the maximally symmetric spaces which are the Minkowski space for
\Lambda=0
, the de Sitter (abbreviated to dS) space for
\Lambda>0
and the Anti-de Sitter (AdS) space for
\Lambda<0
. The latter has the remarkable property of not being globally hyperbolic due to the presence of a timelike conformal boundary. More generally, we are interested in this article in spaces with a similar timelike conformal boundary, called asymptotically Anti-de Sitter (aAdS).

The negative cosmological constant case was first investigated in the physics literature in the late 1970s and 1980s in order to determine the effects of a non-zero cosmological constant on the geometry, in particular in connection with supergravity theories. Linear scalar fields on AdS were studied by Avis, Isham and Storey
[
7
]
, Breitenlohner and Freedman
[
12
,
13
]
while perturbations of the AdS space were examined by Ashtekar and Magnon
[
6
]
, Henneaux and Teitelboim
[
48
]
. In the late 1990s, a significant surge of interest in solutions of (
VE
) with a negative cosmological constant was generated by the AdS/CFT correspondence proposed by Maldacena
[
70
]
. In broad terms, this correspondence establishes a link between a supergravity theory on the interior of aAdS spaces and a superconformal field theory on their conformal boundary.

From a mathematical perspective, the local theory of (
VE
) with
\Lambda<0
presents additional difficulties compared with the well-known case of
\Lambda=0
studied by Choquet-Bruhat
[
34
]
. A first difficulty comes from the fact that the conformal boundary of aAdS spaces is situated at infinity. A conformal embedding allows to bring it at finite distance, however this leads a priori to a singular system of equations on a bounded domain, whose boundary location has yet to be determined. A second difficulty arises from being faced with an initial boundary value problem. Indeed, it is necessary to specify both Cauchy data on an initial hypersurface and boundary conditions on the conformal boundary in order to solve the equations in a unique way. These boundary conditions must satisfy the following key points:

a)

After hyperbolic reduction through some gauge choices, the boundary conditions for the resulting analytic
1
1

1

In this paper, the opposite terms ‘analytic’ and ‘geometric’ are to be understood as ‘gauge-dependent’ and ‘gauge-independent’ respectively. It should be noted that ‘analytic’ does not refer to real analytic or holomorphic functions.
initial boundary value problem must lead to a well-posed problem. This implies that the boundary conditions have to match the number of dynamical degrees of freedom of (
VE
) which is
(n+1)(n-2)/2
, see for instance Christodoulou
[
22
, Section 2.1]
.

b)

The Einstein equations (
VE
) entail the existence of constraints which have to be satisfied initially and propagated by the evolution. To go back from the analytic to the geometric setting and thus obtain geometric existence, the boundary conditions must enable this propagation.

c)

Furthermore, the boundary conditions must make sense in any gauge and therefore have a real geometric meaning in order to ensure geometric uniqueness, see Friedrich
[
40
]
.

1.2
Previous works on local existence of aAdS spaces

Conformal methods

The use of conformal methods for the Einstein equations dates back to the work of Penrose in the 1960s. In particular, he introduced the concept of asymptotically simple spacetimes in
[
75
]
allowing the attachment of infinities through a conformal embedding of the spacetime. The metric prior to (respectively following) the conformal mapping is said to be
physical
(respectively
unphysical
). A fundamental aspect of the construction is that the physical and unphysical spacetimes share the same global causality structure. The common smoothness requirement imposed on the unphysical metric is linked to the peeling property of gravitational radiation, which gives the asymptotic behaviour of the Weyl tensor near a smooth conformal boundary. This is generally stated in the case of a vanishing cosmological constant
\Lambda=0
. Nowadays, it is however understood that smoothness of null infinity is too strong a requirement in the asymptotic flat case, see Kehrberger
[
63
]
.

In the 1970s and 1980s, conformal mappings began to be exploited in the context of partial differential equations related to general relativity. In particular, they are used in order to solve the constraints by Lichnerowicz, Choquet-Bruhat and York, see for instance
[
18
]
, and to prove global existence of some semilinear and then quasilinear wave equations, see for example Christodoulou
[
21
]
. Moreover, Friedrich derived in 1981
[
35
]
a set of smooth tensorial equations on the unphysical spacetime encoding the Einstein equations for the physical spacetime, which are known as the
conformal Einstein equations
. Indeed, these equations allow for conformal mappings with a conformal factor vanishing on subsets of the unphysical spacetime. The conformal Einstein equations led to many applications in the case of
\Lambda>0
, see for instance
[
37
]
.

The first work establishing a local existence and uniqueness theory for (
VE
) with
\Lambda<0
is the celebrated article of Friedrich of 1995
[
38
]
. It is based on an extension of the conformal Einstein equations, enlarging the connections used from Levi-Civita connections to
Weyl connections
. The resulting tensorial equations are called the
extended conformal Einstein equations
. The new gauge freedom, consisting of choosing the Weyl connection, is essential to Friedrich’s local existence result, obtained for
n=3
and a broad set of analytic boundary conditions. These boundary conditions are a priori gauge-dependent, meaning that they only make sense in a specific gauge. A second result of
[
38
]
establishes that among them, there exists one which can be interpreted geometrically as imposing the
boundary conformal class
through an auxiliary evolution system on the boundary. This is commonly referred to as the
Dirichlet boundary condition
for (
VE
) with
\Lambda<0
. Geometric local existence and uniqueness of solutions are thus ensured for this specific boundary condition.

For more details on conformal methods in general relativity and Friedrich’s works, one can refer to the comprehensive book written by Valiente Kroon
[
85
]
.

Asymptotic expansions

The seminal work of Fefferman and Graham on the ambient space construction
[
31
]
, see also the extensive book
[
32
]
, contains in particular an asymptotic expansion nearby the conformal boundary of aAdS metrics satisfying the vacuum Einstein equations (
VE
) with
\Lambda<0
. This expansion is called the
Fefferman-Graham expansion
(FG expansion). The Einstein equations leave two free data in the expansion: the leading term and part of a sub-leading term whose position in the expansion depends on
n
, the dimension of the boundary. The former corresponds to the
conformal class
and imposing it constitutes the
Dirichlet boundary condition
as seen previously. The latter is the trace-free and divergence-free part of the
boundary stress-energy tensor
and imposing it is thereby typically interpreted as the
Neumann boundary condition
.

In 2019, Enciso and Kamran
[
30
]
proved local existence and uniqueness of solutions for the vacuum Einstein equations (
VE
) with
\Lambda<0
under the Dirichlet boundary condition for all boundary dimensions
n\geq 3
, thus extending Friedrich’s result to higher dimensions. Their work is inspired by the Riemannian case studied by Graham and Lee
[
45
]
. The proof uses a peeling strategy to derive iteratively the first terms of an asymptotic expansion of the metric, akin to the FG expansion, as well as the analysis of linear fields on aAdS spaces developed in various articles listed in
Section

1.4
.

1.3
Main result

The main goal of this paper is to derive new geometric boundary conditions for which local existence and uniqueness of solutions to the geometric initial boundary value problem, arising from the vacuum Einstein equations (
VE
) with
\Lambda<0
and
n=3
, hold. To state these new boundary conditions, recall that the FG expansion of a 4-dimensional aAdS metric
\widetilde{g}
takes the following form

\widetilde{g}=\frac{1}{x^{2}}\left(dx^{2}+\mathfrak{h}_{ij}dy^{i}dy^{j}-x^{2}\mathfrak{l}_{ij}dy^{i}dy^{j}+x^{3}\mathfrak{t}_{ij}dy^{i}dy^{j}+\mathcal{O}\left({x^{4}}\right)\right)\,.

In this expansion,
x
is a boundary defining function of the conformal boundary
\mathscr{I}
,
\mathfrak{h}
is a Lorentzian metric on
\mathscr{I}
,
\mathfrak{l}
is the Schouten tensor of
\mathfrak{h}
and
\mathfrak{t}
is the boundary stress-energy tensor. The tensor
\mathfrak{t}
is trace-free and divergence-free for the metric
\mathfrak{h}
. The geometric free data on the conformal boundary is given by the class
[(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{t})]
for the following equivalence relation

(\mathfrak{h}^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})\sim(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{t})\text{ if }\exists\,\Omega\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathscr{I},\mathbb{R}_{+})\text{ such that }\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}=\Omega^{2}\mathfrak{h}\text{ and }\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}=\Omega^{-1}\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}\,.

Recall furthermore that the Cotton-York tensor
\mathfrak{y}
of
\mathfrak{h}
is defined as the dual of the Cotton tensor
\mathfrak{c}
, that is

\mathfrak{y}_{ij}:=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{i}{}^{kl}\mathfrak{c}_{klj}:=\epsilon_{i}{}^{kl}\mathfrak{D}_{[k}\mathfrak{l}_{l]j}\,,

where
\epsilon
,
\mathfrak{D}
and
\mathfrak{l}
denote respectively the volume form, the Levi-Civita connection and the Schouten tensor of
\mathfrak{h}
.

The new family of geometric reflective boundary conditions consist in imposing that, for any representative
(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{t})
of the geometric free data on the conformal boundary, the boundary stress-energy tensor
\mathfrak{t}
is proportional to the Cotton-York tensor of
\mathfrak{h}
by a fixed real constant
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
, that is

\mathfrak{t}_{ij}=\mu\,\mathfrak{y}_{ij}\,.

(1.1)

This family can be interpreted as the homogeneous Robin boundary conditions. Indeed, when
\mu=0
, (
1.1
) reduces to the homogeneous Neumann boundary condition since it sets the boundary stress-energy tensor (class) to zero. Moreover, in the limit
\mu\to\pm\infty
, (
1.1
) becomes
\mathfrak{y}_{ij}=0
. This is equivalent to imposing that the conformal class
[\mathfrak{h}]
on the conformal boundary is locally conformally flat, in agreement with the definition of the homogeneous Dirichlet boundary condition.

In this paper, we prove the local existence and uniqueness of solutions of (
VE
) with
\Lambda<0
and
n=3
for the above family of geometric boundary conditions. A rough version of our main result is the following, see
4.53
for the precise formulation.

Theorem 1.1

(Main theorem, rough version)
.

For any 3-dimensional asymptotically hyperbolic smooth solution to the Vacuum Constraint equations (
4.21
) with cosmological constant
\Lambda=-3
, satisfying a set of geometric regularity conditions on its conformal boundary and a family of compatibility conditions with the boundary conditions (
1.1
), there exists a 4-dimensional asymptotically Anti-de Sitter space which is a smooth local solution to the Vacuum Einstein equations (
VE
) with cosmological constant
\Lambda=-3
, agreeing with the initial data on some initial hypersurface, and satisfying the boundary conditions (
1.1
). Moreover, this space is locally unique.

Comments on the main theorem

•

This addresses the problem for all negative cosmological constants
\Lambda<0
since one can always reduce to the case
\Lambda=-3
by multiplying the fields by a constant.

•

The ‘geometric regularity conditions on the conformal boundary’ imposed on the initial data refer to a set of novel conditions, described in
i)
of
4.51
, for the unphysical fields to be smooth up to the conformal boundary. The ‘compatibility conditions with the boundary conditions (
1.1
)’ are the corner-type conditions defined in
4.47
.

•

The Birmingham-AdS spaces with
m=0
(which includes the AdS space) defined in
Section

3.3.3
and the aAdS spaces defined in
Section

3.3.6
are examples of aAdS spaces verifying the homogeneous Neumann boundary condition, that is (
1.1
) with
\mu=0
. To the best of our knowledge, there are very few known examples of aAdS that are solutions to (
VE
) and satisfy (
1.1
) with
\mu\neq 0
outside of the real analytic class. In
Section

3.3.7
, we give examples of such aAdS spaces constructed out of conformal boundary whose conformal class contains particular pp-wave metrics. Up to solving the geometric initial data problem, our result provides non-trivial solutions which are not real analytic. In particular, for
\mu=0
, these translate into non-real analytic solutions to the Poincaré problem, defined by Fefferman and Graham in
[
31
]
, for 3-dimensional boundaries of Lorentzian signature.

•

A physical interpretation of the boundary condition (
1.1
) in terms of Conformal Field Theory on the conformal boundary can be found in de Haro
[
27
]
.

•

After completion of this work, we became aware of the recent
2
2

2

Note that our geometric boundary conditions (
1.1
) were presented on June 10
th
, 2025 during the conference
Boundaries, stability, and singularities in general relativity - A meeting celebrating Helmut Friedrich’s 80th birthday
.
article
[
33
]
which proposes a similar type of boundary conditions.

Open problems

Our result naturally leads to several open problems.

•

Understanding how to recover solutions such as Schwarzschild-AdS and Kerr-AdS by solving the geometric initial-boundary value problem with inhomogeneous Neumann or Robin-type boundary conditions.

•

Constructing similar geometric initial data satisfying the compatibility conditions.

•

Addressing problems in higher dimensions, that is for
n\geq 4
. This article is also written in the hope that it will be useful to that end. Thus, many definitions and computations are presented in any dimension, even though our main result holds in dimension
n=3
(note that our boundary conditions (
1.1
) do not make sense in higher dimensions).

Comments on the proof

Our proof makes extensive use of Friedrich’s framework and his extended conformal vacuum Einstein equations which we will review in detail in the article. We made some modifications and improvements to it which we highlight here:

•

The analysis is written in a tensorial formalism rather than a spinorial one. This facilitates the dialogue with the FG expansion presented in
Section

3.2.2
and the consequent asymptotic expansions of the curvature tensors which we derive in
Section

3.2.3
. These are crucial to link the electromagnetic decomposition of the rescaled Weyl tensor and the boundary free data.

•

Weyl connections are systematically used, notably to define the extended version of the Friedrich scalar in
Section

4.1.2
and for the extended conformal vacuum constraint equations in
Section

4.1.4
. Similarly to the treatment of the (non-extended) conformal Einstein equations by Friedrich, see for example
[
36
]
, the extended Friedrich scalar enables to only work with equations which are regular up to the conformal boundary, not only for the evolution but also for the propagation of the constraints. This is actually a key feature of the (extended or not) conformal Einstein equations. Regarding the constraints, we find it better to write them down for Weyl connections since the gauge construction and thus the analytic initial data requires them.

•

The gauge construction, see
4.27
, only refer to the extended conformal vacuum Einstein equations and not to the vacuum Einstein equations. This removes an unnecessary extra step which discriminates the conformal boundary.

•

In
Section

4.2.3
, we follow a different strategy to find an evolution system enabling to show the propagation of the constraints. Indeed, we choose to exploit the algebraic dependencies rather than adding a multiple of certain constraints to the evolution equations. The two strategies are linked by the fact that the terms that need to be counteracted by a constraint either do not appear or are removed using the algebraic dependencies.

Similarly to the proof of Friedrich, our geometric boundary conditions (
1.1
) are eventually inferred from a minimal set of analytic boundary conditions by means of an auxiliary evolution system on the boundary, see
Section

4.3.5
.

1.4
Further works on AdS and aAdS spaces

Linear fields

In 1978, Avis, Isham and Storey
[
7
]
showed the global well-posedness of the conformal wave equation on the AdS space under the homogeneous Dirichlet and Neumann boundary conditions. Their work was generalised to the Klein-Gordon equation – also known as the massive wave equation and which is not conformally invariant – by Breitenlohner and Freedman
[
12
,
13
]
in 1982. The spectral properties of the Klein-Gordon operator on AdS and the boundary conditions leading to well-posedness were systematically studied by Ishibaldi and Wald
[
61
]
in 2004. Further analysis was established by Bachelot for the Dirac equation in 2008
[
8
]
and the Klein-Gordon equation on the Poincaré patch of the AdS space in 2011
[
9
]
.

Moving away from the exact AdS space, linear fields on aAds spaces have been investigated since the 2010s. The well-posedness of the Klein-Gordon equation on an aAdS space under the homogeneous Dirichlet boundary condition was proved by Vasy
[
86
]
and Holzegel
[
49
]
. In 2013, Warnick
[
88
]
introduced a new analytic framework by considering twisted derivatives. This allowed him to prove the well-posedness under Robin boundary conditions. See also Enciso and Kamran
[
29
]
. Decay for the Klein-Gordon equation on Kerr-Anti-de Sitter was studied by Holzegel and Smulevici
[
55
,
57
]
. In
[
59
]
, Holzegel and Warnick investigated the boundedness properties of the Klein-Gordon equation on aAdS black holes depending on the boundary conditions imposed. While these previous works focus on scalar equations, the Teukolsky equations were recently investigated by Holzegel and Graf focusing on mode stability on Kerr-Anti-de Sitter
[
41
]
and decay properties on Schwarzschild-Anti-de Sitter
[
43
]
.

Apart from reflective boundary conditions which are used in all above-mentioned works, Holzegel, Luk, Smulevici and Warnick
[
50
]
studied the global dynamics and derived decay estimates for the conformal wave equation, the linearised Bianchi equation and the Maxwell equations on a fixed AdS background in the case of dissipative boundary conditions.

In complement to initial boundary value problems, the existence of a boundary also give rise to unique continuation problems. A unique continuation statement was shown for various wave equations on aAdS spaces by Holzegel and Shao
[
51
,
52
]
. A gauge-invariant unique continuation criterion was later derived by Chatzikaleas and Shao
[
16
]
, which is also applicable in the non-linear setting. Moreover, counterexamples to unique continuation for waves were studied by Guisset and Shao
[
47
]
.

Non-linear fields

With regard to the conformal approach, Friedrich’s framework was extended in the 2010s to several cases: coupling with the Maxwell equations by Lübbe and Valiente Kroon
[
69
]
, system of conformal wave equations by Carranza and Valiente Kroon
[
15
]
, coupling with trace-free matter by Carranza, Hursit and Valiente Kroon
[
14
]
.

The spherically symmetric Einstein-Klein-Gordon system with
\Lambda<0
and under Dirichlet boundary conditions was studied by Holzegel and Smulevici who showed local existence and uniqueness of solutions in
[
54
]
and the stability of Schwarzschild-Anti-de Sitter in
[
56
]
. Holzegel and Warnick constructed solutions of the spherically symmetric Einstein-Klein-Gordon system under Robin boundary conditions in
[
58
]
.

The non-linear (in)stability of the Anti-de Sitter space, originally conjectured by Dafermos and Holzegel
[
26
]
and by Anderson
[
2
]
, was first addressed by Bizoń and Rostworowski
[
11
]
who investigated by means of numerical methods the spherically symmetric Einstein-massless scalar field system with a negative cosmological constant and reflective boundary conditions. This work was completed by Maliborski and Rostworowski who exhibited in
[
71
]
time-periodic solutions using formal series. By restricting to toy models on the Einstein cylinder, Chatzikaleas and Smulevici
[
17
]
constructed non-formal time-periodic solutions to various field equations. In addition of the study of time-periodic solutions, a substantial contribution on the instability of AdS for reflective boundary conditions was made by Moschidis who proved the instability of the spherically symmetric Einstein-null dust and Einstein-massless Vlasov systems with
\Lambda<0
in
[
73
,
74
]
.

In 2021, Shao
[
81
]
showed that partial FG expansions hold when sufficient regularity is assumed on the rescaled metric. Finally, Holzegel and Shao
[
53
]
addressed the unique continuation problem for the Einstein equations with
\Lambda<0
. They also proved the inheritance of symmetries from the conformal boundary to the bulk.

1.5
Structure of the article

In
Section

2
, we recall notions and tools which will be used later in the article. In particular, this includes manifolds with corners in
Section

2.2
and conformal geometry in
Section

2.4
.

Section

3
is dedicated to the definitions of aAdS spaces and a presentation of their fundamental properties. This section includes standard examples of aAdS spaces, giving some insight of what boundary stress-energy tensors can look like.

Section

4
is the main section of the article and contains the proof of
4.53
. We introduced the extended Conformal Vacuum Einstein equations in
Section

4.1
before using them to prove local existence of solutions for some analytic boundary conditions in
Section

4.2
. A subset of these analytic boundary conditions are then interpreted geometrically in
Section

4.3
. In
Section

4.4
, we discuss the geometric initial data problem. Finally, the different parts are combined together in
Section

4.5
leading to
4.53
.

1.6
Acknowledgements

The author would like to thank J. Smulevici for his advice and careful reading of this paper.

2
Preliminaries

In this section, we introduce the notations, notions and tools which will be used in the whole article. Most of the proofs are omitted since they are standard and can be found in the references. Only a few are detailed either because we could not find a suitable reference or due to their importance in the proof of local existence of aAdS spaces.

2.1
Notations and conventions

The symbol
:=
in an equation denotes a definition. The sign convention for the signature of Lorentzian metrics is
(-+\dots+)
. The action of a 1-form
\kappa
on a vector field
X
is denoted by
\langle\kappa,X\rangle
and the action of a vector field
X
on a smooth function
f
by
X\cdot f
or
X(f)
.

The symbols
\nabla
,
\widetilde{\nabla}
,
\overline{\nabla}
,
\breve{\nabla}
(respectively
D
,
\widetilde{D}
,
\mathfrak{D}
and
\not{D}
,
\not{\mathfrak{D}}
) denote the Levi-Civita of the
(n+1)
-dimensional metrics
g
,
\widetilde{g}
,
\overline{g}
,
\breve{g}
(respectively
n
-dimensional metrics
h
,
\widetilde{h}
,
\mathfrak{h}
and
(n-1)
-dimensional metrics
\not{h}
,
\not{\mathfrak{h}}
) whereas
\widehat{\nabla}
,
\widecheck{\nabla}
denote Weyl connections on
(n+1)
-dimensional and
n
-dimensional manifolds. Tensor fields associated to a connection are indicated with the symbol of the connection. For example,
\widehat{W}
is the Weyl tensor of
\widehat{\nabla}
.

The components of tensor fields with respect to a coordinate system are written with Greek alphabet indices for
(n+1)
-dimensional manifolds and with Latin alphabet indices for
n
-dimensional ones. Note that the Latin indices range either from
1
to
n
for Riemaniann manifolds or from
0
to
n-1
for Lorentzian manifolds. Capital Latin alphabet indices are reserved for
(n-1)
-dimensional Riemannian manifolds and range from
1
to
n-1
.

A frame field
(e_{\bf a})
defines a dual coframe field
(\omega^{\bf a})
by
\langle\omega^{\bf a},e_{\bf b}\rangle=\delta^{\bf a}{}_{\bf b}
. The connection coefficients of a connection
\nabla
with respect to a frame field are defined by
\Gamma_{\bf a}{}^{\bf c}{}_{\bf b}:=\langle\omega^{\bf c},\nabla_{e_{\bf a}}e_{\bf b}\rangle
. The frame components of tensor fields are written with the following bold indices
{\bf a,b,c,d,\dots}
(respectively
{\bf i,j,k,l,\dots}
and
{\bf A,B,C,D,\dots}
) for
(n+1)
-dimensional (respectively
n
-dimensional and
(n-1)
-dimensional) manifolds.

The Einstein summation convention is used, that is a repeated index in superscript and subscript is implicitly summed over its range. Symmetric and antisymmetric parts of a tensor field are denoted by
(\;)
and
[\;]
, respectively. Some indices can be excluded using
|\;|
. For instance,

T_{(i_{1}i_{2}|j|i_{3})}:=\frac{1}{6}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{3}}\,T_{i_{\sigma(1)}i_{\sigma(2)}ji_{\sigma(3)}}\,.

2.2
Manifolds with corners

We recall the definitions of smooth manifolds with corners as well as their basic properties. The presentation follows the lines of Melrose
[
72
]
. The main concepts are boundary hypersurfaces (see
2.6
), smooth manifolds with corners (
2.8
) and boundary defining functions (
2.9
).

Corners arise naturally in the context of initial boundary value problems as the intersection of the initial hypersurface and the boundary. Moreover, boundary defining functions are essential to define the conformal boundary and aAdS spaces.

2.2.1
Definitions

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
k,l\in\llbracket 0,N\rrbracket
. Introduce

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{N}_{k}

\displaystyle:=[0,+\infty)^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{N-k}=\{x=(x^{1},\dots,x^{N})\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\mid\forall\,j\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket,\,x_{j}\geq 0\}\,,

\displaystyle\partial_{l}\mathbb{R}^{N}_{k}

\displaystyle:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}_{k}\mid\#\{j\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket\mid x_{j}=0\}=l\}\,.

For any open set
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}_{k}
, define

\partial_{l}U:=U\cap\partial_{l}\mathbb{R}^{N}_{k}\,.

Definition 2.1
.

The algebra of smooth functions on an open set
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}_{k}
is

\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U):=\{f|_{U}\mid f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(V)\}\,

where
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
is an open set such that
U=V\cap\mathbb{R}^{N}_{k}
.

Definition 2.2
.

A map
\phi
from an open set
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}_{k}
to an open set
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}_{j}
is said to be a diffeomorphism if it is a homeomorphism and if the components of
\phi
and
\phi^{-1}
are smooth functions in
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U)
and
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(V)
respectively.

Proposition 2.3
.

Let
\phi:U\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}_{k}\to V\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}_{j}
be a diffeomorphism. Then

\forall\,l\in\llbracket 0,N\rrbracket,\quad\phi(\partial_{l}U)=\partial_{l}V\,.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a paracompact Hausdorff topological space.

•

A (N-dimensional)
chart

(\phi,\mathcal{U},k)
on
\mathcal{M}
is a homeomorphism
\phi
from an open subset
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathcal{M}
to an open subset
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}_{k}
for some
k\in\llbracket 0,N\rrbracket
.

•

Two charts
(\phi_{1},\mathcal{U}_{1},k_{1})
,
(\phi_{2},\mathcal{U}_{2},k_{2})
on
\mathcal{M}
are said to be
compatible
if either
\mathcal{U}_{1}\cap\mathcal{U}_{2}=\varnothing
or
\phi_{2}\circ(\phi_{1})^{-1}:\phi_{1}(\mathcal{U}_{1}\cap\mathcal{U}_{2})\to\phi_{2}(\mathcal{U}_{1}\cap\mathcal{U}_{2})
is a diffeomorphism.

•

A (N-dimensional)
atlas
on
\mathcal{M}
is a family of (N-dimensional) charts
(\phi_{a},\mathcal{U}_{a},k_{a})_{a\in A}
such that

i)

any two charts in the family are compatible,

ii)

the family covers
\mathcal{M}
that is
\mathcal{M}=\bigcup_{a\in A}\mathcal{U}_{a}
.

•

An atlas on
\mathcal{M}
is said to be
maximal
if it contains all charts compatible with each chart of the atlas.

Definition 2.5
.

A N-dimensional
t-manifold
is a connected, paracompact and Hausdorff topological space endowed with a maximal N-dimensional atlas.

Definition 2.6
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a t-manifold.

•

A
coordinate system at

p\in\mathcal{M}
is a chart
(\phi,\mathcal{U},k)
such that
\phi(p)=0
.

•

The
set of
l
-corner points

\partial_{l}\mathcal{M}
is defined by

\partial_{l}\mathcal{M}:=\{p\in\mathcal{M}\mid\exists\,(\phi,\mathcal{U},k)\text{ coordinate system at $p$ such that }k=l\}\,.

For
l=0
, the notation
\mathring{\mathcal{M}}
is used instead of
\partial_{0}\mathcal{M}
.

•

The
boundary
of
\mathcal{M}
is defined by

\partial\mathcal{M}:=\overline{\partial_{1}\mathcal{M}}=\bigcup_{l\geq 1}\partial_{l}\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathring{\mathcal{M}}\,,

•

A
boundary hypersurface

\mathcal{S}\subset\partial\mathcal{M}
is the closure of a connected component of
\partial_{1}\mathcal{M}
,

•

The algebra of smooth function on
\mathcal{M}
is defined by

\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M}):=\{f:\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{R}\mid\forall\text{ chart }(\phi,\mathcal{U},k),f\circ\phi^{-1}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\phi(\mathcal{U}))\}\,.

Example
.

The cube
[0,1]^{3}
is a t-manifold. The sets of
0
-corner points,
1
-corner points,
2
-corner points and
3
-corner points are respectively the interior
(0,1)^{3}
, the faces, the edges and the corners.
∎

Definition 2.7
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a N-dimensional t-manifold. A connected subset
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathcal{M}
is said to be a
submanifold
if for all
p\in\mathcal{S}
, there exists a coordinate system
(\phi,\mathcal{U},k)
at
p
, an invertible matrix
G\in GL_{N}(\mathbb{R})
and an open neighbourhood
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
of
0_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}
such that

\phi(\mathcal{S}\cap\mathcal{U})=G(\mathbb{R}^{N^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}\times\{0_{\mathbb{R}^{N-N^{\prime}}}\})\cap V

for some
k^{\prime}\leq N^{\prime}\leq N
.

Definition 2.8
.

A
smooth manifold with corners
is a t-manifold
\mathcal{M}
such that all boundary hypersurfaces of
\mathcal{M}
are submanifolds in the sense of
2.7
.

In particular, a
smooth manifold with boundary
(respectively
smooth manifold without boundary
) is a smooth manifold with corners
\mathcal{M}
such that
\partial_{l}\mathcal{M}=\varnothing
for
l\geq 2
and
\partial_{1}\mathcal{M}\neq\varnothing
(respectively
\partial_{l}\mathcal{M}=\varnothing
for
l\geq 1
).

2.2.2
Boundary defining functions

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a smooth manifold with corners and
\mathcal{I}
be a non-empty union of disjoint boundary hypersurfaces.

Definition 2.9
.

A
local boundary defining function
of
\mathcal{I}
is a smooth function
x
on an open set
\mathcal{U}
with
\mathcal{I}\cap\mathcal{U}\neq\varnothing
such that

i)

x\geq 0
on
\mathcal{U}
,

ii)

\mathcal{I}\cap\mathcal{U}=\{p\in\mathcal{U}\mid x(p)=0\}
,

iii)

for all
p\in\mathcal{I}\cap\mathcal{U}
, there exists a coordinate system at
p
with
x
as first coordinate.

If
\,\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{M}
,
x
is said to be a
global boundary defining function
of
\mathcal{I}
.

Remark
.

If
x
is a local boundary defining function of
\mathcal{I}
on an open set
\mathcal{U}
then one deduces from
iii)
that

\forall\,p\in\mathcal{I}\cap\mathcal{U},\quad dx|_{p}\neq 0\,.

(2.1)

This is the common version of
iii)
.
∎

Proposition 2.10
.

There exists a global boundary defining function of
\mathcal{I}
.

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
x\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{U})
be a local boundary defining function of
\mathcal{I}
and
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{U},\mathbb{R})
be a smooth function on
\mathcal{U}
. Then the function
f/x
extends smoothly on
\mathcal{U}\cap\mathcal{I}
if and only if
f
vanishes on
\mathcal{U}\cap\mathcal{I}
.

Remark
.

Since
f,x
are smooth functions on
\mathcal{U}\setminus\mathcal{I}
and
x
nowhere vanishes on
\mathcal{U}\setminus\mathcal{I}
by
ii)
of
2.9
, the function
f/x
is always well-defined and smooth on
\mathcal{U}\setminus\mathcal{I}
.
∎

Proof.

\Longrightarrow
This is evident by evaluating
f=x(f/x)
on
\mathcal{I}
.

\Longleftarrow
Let
p\in\mathcal{U}\cap\mathcal{I}
. By
iii)
of
2.9
, there exists a coordinate system
(\phi,\mathcal{V},k)
at
p
such that
\phi=(x,y^{1},\dots,y^{N-1})
. One can assume that
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{U}
. Since
x
and
f
are smooth, there exist smooth extensions
\overline{x}
and
\overline{f}
of respectively
x\circ\phi^{-1}
and
f\circ\phi^{-1}
on an open set
W
of
\mathbb{R}^{N}
with
\phi(\mathcal{V})=W\cap\mathbb{R}^{N}_{1}
(see
2.1
). Thanks to (
2.1
), one can assume that
\overline{x}
nowhere vanishes on
W\setminus\phi(\mathcal{V}\cap\mathcal{I})
up to reducing
W
. Hence
\overline{f}/\overline{x}
is well-defined and smooth on
W\setminus\phi(\mathcal{V}\cap\mathcal{I})
. Finally,
\overline{f}/\overline{x}
extend smoothly on
\phi(\mathcal{V}\cap\mathcal{I})
using Taylor expansions of
f
and
f
vanishing on
\mathcal{U}\cap\mathcal{I}
. Hence
\overline{f}/\overline{x}
is a smooth extension on
W
of
f/x\circ\phi^{-1}
, which implies that
f/x
is well-defined and smooth on
\mathcal{V}
.
∎

A consequence of the previous lemma and a key property of boundary defining functions is that they are all ‘linearly equivalent’ as stated below.

Corollary 2.12
.

Let
x\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{U})
and
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{U}^{\prime})
be two local boundary defining functions of
\mathcal{I}
such that
\mathcal{U}\cap\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\neq\varnothing
. Then the function
\Theta:=x^{\prime}/x
is a smooth positive function on
\mathcal{U}\cap\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
.

Example
.

If
x
is a local boundary defining function then so is
x(2+x)
but not
x^{2}
.
∎

Proof.

A fortiori,
x
is a local boundary defining function on
\mathcal{U}\cap\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
and
x^{\prime}
vanishes on
\mathcal{U}\cap\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{I}
. By application of
2.11
,
x^{\prime}/x
extends smoothly on
\mathcal{U}\cap\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{I}
. By
i)
and
ii)
of
2.9
, it is clear that
x^{\prime}/x
is positive on
(\mathcal{U}\cap\mathcal{U}^{\prime})\setminus\mathcal{I}
. Using L’Hôpital’s rule, one can deduce that
x^{\prime}/x
is also positive on
\mathcal{U}\cap\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{I}
.
∎

2.2.3
Polyhomogeneous functions

We define polyhomogeneous functions in a simpler version than Melrose in
[
72
]
since it will be sufficient for our case. Polyhomogeneous functions is the right class of functions to consider for the metric components of aAdS spaces in arbitrary dimension as we will see in
3.8
.

Definition 2.13
.

An
index set

E
is a subset of
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that

i)

\forall\,s\in\mathbb{R},\;E_{\leq s}:=\{(r,k)\in E\mid r\leq s\}
is finite,

ii)

(r,k)\in E\implies\forall\;0\leq l\leq k,\;(r,l)\in E
,

iii)

(r,k)\in E\implies(r+1,k)\in E
.

Example
.

The minimal index set containing an element
(r,k)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{N}_{0}
is
E=\{(s,l)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{N}_{0}\mid s-r\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\,l\leq k\}
.
∎

Definition 2.14
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a smooth manifold with corners of dimension
N
,
\mathcal{I}
be a union of disjoint boundary hypersurfaces and
E
be an index set. A map
f:\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{R}
is said to be
polyhomogeneous of class

m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\cup\{+\infty\}
with respect to
\mathcal{I}
and with index set
E
if

i)

f\in\mathcal{C}^{m}(\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathcal{I},\mathbb{R})
,

ii)

for all local boundary defining function
x
of
\mathcal{I}
and for all coordinate system
(\phi=(x,y),\mathcal{V},k)
at a point
p\in\mathcal{I}\cap\mathcal{U}
with
x
as first coordinate, there exists a family of functions
(f_{(r,k)})_{(r,k)\in E}\in\mathcal{C}^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{N-1},\mathbb{R})
such that for all
0\leq j\leq m
, for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{N-1}
with
|\alpha|\leq m-j
and for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
,

(x\partial_{x})^{j}\partial_{y}^{\alpha}\left(f\circ\phi^{-1}-\sum_{(r,k)\in E_{\leq s}}f_{(r,k)}x^{r}(\log x)^{k}\right)=o\left(x^{s}\right)\,.

The algebra of such functions is denoted by
\mathcal{C}^{m}_{\text{phg}}(\mathcal{M}\mid\mathcal{I},E)
.

Remark
.

Index sets are designed so that if
ii)
holds for one boundary defining function then it remains true for all.
∎

2.3
Curvature tensors

Let
(\mathcal{M},g)
be a smooth pseudo-Riemannian manifold with corners of dimension
N\geq 2
. Let us recall the definitions and the properties of the standard curvature tensors associated to the Levi-Civita connection
\nabla
of
g
, namely the Riemann, Ricci, Weyl, Schouten, Cotton and Bach tensors. Smoothness is assumed for simplicity but note that the definitions hold for lower regularity:
\mathcal{C}^{2}
for Riemann, Ricci, Schouten and Weyl;
\mathcal{C}^{3}
for Cotton;
\mathcal{C}^{4}
for Bach.

2.3.1
The Riemann and Ricci tensors

Definition 2.15
.

The
Riemann tensor
is defined by

R^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\nu}X^{\beta}Y^{\mu}Z^{\nu}:=\left(\nabla_{Y}\nabla_{Z}X-\nabla_{Z}\nabla_{Y}X-\nabla_{[Y,Z]}X\right)^{\alpha}

(2.2)

for all smooth vector fields
X,Y,Z
on
\mathcal{M}
. The
Ricci tensor
and the
scalar curvature
are the following contractions of the Riemann tensor

R_{\mu\nu}:=R^{\alpha}{}_{\mu\alpha\nu},\qquad R:=g^{\mu\nu}R_{\mu\nu}\,.

(2.3)

The Riemann tensor of a Levi-Civita connection:

•

enjoys the following symmetries

\displaystyle R^{\alpha}{}_{\beta(\mu\nu)}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle R_{(\alpha\beta)\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=0\,,

•

verifies the first and second Bianchi identities

\displaystyle R^{\alpha}{}_{[\beta\mu\nu]}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(2.4a)

\displaystyle\nabla_{[\lambda}R^{\alpha}{}_{|\beta|\mu\nu]}

\displaystyle=0\,.

(2.4b)

It follows that

R_{\alpha\beta\mu\nu}=R_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}\,,\qquad R_{[\mu\nu]}=0\,,\qquad\nabla^{\mu}R_{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{\nu}R\,.

Definition 2.16
.

For
N\geq 3
,
(\mathcal{M},g)
is said to be
Einstein
if it is solution to the
Vacuum Einstein equations (VE)

R_{\mu\nu}=\frac{2\Lambda}{N-2}g_{\mu\nu}\,,

(
VE
)

where
\Lambda\in\mathbb{R}
is the cosmological constant. If
N=2
,
(\mathcal{M},g)
is said to be Einstein if its scalar curvature
R
is constant.

2.3.2
The Weyl, Schouten and Cotton tensors

Instead of working with the Riemann and Ricci tensors, one can use the Weyl and Schouten tensors. This enables to define a practical orthogonal decomposition of the Riemann tensor, see (
2.6
). Moreover, these tensors are particularly useful in conformal geometry as we will see in
Section

2.4
where the definitions will be extended to Weyl connections.

In what follows, it will be assumed that
N\geq 3
.

Definition 2.17
.

The
Weyl tensor
, whose components are denoted by
W^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\nu}
, is defined as the totally trace-free part of the Riemann tensor
R^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\nu}
. The
Schouten tensor
is defined by

L_{\mu\nu}:=\frac{1}{N-2}\left(R_{\mu\nu}-\frac{R}{2(N-1)}g_{\mu\nu}\right)\,.

(2.5)

Note that the Weyl (respectively Schouten) tensor of a Levi-Civita connection has the same symmetries than its Riemann (respectively Ricci) tensor. Furthermore, the following decomposition holds

R^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\nu}=W^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\nu}+2S_{\beta[\mu}{}^{\alpha\sigma}L_{\nu]\sigma}\,,

(2.6)

where

S_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\mu\nu}:=\delta_{\alpha}{}^{\mu}\delta_{\beta}{}^{\nu}+\delta_{\alpha}{}^{\nu}\delta_{\beta}{}^{\mu}-g_{\alpha\beta}g^{\mu\nu}\,.

(2.7)

Remark
.

Thanks to metric compatibility, one has
\nabla_{\gamma}S_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\mu\nu}=0
.
∎

Definition 2.18
.

The
Cotton tensor
is defined by

C_{\lambda\mu\nu}:=\nabla_{\lambda}L_{\mu\nu}-\nabla_{\mu}L_{\lambda\nu}\,.

(2.8)

By definition, the Cotton tensor verifies

C_{(\alpha\beta)\gamma}=0\,,\qquad C_{[\alpha\beta\gamma]}=0\,.

(2.9)

The second property is called the third Bianchi identity.

Remark
.

If
(\mathcal{M},g)
is Einstein then its Cotton tensor vanishes identically.
∎

The first and second Bianchi identities (
2.4
) rewrite as

\displaystyle W^{\alpha}{}_{[\beta\mu\nu]}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(2.10a)

\displaystyle\nabla_{[\lambda}W^{\alpha}{}_{|\beta|\mu\nu]}

\displaystyle=S_{\beta[\lambda}{}^{\alpha\rho}C_{\mu\nu]\rho}\,.

(2.10b)

The contractions of the second Bianchi identity give

\displaystyle\nabla_{\alpha}W^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=(N-3)C_{\mu\nu\beta}\,,

(2.11a)

\displaystyle C_{\lambda\mu}{}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=0\,.

(2.11b)

Furthermore, one can prove the fourth Bianchi identity which writes

\nabla_{[\lambda}C_{\mu\nu]\alpha}=-W^{\beta}{}_{\alpha[\lambda\mu}L_{\nu]\beta}\,.

(2.12)

Its trace give

\nabla^{\alpha}C_{\mu\nu\alpha}=0\,.

(2.13)

2.3.3
The Bach tensor

Definition 2.19
.

The
Bach tensor
is defined by

B_{\mu\nu}:=\nabla^{\lambda}C_{\lambda\mu\nu}+L^{\lambda\sigma}W_{\mu\lambda\nu\sigma}\,.

(2.14)

By definition, one can check that

B_{[\mu\nu]}=0\,\qquad B^{\mu}{}_{\mu}=0\,.

Remark
.

If
(\mathcal{M},g)
is Einstein then its Bach tensor vanishes identically.
∎

Furthermore, one has

\nabla^{\mu}B_{\mu\nu}=(N-4)L^{\lambda\sigma}C_{\nu\lambda\sigma}\,.

2.4
Conformal geometry

Conformal geometry is based on the freedom to positively rescale metrics. It plays an important role to study the conformal boundary of aAdS spaces and is at the heart of the (extended) conformal Einstein equations.

We start by reviewing the basic definitions of confomorphisms and conformal structures in
Section

2.4.1
, before defining Weyl connections and their associated curvature tensors in
Section

2.4.2
. Weyl connections are extensively used in the extended conformal Einstein equations presented in
Section

4.1
. Conformally invariant tensors are introduced in
Section

2.4.4
and we recall remarkable conformal structures in
Section

2.4.5
, stressing the importance of the Weyl and Cotton tensors.

We then introduce special fields of conformal structures such as conformal Killing fields in
Section

2.4.6
and conformal geodesics in
Section

2.4.7
. The congruences of timelike conformal geodesics studied in
Section

2.4.8
will be used later to construct a gauge for the extended conformal vacuum Einstein equations, see
Section

4.2.2
. Conformal Killing fields will only be used in connection with boundary stress-energy tensors in
Section

3.4
.

The main reference of this section is
[
85
, Section 5]
, see also
[
39
]
for a detailed discussion on conformal geodesics.

2.4.1
Confomorphisms and conformal structures

Definition 2.20
.

A
confomorphism
between two smooth pseudo-Riemannian manifolds
(\mathcal{M}_{1},g_{1})
and
(\mathcal{M}_{2},g_{2})
is a diffeomorphism
\phi:\mathcal{M}_{1}\to\mathcal{M}_{2}
such that there exists a smooth positive function
\Omega\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M}_{1},\mathbb{R}^{\star}_{+})
verifying
\phi^{\star}g_{2}=\Omega^{2}g_{1}
. The function
\Omega
is called the
conformal factor
associated to
\phi
.

A confomorphism is said to be an
isometry
if
\Omega=1
and a
conformal rescaling
if
\mathcal{M}_{1}=\mathcal{M}_{2}
and
g_{2}=\Omega^{2}g_{1}
.

Definition 2.21
.

Two metrics
g
and
\widetilde{g}
on a smooth manifold
\mathcal{M}
are said to be
conformally related
if they are related by a conformal rescaling. This defines an equivalence relation on metrics on
\mathcal{M}
.

Definition 2.22
.

A
conformal structure
is a pair
(\mathcal{M},[\widetilde{g}])
where
\mathcal{M}
is a smooth manifold and
[\widetilde{g}]
is a conformal class of metrics on
\mathcal{M}
.

2.4.2
Weyl connections

Weyl connections are the natural extension of Levi-Civita connections in the framework of conformal geometry. In what follows, consider a conformal structure
(\mathcal{M},[\widetilde{g}])
of dimension
N\geq 3
.

Definition 2.23
.

A
Weyl connection
for
(\mathcal{M},[\widetilde{g}])
is a connection
\widehat{\nabla}
such that

i)

it is torsion-free: for all smooth vector fields
X
,
Y
on
\mathcal{M}

\widehat{\Sigma}(X,Y):=\widehat{\nabla}_{X}Y-\widehat{\nabla}_{Y}X-[X,Y]=0\,,

(2.15)

ii)

for all
g\in[\widetilde{g}]
, there exists a smooth covector field
\widehat{\kappa}_{g}\in T^{\star}\mathcal{M}
verifying

\widehat{\nabla}_{\alpha}g_{\beta\gamma}=-2\,(\widehat{\kappa}_{g})_{\alpha}\,g_{\beta\gamma}\,.

(2.16)

Remarks
.

•

If (
2.16
) holds for a representative
g\in[\widetilde{g}]
then it holds for all representatives. Indeed, if
\overline{g}=\Omega^{2}g
is another representative, it suffices to take
\widehat{\kappa}_{\overline{g}}=\widehat{\kappa}_{g}+d(\ln\Omega)
.

•

The Levi-Civita connection
\nabla
of a representative
g\in[\widetilde{g}]
is a Weyl connection.

•

From (
2.16
), one deduces that
\widehat{\nabla}_{\alpha}g^{\beta\gamma}=2(\widehat{\kappa}_{g})_{\alpha}g^{\beta\gamma}
and
\widehat{\nabla}_{\sigma}S_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\mu\nu}=0
where
S_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\mu\nu}
is defined by (
2.7
). ∎

Proposition 2.24
.

Let
g\in[\widetilde{g}]
be a representative and
\kappa
be a smooth covector field on
\mathcal{M}
. There exists a unique Weyl connection
\widehat{\nabla}
for
(\mathcal{M},[\widetilde{g}])
such that the covector field associated to
\widehat{\nabla}
with respect to
g
by (
2.16
) is equal to
\kappa
.

Proof.

The Weyl connection is entirely characterised by the following modified Koszul formula

\displaystyle 2g(\widehat{\nabla}_{X}Y,Z)

\displaystyle=X\cdot g(Y,Z)+g(Y,[Z,X])+2\langle\kappa,X\rangle g(Y,Z)

\displaystyle\quad+Y\cdot g(Z,X)+g(Z,[X,Y])+2\langle\kappa,Y\rangle g(Z,X)

\displaystyle\quad-Z\cdot g(X,Y)-g(X,[Y,Z])-2\langle\kappa,Z\rangle g(X,Y)\,,

(2.17)

for all smooth vector fields
X,Y,Z
on
\mathcal{M}
.
∎

The Riemann and Ricci tensors of a Weyl connection are defined similarly as these of a Levi-Civita connection.

Definition 2.25
.

Let
\widehat{\nabla}
be a Weyl connection for the conformal structure
(\mathcal{M},[\widetilde{g}])
. Its
Riemann tensor

\widehat{R}^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\nu}
is defined by

\widehat{R}^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\nu}X^{\beta}Y^{\mu}Z^{\nu}:=\left(\widehat{\nabla}_{Y}\widehat{\nabla}_{Z}X-\widehat{\nabla}_{Z}\widehat{\nabla}_{Y}X-\widehat{\nabla}_{[Y,Z]}X\right)^{\alpha}

(2.18)

for all smooth vector fields
X,Y,Z
on
\mathcal{M}
. Its
Ricci tensor
is defined by

\widehat{R}_{\mu\nu}:=\widehat{R}^{\alpha}{}_{\mu\alpha\nu}\,.

The Riemann and Ricci tensors of a Weyl connection:

•

enjoy the following algebraic properties

\displaystyle\widehat{R}^{\alpha}{}_{\beta(\mu\nu)}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(2.19a)

\displaystyle g_{\alpha(\gamma}\widehat{R}^{\alpha}{}_{\beta)\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{N}\widehat{R}_{[\mu\nu]}g_{\gamma\beta}\,,

(2.19b)

\displaystyle\widehat{R}_{[\alpha\beta]}

\displaystyle=N\widehat{\nabla}_{[\alpha}(\widehat{\kappa}_{g})_{\beta]}\,,

(2.19c)

for any representative
g\in[\widetilde{g}]
,

•

verify the first and second Bianchi identities

\displaystyle\widehat{R}^{\alpha}{}_{[\beta\mu\nu]}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\widehat{\nabla}_{[\lambda}\widehat{R}^{\alpha}{}_{|\beta|\mu\nu]}

\displaystyle=0\,.

Such as for Levi-Civita connections in
Section

2.3.2
, let us introduce the Weyl, Schouten and Cotton tensors of Weyl connections.

Definition 2.26
.

The
Weyl tensor

\widehat{W}^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\nu}
of a Weyl connection
\widehat{\nabla}
is defined as the totally trace-free part of its Riemann tensor
\widehat{R}^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\nu}
with respect to any representative metric
g\in[\widetilde{g}]
.

Definition 2.27
.

The
Schouten tensor

\widehat{L}_{\alpha\beta}
of a Weyl connection
\widehat{\nabla}
is defined by

\widehat{L}_{\alpha\beta}:=\frac{1}{N-2}\left(\widehat{R}_{(\alpha\beta)}-\frac{g^{\mu\nu}\widehat{R}_{\mu\nu}}{2(N-1)}g_{\alpha\beta}\right)-\frac{1}{N}\widehat{R}_{[\alpha\beta]}\,,

(2.21)

for any representative
g\in[\widetilde{g}]
, the expression being independent of the choice of representative.

Proposition 2.28
.

The Riemann tensor of a Weyl connection
\widehat{\nabla}
verifies the following decomposition

\widehat{R}^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\nu}=\widehat{W}^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\nu}+2S_{\beta[\mu}{}^{\alpha\sigma}\widehat{L}_{\nu]\sigma}\,,

(2.22)

where
S_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\mu\nu}
is defined by (
2.7
).

Definition 2.29
.

The
Cotton tensor
of a Weyl connection
\widehat{\nabla}
is defined by

\widehat{C}_{\lambda\mu\nu}:=\widehat{\nabla}_{\lambda}\widehat{L}_{\mu\nu}-\widehat{\nabla}_{\mu}\widehat{L}_{\lambda\nu}\,.

(2.23)

By definition, one has

\widehat{C}_{(\alpha\beta)\gamma}=0\,.

Proposition 2.30
.

For any Weyl connection
\widehat{\nabla}
, one has

\displaystyle\widehat{L}_{[\alpha\beta]}

\displaystyle=-\widehat{\nabla}_{[\alpha}(\widehat{\kappa}_{g})_{\beta]}\,,

(2.24a)

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{[\lambda\mu\nu]}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(2.24b)

where
\widehat{\kappa}_{g}
is the covector field associated to
\widehat{\nabla}
with respect to a representative
g\in[\widetilde{g}]
. The second equation is called the third Bianchi identity.

Using the decomposition (
2.22
), the first and second Bianchi identities for a Weyl connection
\widehat{\nabla}
can be written under the form

\displaystyle\widehat{W}^{\alpha}{}_{[\beta\mu\nu]}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(2.25a)

\displaystyle\widehat{\nabla}_{[\lambda}\widehat{W}^{\alpha}{}_{|\beta|\mu\nu]}

\displaystyle=S_{\beta[\lambda}{}^{\alpha\sigma}\widehat{C}_{\mu\nu]\sigma}\,.

(2.25b)

The contractions of the second Bianchi (
2.25b
) identity give

\displaystyle\widehat{\nabla}_{\alpha}\widehat{W}^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=(N-3)\widehat{C}_{\mu\nu\beta}\,,

(2.26a)

\displaystyle g^{\mu\nu}\widehat{C}_{\lambda\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=0

(2.26b)

where
g\in[\widetilde{g}]
is any representative. The fourth Bianchi identity also holds

\widehat{\nabla}_{[\lambda}\widehat{C}_{\mu\nu]\alpha}=-\widehat{W}^{\beta}{}_{\alpha[\lambda\mu}\widehat{L}_{\nu]\beta}\,.

(2.27)

2.4.3
Weyl connection changes

Remember that the difference between any two connections
\acute{\nabla}
and
\grave{\nabla}
on
\mathcal{M}
is measured by their transition tensor defined as follows

Q_{\mu}{}^{\sigma}{}_{\nu}:=\acute{\Gamma}_{\mu}{}^{\sigma}{}_{\nu}-\grave{\Gamma}_{\mu}{}^{\sigma}{}_{\nu}\,.

For instance, for all smooth (1,1)-tensor fields
X^{\mu}{}_{\nu}
, one has

\acute{\nabla}_{\alpha}X^{\beta}{}_{\gamma}=\grave{\nabla}_{\alpha}X^{\beta}{}_{\gamma}+Q_{\alpha}{}^{\beta}{}_{\mu}X^{\mu}{}_{\gamma}-Q_{\alpha}{}^{\mu}{}_{\gamma}X^{\beta}{}_{\mu}\,.

It follows that the torsion and Riemann tensors of
\acute{\nabla}
and
\grave{\nabla}
, defined as in (
2.15
) and (
2.18
), are linked by

\displaystyle\acute{\Sigma}_{\alpha}{}^{\gamma}{}_{\beta}

\displaystyle=\grave{\Sigma}_{\alpha}{}^{\gamma}{}_{\beta}+2Q_{[\alpha}{}^{\gamma}{}_{\beta]}\,,

\displaystyle\acute{R}^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\grave{R}^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\nu}+2\acute{\nabla}_{[\mu}Q_{\nu]}{}^{\alpha}{}_{\beta}-2Q_{[\mu}{}^{\alpha}{}_{|\gamma|}Q_{\nu]}{}^{\gamma}{}_{\beta}+\acute{\Sigma}_{\mu}{}^{\gamma}{}_{\nu}Q_{\gamma}{}^{\alpha}{}_{\beta}\,.

Let us now restrict to the case of two Weyl connections.

Lemma 2.31
.

The transition tensor between two Weyl connections
\widecheck{\nabla}
and
\widehat{\nabla}
is given by

Q_{\alpha}{}^{\gamma}{}_{\beta}=S_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\gamma\mu}\left(\widecheck{\kappa}_{g}-\widehat{\kappa}_{g}\right)_{\mu}

where
\widecheck{\kappa}_{g}
and
\widehat{\kappa}_{g}
are the covector fields associated to respectively
\widecheck{\nabla}
and
\widehat{\nabla}
with respect to any representative
g\in[\widetilde{g}]
by (
2.16
) and
S_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\mu\nu}
is the tensor defined previously by (
2.7
).

Proposition 2.32
.

The Schouten, Cotton and Weyl tensors of two Weyl connections
\widecheck{\nabla}
and
\widehat{\nabla}
are linked by

\displaystyle\widecheck{L}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\widehat{L}_{\mu\nu}-\widecheck{\nabla}_{\mu}(\widecheck{\kappa}_{g}-\widehat{\kappa}_{g})_{\nu}-\frac{1}{2}S_{\mu\nu}{}^{\sigma\rho}(\widecheck{\kappa}_{g}-\widehat{\kappa}_{g})_{\sigma}(\widecheck{\kappa}_{g}-\widehat{\kappa}_{g})_{\rho}\,,

(2.28a)

\displaystyle\widecheck{C}_{\lambda\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\widehat{C}_{\lambda\mu\nu}+(\widecheck{\kappa}_{g}-\widehat{\kappa}_{g})_{\rho}\widecheck{W}^{\rho}{}_{\nu\lambda\mu}\,,

(2.28b)

\displaystyle\widecheck{W}^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\widehat{W}^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\nu}\,,

(2.28c)

where
\widecheck{\kappa}_{g}
and
\widehat{\kappa}_{g}
are the covector fields associated to
\widecheck{\nabla}
and
\widehat{\nabla}
with respect to a representative
g\in[\widetilde{g}]
by (
2.16
).

Remark
.

Thus all the Weyl tensors of Weyl connections for
(\mathcal{M},[\widetilde{g}])
, and in particular of the Levi-Civita connection of representatives
g\in[\widetilde{g}]
, coincide. In dimension
N=3
, the same property holds for the Cotton tensor since the Weyl tensor vanishes identically. Hence the Cotton tensor plays the role of the Weyl tensor in dimension
N=3
.
∎

2.4.4
Conformal invariance

Definition 2.33
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a smooth manifold and consider a map
X:g\mapsto X_{g}
which associates a tensor field
X_{g}
to any metric
g
on
\mathcal{M}
. The map
X
is said to be
conformally invariant of weight
k
if

\forall\,\Omega\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M},\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\star}),\quad X_{\Omega^{2}g}=\Omega^{-k}X_{g}

The conformally invariant maps of weight
0
will simply be called conformally invariant. In that case, the map
[\widetilde{g}]\mapsto X_{g}
where
g\in[\widetilde{g}]
is a representative is well defined.

Proposition 2.34
.

In any dimension, the tensor
S_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\mu\nu}
and the Weyl tensor
W^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\nu}
are conformally invariant. In dimension
N=3
, the Cotton tensor
C_{\alpha\beta\gamma}
is conformally invariant. In dimension
N=4
, the Bach tensor
B_{\mu\nu}
is conformally invariant of weight
2
.

2.4.5
Conformally Einstein and conformally flat spaces

One can distinguish special conformal structures such as conformally Einstein spaces and conformally flat spaces.

Definition 2.35
.

A pseudo-Riemannian manifold
(\mathcal{M},\widetilde{g})
is said to be
locally conformally Einstein
if for all
p\in\mathcal{M}
, there exists an open neighbourhood
\mathcal{U}
of
p
in
\mathcal{M}
and a local conformal factor
\Omega\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{U},\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\star})
such that
(\mathcal{U},\Omega^{2}\widetilde{g})
is Einstein. If the conformal factor
\Omega
can be defined globally on
\mathcal{M}
then it is said to be
globally conformally Einstein
.

Definition 2.36
.

A pseudo-Riemannian manifold
(\mathcal{M},\widetilde{g})
is said to be
locally conformally flat
if for all
p\in\mathcal{M}
, there exists an open neighbourhood
\mathcal{U}
of
p
in
\mathcal{M}
and a local conformal factor
\Omega\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{U},\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\star})
such that
(\mathcal{U},\Omega^{2}\widetilde{g})
is Riemann-flat. If the conformal factor
\Omega
can be defined globally on
\mathcal{M}
then it is said to be
globally conformally flat
.

Remark
.

Since the definitions depend only on the conformal class
[\widetilde{g}]
, it extends to conformal structures.
∎

Locally conformally flat spaces are characterised by the following theorem.

Theorem 2.37

(Weyl-Schouten theorem)
.

Let
(\mathcal{M},\widetilde{g})
be a pseudo-Riemannian manifold of dimension
N\geq 2
. Then

i)

if
N=2
, it is locally conformally flat,

ii)

if
N=3
, it is locally conformally flat if and only if the Cotton tensor vanishes identically,

iii)

if
N\geq 4
, it is locally conformally flat if and only if the Weyl tensor vanishes identically.

Remarks
.

•

If
\mathcal{M}
is contractible, one can pass from locally to globally conformally flat.

•

In dimension
N=3
, an Einstein manifold is always locally conformally flat. ∎

2.4.6
Conformal Killing fields

Conformal Killing fields are the generalisation of Killing fields in conformal geometry. Let
(\mathcal{M},[\widetilde{g}])
be a conformal structure.

Definition 2.38
.

Let
K
be a smooth vector field on
\mathcal{M}
. The following statements are equivalent

i)

\forall\,g\in[\widetilde{g}]\,,\exists\,\phi_{g,K}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M}),\quad\mathcal{L}_{K}g=2\phi_{g,K}\,g
,

ii)

\exists\,g\in[\widetilde{g}]\,,\exists\,\phi_{g,K}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M}),\quad\mathcal{L}_{K}g=2\phi_{g,K}\,g
,

iii)

K
generates a flow of confomorphisms locally on
\mathcal{M}
,

iv)

\forall
Weyl connection
\widehat{\nabla},\quad Z_{\alpha}{}^{\beta\mu}{}_{\nu}\widehat{\nabla}_{\mu}K^{\nu}=0
,

v)

\exists
Weyl connection
\widehat{\nabla},\quad Z_{\alpha}{}^{\beta\mu}{}_{\nu}\widehat{\nabla}_{\mu}K^{\nu}=0
,

where
\mathcal{L}
is the Lie derivative and
Z_{\alpha}{}^{\beta\mu}{}_{\nu}
is the projection operator on symmetric trace-free 2-tensors defined by

Z_{\alpha}{}^{\beta\mu}{}_{\nu}:=\delta_{\alpha}{}^{\mu}\delta_{\nu}{}^{\beta}+g_{\alpha\nu}g^{\beta\mu}-\frac{2}{N}\delta_{\nu}{}^{\mu}\delta_{\alpha}{}^{\beta}\,,

(2.29)

for any
g\in[\widetilde{g}]
(it is conformally invariant). If one statement (and thus all) is verified,
K
is said to be a conformal Killing vector field of
(\mathcal{M},[\widetilde{g}])
.

Remark
.

The Killing fields of a representative
g\in[\widetilde{g}]
are the conformal Killing fields which are divergence-free for the Levi-Civita connection
\nabla
of
g
.
∎

Proposition 2.39
.

The set of all conformal Killing fields of
(\mathcal{M},[\widetilde{g}])
forms a Lie subalgebra of smooth vector fields on
\mathcal{M}
. The latter is infinite dimensional if
N=2
and has dimension at most
(N+1)(N+2)/2
if
N\geq 3
.

2.4.7
Conformal geodesics

Let
(\mathcal{M},[\widetilde{g}])
be a conformal structure. Only the null geodesics of representatives
g\in[\widetilde{g}]
are invariant up to a reparametrisation under conformal rescalings. However, one can still define a type of curves which enjoys nice conformal properties and includes all geodesic curves of representatives. These are called conformal geodesics.

Definition 2.40
.

Let
\widehat{\nabla}
be a Weyl connection for
(\mathcal{M},[\widetilde{g}])
. A
\widehat{\nabla}
-
conformal geodesic (curve)
is a pair
(x,\widehat{\beta})
consisting of a curve
x:\tau\in I\subset\mathbb{R}\mapsto x(\tau)\in\mathcal{M}
, whose tangent vector is denoted by
\dot{x}
, and a covector field
\widehat{\beta}
along
x
satisfying the following equations

\displaystyle\left(\widehat{\nabla}_{\dot{x}}\dot{x}\right)^{\mu}

\displaystyle=-S_{\lambda\sigma}{}^{\mu\nu}\dot{x}^{\lambda}\dot{x}^{\sigma}\widehat{\beta}_{\nu}\,,

(2.30a)

\displaystyle\left(\widehat{\nabla}_{\dot{x}}\widehat{\beta}\right)_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}S_{\lambda\sigma}{}^{\mu\nu}\dot{x}^{\sigma}\widehat{\beta}_{\mu}\widehat{\beta}_{\nu}+\widehat{L}_{\sigma\lambda}\dot{x}^{\sigma}\,.

(2.30b)

Remarks
.

•

The initial data
(x_{\star},\dot{x}_{\star},\widehat{\beta}_{\star})
of a
\widehat{\nabla}
-conformal geodesic consists of an initial position, an initial tangent vector and an initial covector

x_{\star}\in\mathcal{M}\,,\qquad\dot{x}_{\star}\in T_{x_{\star}}\mathcal{M}\,,\qquad\widehat{\beta}_{\star}\in T^{\star}_{x_{\star}}\mathcal{M}\,.

•

Along any
\widehat{\nabla}
-conformal geodesic
(x,\widehat{\beta})
and for any representative
g\in[\widetilde{g}]
,

\dot{x}\cdot g(\dot{x},\dot{x})=-2\langle\widehat{\kappa}_{g}+\widehat{\beta},\dot{x}\rangle g(\dot{x},\dot{x})

(2.31)

where
\widehat{\kappa}_{g}
is the covector field associated to
\widehat{\nabla}
with respect to
g
by (
2.16
). In particular,
g(\dot{x},\dot{x})
has constant sign along a conformal geodesic. This allows to classify conformal geodesics into timelike, null or spacelike categories. ∎

The following proposition links conformal geodesics for different Weyl connections.

Proposition 2.41
.

Let
\widehat{\nabla}
and
\widecheck{\nabla}
be two Weyl connections for
(\mathcal{M},[\widetilde{g}])
. Then
(x,\widehat{\beta})
is a
\widehat{\nabla}
-conformal geodesic if and only if
(x,\widecheck{\beta}:=\widehat{\beta}+\widehat{\kappa}_{g}-\widecheck{\kappa}_{g})
is a
\widecheck{\nabla}
-conformal geodesic, where
\widehat{\kappa}_{g}
and
\widecheck{\kappa}_{g}
are the covector fields associated to
\widehat{\nabla}
and
\widecheck{\nabla}
with respect to a representative
g\in[\widetilde{g}]
by (
2.16
).

Remark
.

Note that the curve
x
(and its parametrisation) is left unchanged.
∎

2.4.8
Congruences of timelike conformal geodesics

Congruences of timelike conformal geodesics are crucial in the gauge construction presented in
Section

4.2.2
. Let
(\mathcal{M},[\widetilde{g}])
be a Lorentzian conformal structure of dimension
N=n+1
and
\widehat{\nabla}
be a Weyl connection.

Definition 2.42
.

A
congruence of timelike
\widehat{\nabla}
-conformal geodesics
on an open set
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathcal{M}
is a smooth family of timelike
\widehat{\nabla}
-conformal geodesics
(x,\widehat{\beta})
such that at any point
p\in\mathcal{U}
there is one and only one conformal geodesic passing through
p
.

Remark
.

A congruence of geodesics is usually defined as integral curves of a non-vanishing vector field satisfying the geodesic equation. The vector field is not unique and the parametrisation of the curves is not imposed. Here, we choose to fix the vector field, the Weyl connection and the parametrisation in the definition.
∎

Example
.

Consider the Einstein cylinder
(\mathcal{M}_{EC},g_{EC})
where

\mathcal{M}_{EC}:=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{3}\quad\text{and}\quad g_{EC}:=-dt^{2}+d\sigma_{\mathbb{S}^{3}}^{2}\,.

(2.32)

The Schouten tensor of the associated Levi-Civita connection
\nabla
is given by

L=\frac{1}{2}\left(dt^{2}+d\sigma_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}^{2}\right)=\frac{1}{2}g_{EC}+dt^{2}\,.

Take the spacelike hypersurface

\mathcal{S}_{\star}:=\left\{(t,\psi,\theta,\varphi)\in\mathbb{R}\times(0,\pi)^{2}\times(0,2\pi)\;\middle|\;t=\frac{2}{3}\sin\psi\right\}\,.

The
\nabla
-conformal geodesics starting on
\mathcal{S}_{\star}
, with initial tangent vector
\dot{x}_{\star}
the unit normal vector to
\mathcal{S}_{\star}
and initial covector
\beta_{\star}=0
, form locally a congruence of timelike conformal geodesics. Note that each curve remain on a great circle.

In
Figure

1
, these conformal geodesics are represented in dotted or dashed lines while
\mathcal{S}_{\star}
is depicted with a solid line. Here,
\psi
goes from
-\pi
to
\pi
to fully cover a great circle:
(\psi,\theta,\varphi)
with a negative
\psi
shall be understood as
(-\psi,\pi-\theta,\varphi+\pi\mod 2\pi)
. Eventually, caustics form at
\psi=-\pi/2
and at least some conformal geodesics present a finite time blow-up. These two behaviours result in the end of the congruence.
∎

Figure 1
:
A congruence of timelike conformal geodesics in the Einstein cylinder

Definition 2.43
.

A
conformal Gaussian coordinate system
on an open set
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathcal{M}
is a coordinate system
(\tau,y^{1},\dots,y^{n})
on
\mathcal{U}
such that there exists a congruence of timelike conformal geodesics on
\mathcal{U}
satisfying

•

\tau
is the parameter function of the conformal geodesics in the congruence,

•

the functions
(y^{i})_{1\leq i\leq n}
are constant along the conformal geodesics.

Remark
.

The Weyl connection is not specified here because the two properties above are independent of it thanks to
2.41
.
∎

In the next proposition, we recall that a congruence of timelike
\widehat{\nabla}
-conformal geodesics on an open set
\mathcal{U}
singles out a canonical Weyl connection
\widecheck{\nabla}
on
\mathcal{U}
and a canonical representative
\breve{g}
of the conformal class
[\widetilde{g}]
on
\mathcal{U}
.

Proposition 2.44
.

Consider a congruence of timelike
\widehat{\nabla}
-conformal geodesics
(x,\widehat{\beta})
on an open set
\mathcal{U}
. Then there exists a unique Weyl connection
\widecheck{\nabla}
on
\mathcal{U}
and a unique representative
\breve{g}
of the conformal class
[\widetilde{g}]
on
\mathcal{U}
such that

\displaystyle\widecheck{\nabla}_{\dot{x}}\dot{x}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(2.33a)

\displaystyle\widecheck{L}(\dot{x},.)

\displaystyle=0\,,

(2.33b)

\displaystyle\breve{g}(\dot{x},\dot{x})

\displaystyle=-1\,,

(2.33c)

Remark
.

This means that one can find a Weyl connection
\widecheck{\nabla}
for which
\widecheck{\beta}=0
and a conformally related metric
\breve{g}
for which the parameter
\tau
of the curves
x
corresponds to the
\breve{g}
-proper time.
∎

Proof.

Fix a representative
g\in[\widetilde{g}]
. Since the conformal geodesics in the congruence are timelike,
g(\dot{x},\dot{x})
is a smooth negative function on
\mathcal{U}
. Define

\Omega:=(-g(\dot{x},\dot{x}))^{-1/2}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{U},\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\star})\,.

(2.34)

Then the metric
\breve{g}:=\Omega^{2}\,g
on
\mathcal{U}
does not depend on the choice of
g
and
\breve{g}(\dot{x},\dot{x})=-1
. This defines uniquely the metric
\breve{g}
.

Let
\widehat{\kappa}_{\breve{g}}
be the smooth covector field on
\mathcal{U}
associated to
\widehat{\nabla}
with respect to
\breve{g}
by (
2.16
). Take the Weyl connection
\widecheck{\nabla}
associated to the smooth covector field
\widecheck{\kappa}_{\breve{g}}:=\widehat{\kappa}_{\breve{g}}+\widehat{\beta}
on
\mathcal{U}
with respect to
\breve{g}
given by
2.24
. It follows from
2.41
that the curves
(x,0)
are
\widecheck{\nabla}
-conformal geodesics and this is the only choice of Weyl connection which gives
\widecheck{\beta}=0
.
∎

When the conformal structure on
\mathcal{U}
is globally conformally Einstein, one can determine a simple expression for the conformal factor between the metric
\breve{g}
given by
2.44
and the Einstein metric. This property, used in Friedrich’s original proof, will be obtained through an alternative derivation directly from the extended conformal vacuum Einstein equations in the gauge construction, see
4.27
and in particular (
4.33c
).

Proposition 2.45

(Friedrich
[
38
, Lemma 3.1]
)
.

Consider a congruence of timelike
\widehat{\nabla}
-conformal geodesics
(x,\widehat{\beta})
on an open set
\mathcal{U}
. Introduce the hypersurface
\mathcal{S}_{\star}:=\{x\in\mathcal{U}\mid\tau(x)=\tau_{\star}\}
for some fixed parameter value
\tau_{\star}\in\mathbb{R}
. Assume that there exists a representative
\widetilde{g}
in the conformal class such that
(\mathcal{U},\widetilde{g})
is solution to the (
VE
). Then the metric
\breve{g}
on
\mathcal{U}
defined through (
2.33c
) is conformally related to
\widetilde{g}
on
\mathcal{U}
by a conformal factor
\Omega
(that is
\breve{g}=\Omega^{2}\widetilde{g}
on
\mathcal{U}
) expressed in a Gaussian coordinate system
(\tau,y)
associated to the congruence as follows

\Omega(\tau,y)=\Omega_{\star}(y)\left(1+v_{\star}(y)(\tau-\tau_{\star})-f_{\star}(y)\frac{(\tau-\tau_{\star})^{2}}{2}\right)\,,

(2.35)

where
\Omega_{\star}
,
q_{\star}
and
f_{\star}
are the following smooth functions on
\mathcal{S}_{\star}

\displaystyle\Omega_{\star}

\displaystyle:=(-\widetilde{g}(\dot{x}_{\star},\dot{x}_{\star}))^{-1/2}\,,

(2.36a)

\displaystyle v_{\star}

\displaystyle:=\langle\widetilde{\beta}_{\star},\dot{x}_{\star}\rangle\,,

(2.36b)

\displaystyle f_{\star}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2\Omega_{\star}^{2}}\left(\widetilde{g}^{-1}(\widetilde{\beta}_{\star},\widetilde{\beta}_{\star})+\frac{2\Lambda}{n(n-1)}\right)\,,

(2.36c)

with
\widetilde{\beta}:=\widehat{\beta}+\widehat{\kappa}_{\widetilde{g}}
.

Remark
.

A more detailed analysis can be made with the hypotheses of
2.45
such as in the case of a zero cosmological constant presented in
[
39
]
. Indeed, the following auxiliary variables

u:=\widetilde{g}(\dot{x},\dot{x})\,,\qquad v:=\langle\widetilde{\beta},\dot{x}\rangle\,,\qquad w:=\frac{1}{2}\left(\widetilde{g}^{-1}(\widetilde{\beta},\widetilde{\beta})+\frac{2\Lambda}{n(n-1)}\right)\,,

satisfy a system of ordinary differential equations given by

\dot{u}=-2vu\,,\qquad\dot{v}=-v^{2}+uw\,,\qquad\dot{w}=vw\,.

(2.37)

After solving for these auxiliary variables, the
\widetilde{\nabla}
-conformal geodesic equations reduce to a linear system of ordinary differential equations. Thus finite time blow-ups are entirely controlled by the auxiliary system (
2.37
) in this case. Yet integrating (
2.37
) gives

u(\tau)=\frac{u_{\star}}{P(\tau)^{2}}\,,\qquad v(\tau)=\frac{P^{\prime}(\tau)}{P(\tau)}\,,\qquad w(\tau)=w_{\star}P(\tau)\,,

where

P(\tau):=1+v_{\star}(\tau-\tau_{\star})+\frac{u_{\star}w_{\star}}{2}(\tau-\tau_{\star})^{2}\,.

One concludes that a finite time blow-up occurs if and only if

•

u_{\star}w_{\star}=0
and
v_{\star}\neq 0
,

•

u_{\star}w_{\star}\neq 0
and
v_{\star}^{2}\geq 2u_{\star}w_{\star}
. ∎

Proof.

For completeness, we include Friedrich’s proof presented in
[
38
, Section 3.3]
. Let
\widehat{\kappa}_{\widetilde{g}}
be the covector associated to
\widehat{\nabla}
with respect to
\widetilde{g}
. By
2.41
,
(x,\widetilde{\beta}:=\widehat{\beta}+\widehat{\kappa}_{\widetilde{g}})
is solution to the
\widetilde{\nabla}
-conformal geodesic equations where
\widetilde{\nabla}
is the Levi-Civita connection of
\widetilde{g}
. With (
2.31
) and (
2.34
), one finds

\dot{\Omega}:=\dot{x}\cdot\Omega=\widetilde{\nabla}_{\dot{x}}\Omega=\langle\widetilde{\beta},\dot{x}\rangle\Omega\,.

By further differentiating with respect to
\tau
and using the
\widetilde{\nabla}
-conformal geodesic equations, one deduces that

\displaystyle\ddot{\Omega}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{2}\widetilde{g}(\dot{x},\dot{x})\widetilde{g}^{-1}(\widetilde{\beta},\widetilde{\beta})+\widetilde{L}(\dot{x},\dot{x})\right)\Omega\,,

\displaystyle\dddot{\Omega}

\displaystyle=\left((\widetilde{\nabla}_{\dot{x}}\widetilde{L})(\dot{x},\dot{x})+3\left(\widetilde{L}(\dot{x},\widetilde{\beta}^{\sharp})\widetilde{g}(\dot{x},\dot{x})-\langle\widetilde{\beta},\dot{x}\rangle\widetilde{L}(\dot{x},\dot{x})\right)\right)\Omega\,,

where
\widetilde{\beta}^{\sharp}
is the vector field obtained by raising the index with the metric
\widetilde{g}
. Since
(\mathcal{U},\widetilde{g})
is solution to the (VE), one has
\dddot{\Omega}=0
. It follows that
\Omega
is a second order polynomial in
\tau
. Let
(\dot{x}_{\star},\widetilde{\beta}_{\star})
be the values of
(\dot{x},\widetilde{\beta})
on
\mathcal{S}_{\star}
. By evaluating the previous equations and (
2.34
) on
\mathcal{S}_{\star}
, one gets the expressions (
2.36
).
∎

2.5
Candidates and dual tensors

In this section, we introduce tensors sharing the same algebraic properties than the Weyl or the Cotton tensor, which we call Weyl or Cotton candidates. The former are sometimes referred to as Weyl fields
[
23
]
or spin-2 fields
[
20
]
. These candidates can verify covariant differential equations similar to the Bianchi identities. In small dimensions, such equations simplify greatly by mean of duality.

These notions will be exploited multiple times in
Section

4
. In particular, let us highlight
2.48
of the Cotton-York tensor which plays a key role with regard to the boundary conditions in
Section

4.3
.

2.5.1
Weyl and Cotton candidates

Let
(\mathcal{M},g)
be a smooth pseudo-Riemannian manifold.

Definition 2.46
.

A
Weyl candidate
on
(\mathcal{M},g)
is a tensor field
Q^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\nu}
enjoying the same algebraic properties than a Weyl tensor, that is

Q_{(\alpha\beta)\mu\nu}=0\,,\qquad Q^{\alpha}{}_{\beta(\mu\nu)}=0\,,\qquad Q^{\alpha}{}_{[\beta\mu\nu]}=0\,,\qquad Q^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\alpha\nu}=0\,.

Definition 2.47
.

A
Cotton candidate
on
(\mathcal{M},g)
is a tensor field
J_{\lambda\mu\nu}
satisfying the same algebraic properties than a Cotton tensor, that is

J_{(\lambda\mu)\nu}=0\,,\qquad J_{[\lambda\mu\nu]}=0\,,\qquad J_{\lambda\mu}{}^{\mu}=0\,.

2.5.2
Hodge dual

Let
(\mathcal{M},g)
be a smooth oriented pseudo-Riemannian manifold of signature
3
3

3

With the convention that
(N,0)
is the Riemannian signature and
(N-1,1)
is the Lorentzian.

(p,q)
and dimension
N=p+q
. Its volume form is the
N
-form defined locally by

\epsilon:=\sqrt{|\det g|}\;dy^{1}\wedge\dots\wedge dy^{N}\,,

for any local coordinate system
(y^{1},\dots,y^{N})
and where
\det g
is the determinant of the metric in this coordinate system. It is clear that
\epsilon_{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{N}}
is conformally invariant of weight
-N
. Moreover, if
\widehat{\nabla}
is a Weyl connection for the conformal structure
(\mathcal{M},[g])
then

\widehat{\nabla}_{\alpha}\epsilon_{\beta_{1}\dots\beta_{N}}=-N(\widehat{\kappa}_{g})_{\alpha}\epsilon_{\beta_{1}\dots\beta_{N}}\,,

where
\widehat{\kappa}_{g}
is the covector field defined by (
2.16
).

Recall that the Hodge dual of a
k
-form
\omega
is the
(N-k)
-form
\star\omega
defined by

(\star\omega)_{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{N-k}}:=\frac{1}{k!}\epsilon_{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{N-k}}{}^{\beta_{1}\dots\beta_{k}}\omega_{\beta_{1}\dots\beta_{k}}\,.

From the identity, see
[
85
, Section 2.5.3]
and references therein,

\epsilon^{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{k}\,\beta_{1}\dots\beta_{N-k}}\epsilon_{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{k}\,\gamma_{1}\dots\gamma_{N-k}}=(-1)^{q}\,k!\,(N-k)!\;\delta_{[\gamma_{1}}{}^{\beta_{1}}\dots\,\delta_{\gamma_{N-k}]}{}^{\beta_{N-k}}\,,

(2.38)

one deduces that for all
k
-form
\omega
,

\star(\star\omega)=(-1)^{q+k(N-k)}\omega\,.

2.5.3
Duality in dimension 4

Let
(\mathcal{M},g)
be an oriented smooth Lorentzian manifold of dimension
4
. The right and left dual tensors of a Weyl candidate
Q^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\nu}
are defined by

\displaystyle(Q\star)_{\alpha\beta\lambda\xi}:=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{\lambda\xi}{}^{\mu\nu}Q_{\alpha\beta\mu\nu}\,,

\displaystyle(\star Q)_{\alpha\beta\lambda\xi}:=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\mu\nu}Q_{\mu\nu\lambda\xi}\,.

It is classical that the left and right duals coincide and that they are Weyl candidates as well, see for instance
[
20
, Section 4]
. The left dual tensor of a Cotton candidate
J_{\alpha\beta\gamma}
is defined by

(\star J)_{\alpha\beta\gamma}:=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\mu\nu}J_{\mu\nu\gamma}\,.

Then
(\star J)_{\alpha\beta\gamma}
is also a Cotton candidate. Indeed,

\displaystyle(\star J)_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\mu\nu}J_{\mu\nu}{}^{\beta}=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{\alpha}{}^{\beta\mu\nu}J_{[\beta\mu\nu]}=0\,,

\displaystyle(\star J)_{[\rho\sigma\tau]}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}\epsilon^{\alpha}{}_{\rho\sigma\tau}J_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\beta}=0\,.

Moreover, for a second Bianchi identity type equation between a Weyl candidate
Q_{\alpha\beta\mu\nu}
and a Cotton candidate
J_{\alpha\beta\gamma}
with respect to a Weyl connection
\widehat{\nabla}
for
(\mathcal{M},[g])
, one has

\displaystyle\widehat{\nabla}_{\alpha}Q^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\nu}=J_{\mu\nu\beta}

\displaystyle\iff\widehat{\nabla}_{\alpha}(Q\star)^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\nu}=(\star J)_{\mu\nu\beta}

\displaystyle\iff\widehat{\nabla}_{[\mu}Q^{\alpha}{}_{|\beta|\nu\xi]}=S_{\beta[\mu}{}^{\alpha\gamma}J_{\nu\xi]\gamma}

\displaystyle\iff\widehat{\nabla}_{[\mu}(Q\star)^{\alpha}{}_{|\beta|\nu\xi]}=S_{\beta[\mu}{}^{\alpha\gamma}(\star J)_{\nu\xi]\gamma}\,.

More precisely,

\Big(\widehat{\nabla}_{\alpha}(Q\star)^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\lambda\xi}-(\star J)_{\lambda\xi\beta}\Big)=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{\lambda\xi}{}^{\mu\nu}\Big(\widehat{\nabla}_{\alpha}Q^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\nu}-J_{\mu\nu\beta}\Big)\,,

(2.39)

and

-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{\beta}{}^{\alpha\lambda\xi}g_{\mu\rho}\Big(\widehat{\nabla}_{[\alpha}Q^{\rho}{}_{|\nu|\lambda\xi]}-S_{\nu[\alpha}{}^{\rho\gamma}J_{\lambda\xi]\gamma}\Big)=\Big(\widehat{\nabla}_{\alpha}(Q\star)^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\nu}-(\star J)_{\mu\nu\beta}\Big)\,.

(2.40)

The last equation rewrites under the form

3\Big(\widehat{\nabla}_{[\alpha}Q^{\rho}{}_{|\nu|\lambda\xi]}-S_{\nu[\alpha}{}^{\rho\gamma}J_{\lambda\xi]\gamma}\Big)=\epsilon^{\beta}{}_{\alpha\lambda\xi}g^{\rho\mu}\Big(\widehat{\nabla}_{\sigma}(Q\star)^{\sigma}{}_{\beta\mu\nu}-(\star J)_{\mu\nu\beta}\Big)\,.

(2.41)

Remark
.

Let
\omega_{\alpha}
be a smooth covector field on
\mathcal{M}
. Then
J_{\mu\nu\beta}:=\omega_{\alpha}Q^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\nu}
is a Cotton candidate and

\displaystyle(\star J)_{\mu\nu\beta}

\displaystyle=\omega_{\alpha}(Q\star)^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\nu}=\omega_{\alpha}(\star Q)^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\sigma\tau}\omega_{\alpha}Q^{\sigma\tau}{}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{\beta}{}^{\alpha\sigma\tau}\omega_{[\alpha}Q_{\sigma\tau]\mu\nu}=-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{\beta}{}^{\alpha\lambda\xi}g_{\mu\rho}\left(Q^{\rho}{}_{\nu[\alpha\lambda}\omega_{\xi]}\right)\,.

Thus, using (
2.40
) with
Q^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\nu}=0
, one has

S_{\nu[\alpha}{}^{\rho\gamma}J_{\lambda\xi]\gamma}=Q^{\rho}{}_{\nu[\alpha\lambda}\omega_{\xi]}\,.\qed

(2.42)

2.5.4
Duality in dimension 3 and the Cotton-York tensor

Let
(\mathcal{S},h)
be an oriented smooth Lorentzian manifold of dimension
3
,
D
be its Levi-Civita connection and
J_{ijk}
be a Cotton candidate. Define the left dual tensor of
J_{ijk}
by

(\star J)_{ij}:=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{i}{}^{kl}J_{klj}\,.

Then
(\star J)_{ij}
is symmetric and trace-free. Indeed,

\displaystyle 2(\star J)_{[ij]}

\displaystyle=2\delta_{[i}{}^{k}\delta_{j]}{}^{l}(\star J)_{kl}=-\epsilon^{m}{}_{ij}\epsilon_{m}{}^{kl}(\star J)_{kl}=-\epsilon^{m}{}_{ij}J_{ml}{}^{l}=0\,,

\displaystyle 2(\star J)^{i}{}_{i}

\displaystyle=\epsilon^{ijk}J_{ijk}=\epsilon^{ijk}J_{[ijk]}=0\,.

Moreover, for any Weyl connection
\widehat{D}
for
(\mathcal{S},[h])
,

\displaystyle\widehat{D}_{i}(\star J)^{i}{}_{j}-3(\widehat{\kappa}_{h})_{i}(\star J)^{i}{}_{j}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ikl}\widehat{D}_{[i}J_{kl]j}\,,

\displaystyle\widehat{D}_{i}(\star J)^{i}{}_{j}-3(\widehat{\kappa}_{h})_{i}(\star J)^{i}{}_{j}

\displaystyle=\widehat{D}_{i}(\star J)_{j}{}^{i}-3(\widehat{\kappa}_{h})_{i}(\star J)_{j}{}^{i}=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{j}{}^{kl}\left(\widehat{D}_{i}J_{kl}{}^{i}-2(\widehat{\kappa}_{h})_{i}J_{kl}{}^{i}\right)\,.

In particular, for a fourth Bianchi identity type equation on the Cotton candidate
J_{ijk}
with respect to a Weyl connection
\widehat{D}
,
