\widehat{D}_{[i}J_{jk]l}=0\iff\widehat{D}_{i}(\star J)^{i}{}_{j}=3(\widehat{\kappa}_{h})_{i}(\star J)^{i}{}_{j}\iff h^{ij}\widehat{D}_{i}J_{klj}=0\,.

Definition 2.48
.

The
Cotton-York tensor

Y_{ij}
of a
3
-dimensional oriented smooth Lorentzian manifold
(\mathcal{S},h)
is the left dual tensor of the Cotton tensor
C_{ijk}
of the Levi-Civita connection
D
.

Proposition 2.49
.

The Cotton-York tensor of a
3
-dimensional oriented smooth Lorentzian manifold
(\mathcal{S},h)
is symmetric, trace-free with respect to the metric
h
and divergence-free for the Levi-Civita connection
D
of
h
. It is furthermore conformally invariant of weight
1
.

Proof.

From the above general discussion, it is clear that the Cotton-York is symmetric, trace-free and divergence-free. Since
C_{ijk}
is conformally invariant of weight
0
and
\epsilon_{i}{}^{jk}
is conformally invariant of weight
1
, the Cotton-York tensor is also conformally invariant of weight
1
.
∎

2.5.5
Duality on a distribution of dimension 2

To interpret geometrically certain analytic boundary conditions, it is convenient in
Section

4.3.1
to exploit duality on a 2-dimensional distribution arising from the gauge constructed in
4.27
.

To that end, consider an oriented
4
-dimensional smooth Lorentzian manifold
(\mathcal{M},g)
and let
(e_{\bf a})
be a smooth
g
-orthonormal frame field on
\mathcal{M}
with
e_{\bf 0}
timelike and
\epsilon_{\bf 0123}=+1
where
\epsilon
is the volume form of
g
. Define

•

\mathcal{D}^{2}:=\vectorspan(e_{\bf 1},e_{\bf 2})
the distribution of dimension 2 spanned by
e_{\bf 1}
and
e_{\bf 2}
, equipped with the induced scalar product
\delta_{\bf AB}
,

•

\not{\epsilon}_{\bf AB}:=\epsilon_{\bf 0AB3}
,

•

\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{D}^{2})
be the space of trace-free symmetric smooth 2-tensor
q_{\bf AB}
on
\mathcal{D}^{2}
,

•

\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{D}^{2})
be the space of smooth 4-tensors
L_{\bf AB}{}^{\bf CD}
on
\mathcal{D}^{2}
verifying

L_{\bf AB}{}^{\bf CD}=L_{\bf(AB)}{}^{\bf(CD)}\,,\qquad L_{\bf A}{}^{\bf ACD}=0\,,\qquad L_{\bf ABC}{}^{\bf C}=0\,.

Note that
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{D}^{2})
can be seen as the space of endomorphisms of
\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{D}^{2})
.

Definition 2.50
.

The dual of any
q_{\bf AB}\in\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{D}^{2})
is the element of
\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{D}^{2})
defined by

(\star q)_{\bf AB}:=\not{\epsilon}\vphantom{q}_{\bf(A}{}^{\bf C}q_{\bf B)C}\,.

(2.43)

This defines an operator
\star\in\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{D}^{2})
.

Lemma 2.51
.

For all
q_{\bf AB}
,
(q^{\prime})_{\bf AB}\in\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{D}^{2})
,

\displaystyle(\star q)_{\bf AB}(q^{\prime})^{\bf AB}

\displaystyle=-q_{\bf AB}(\star q^{\prime})^{\bf AB}\,,

\displaystyle(\star(\star q))_{\bf AB}

\displaystyle=-q_{\bf AB}\,.

In other words,
\star
is anti self-adjoint and
\star\star=-\Id
. In particular,

\displaystyle(\star q)_{\bf AB}(\star q)^{\bf AB}

\displaystyle=q_{\bf AB}q^{\bf AB}\,,

\displaystyle(\star q)_{\bf AB}q^{\bf AB}

\displaystyle=0\,.

Proof.

The identities come from (
2.38
) and simple calculus.
∎

Lemma 2.52
.

For any
q_{\bf AB}\in\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{D}^{2})
and any
(u,v)\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M},\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{(0,0)\})
, there exists a unique
(q^{\prime})_{\bf AB}\in\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{D}^{2})
such that

q_{\bf AB}=u\,(q^{\prime})_{\bf AB}+v\,(\star q^{\prime})_{\bf AB}\,.

In other words, for all
(u,v)\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M},\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{(0,0)\})
,
u\Id+v\star\in\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{D}^{2})
is an automorphism of
\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{D}^{2})
.

Proof.

Let
q_{\bf AB}\in\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{D}^{2})
and
(u,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{(0,0)\}
. If
(q^{\prime})_{\bf AB}
exists then one deduces, by taking a linear combination of the decomposition and its dual, that it is given by

(q^{\prime})_{\bf AB}=\frac{u}{u^{2}+v^{2}}\,q_{\bf AB}-\frac{v}{u^{2}+v^{2}}\,(\star q)_{\bf AB}\,.

Furthermore, one can check that this choice of
(q^{\prime})_{\bf AB}
yields the decomposition.
∎

Corollary 2.53
.

Let
q_{\bf AB}\in\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{D}^{2})
. The following statements are equivalent

i)

q_{\bf AB}=0
,

ii)

\forall\,(u,v)\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M},\mathbb{R}^{2})
,
u\,q_{\bf AB}+v\,(\star q)_{\bf AB}=0
,

iii)

\exists\,(u,v)\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M},\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{(0,0)\})
,
u\,q_{\bf AB}+v\,(\star q)_{\bf AB}=0
.

Proof.

The implications
i)\implies ii)
and
ii)\implies iii)
are trivial. The implication
iii)\implies i)
follows from
2.52
.
∎

Let us describe more precisely the spaces
\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{D}^{2})
and
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{D}^{2})
. Define
\omega\in\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{D}^{2})
by

\omega_{\bf 11}=-\omega_{\bf 22}=0\,,\qquad\omega_{\bf 12}=\omega_{\bf 21}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\,.

Since
(e_{\bf 1},e_{\bf 2})
is an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{D}^{2}
, one deduces that
(\omega,\star\omega)
is an orthonormal basis of
\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{D}^{2})
. One has

\Mat_{(e_{\bf 1},e_{\bf 2})}(\omega)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\,,\qquad\Mat_{(e_{\bf 1},e_{\bf 2})}(\star\omega)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}\,.

Now let
\sigma\in\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{D}^{2})
be the orthogonal reflection symmetry with respect to the hyperplane
\vectorspan(\omega)
. Then

\displaystyle\Mat_{(\omega,\star\omega)}(\Id)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\,,

\displaystyle\qquad\Mat_{(\omega,\star\omega)}(\star)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\,,

\displaystyle\Mat_{(\omega,\star\omega)}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}\,,

\displaystyle\qquad\Mat_{(\omega,\star\omega)}(\star\sigma)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\,.

and
(\Id/\sqrt{2},\star/\sqrt{2},\sigma/\sqrt{2},(\star\sigma)/\sqrt{2})
is an orthonormal basis of
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{D}^{2})
. Moreover, one has
\{\star,\sigma\}:=\star\sigma+\sigma\star=0
. The following lemma is a generalisation of
2.52
.

Lemma 2.54
.

An endomorphism
L=\alpha\Id+\beta\star+\gamma\sigma+\delta(\star\sigma)\in\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{D}^{2})
is everywhere invertible if and only if
\alpha^{2}+\beta^{2}-\delta^{2}-\gamma^{2}
nowhere vanishes. If
\alpha^{2}+\beta^{2}=\gamma^{2}+\delta^{2}
and
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta)\neq(0,0,0,0)
at a point
p\in\mathcal{M}
then
\ker L=\vectorspan\left((\alpha-\gamma)\omega-(\beta+\delta)(\star\omega)\right)
and
\im L=\vectorspan\left((\alpha+\gamma)\omega+(\beta+\delta)(\star\omega)\right)
at
p
.

Proof.

If
\alpha^{2}+\beta^{2}-\delta^{2}-\gamma^{2}\neq 0
at a point
p\in\mathcal{M}
, then the inverse of
L
at
p
is given by

L^{-1}=\frac{\alpha\Id-\beta\star-\gamma\sigma-\delta(\star\sigma)}{\alpha^{2}+\beta^{2}-\delta^{2}-\gamma^{2}}\,.

If not and if
L\neq 0
at
p
, the expressions of the kernel and the image can be easily deduced using the matrix representation in the basis
(\omega,\star\omega)
.
∎

2.6
Non-null hypersurfaces of Lorentzian conformal structures

We detail here structure inheritance from a Lorentzian conformal structure on a non-null hypersurface. In particular, the induced conformal structure and induced Weyl connections in
Section

2.6.2
and extrinsic curvatures in
Section

2.6.3
. The links between curvature tensors are given by the Codazzi equations derived for Weyl connections in
Section

2.6.5
. All decompositions, including the decomposition of Weyl candidates for all dimensions established in
Section

2.6.4
, use frame fields adapted to the hypersurface as defined in
Section

2.6.1
.

Let
(\mathcal{M},[\widetilde{g}])
be a smooth Lorentzian conformal structure of dimension
N=n+1
. Consider a non-null hypersurface
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathcal{M}
. The tangential part of a vector field or covector field with respect to
\mathcal{S}
is denoted by
\Tan
.

2.6.1
Adapted frame fields

Definition 2.55
.

An adapted frame field to
\mathcal{S}
with respect to a representative
\widetilde{g}\in[\widetilde{g}]
is a frame field
(e_{\bf a})=(e_{\perp},(e_{\bf i}))
on
\mathcal{M}
such that the restriction of
e_{\perp}
on
\mathcal{S}
is a normal unit vector field to
\mathcal{S}
with respect to
\widetilde{g}
and the restriction of the vector fields
(e_{\bf i})
on
\mathcal{S}
are tangent to
\mathcal{S}
. The dual coframe field is denoted by
(\omega^{\bf a})=(\omega^{\perp},(\omega^{\bf i}))
.

Remarks
.

•

The family
(e_{\bf i})
is not necessarily orthogonal.

•

Let
\varepsilon:=\widetilde{g}(e_{\perp},e_{\perp})\in\{\pm 1\}
be the pseudo-norm of
e_{\perp}
. The sign depends on whether
\mathcal{S}
is timelike or spacelike. One has
\omega^{\perp}=\varepsilon\widetilde{g}(e_{\perp},.)
.

•

If
g=\Omega^{2}\widetilde{g}\in[\widetilde{g}]
is another representative then
(\Omega^{-1}e_{\perp},(e_{\bf i}))
is an adapted frame with respect to
g
. ∎

In what follows, the tensorial indices
\perp
and
{\bf i},{\bf j},{\bf k}\dots
will refer to the decomposition in an adapted frame field to
\mathcal{S}
with respect to a representative
\widetilde{g}\in[\widetilde{g}]
and its dual coframe field. For example,

\displaystyle\widetilde{g}_{\perp\perp}

\displaystyle:=\widetilde{g}(e_{\perp},e_{\perp})=\varepsilon\,,

\displaystyle\widetilde{g}_{\perp\bf i}

\displaystyle:=\widetilde{g}(e_{\perp},e_{\bf i})=0\,.

2.6.2
The induced conformal structure and Weyl connection

Let
\widetilde{g}
,
g
be two representatives of
[\widetilde{g}]
and
\widetilde{h}
,
h
be the corresponding induced metrics on
\mathcal{S}
. Since
g=\Omega^{2}\widetilde{g}
for some
\Omega\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M},\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\star})
, one has
h=\Psi^{2}\widetilde{h}
with
\Psi:=\Omega|_{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{S},\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\star})
. Consequently, the conformal structure
(\mathcal{M},[\widetilde{g}])
induces the conformal structure
(\mathcal{S},[\widetilde{h}])
on
\mathcal{S}
.

Recall that a Weyl connection
\widehat{\nabla}
on
(\mathcal{M},[\widetilde{g}])
induces a connection
\widehat{D}
on
\mathcal{S}
through

\widehat{D}_{X}Y:=\Tan\widehat{\nabla}_{V}W\,,

for all smooth vector fields
X,Y
on
\mathcal{S}
and where
V,W
are smooth vector fields on
\mathcal{M}
such that
V|_{\mathcal{S}}=X
and
W|_{\mathcal{S}}=Y
.

Lemma 2.56
.

Let
\widehat{\nabla}
be a Weyl connection for
(\mathcal{M},[\widetilde{g}])
and
\widehat{D}
be its induced connection on
\mathcal{S}
. Then
\widehat{D}
is a Weyl connection for the induced conformal structure
(\mathcal{S},[\widetilde{h}])
. More precisely, if
\widehat{\kappa}_{\widetilde{g}}
is the covector field associated to
\widehat{\nabla}
with respect to a representative
\widetilde{g}\in[\widetilde{g}]
then
\widehat{D}
is the Weyl connection associated to
\Tan\widehat{\kappa}_{\widetilde{g}}
with respect to
\widetilde{h}
, the induced metric of
\widetilde{g}
on
\mathcal{S}
.

Proof.

Since
\widehat{\nabla}
is torsion-free, so is
\widehat{D}
. Let
\widetilde{g}\in[\widetilde{g}]
be a representative,
\widetilde{h}
be the induced metric on
\mathcal{S}
by
\widetilde{g}
and
\widehat{\kappa}_{\widetilde{g}}
be the covector field associated to
\widehat{\nabla}
with respect to
\widetilde{g}
. In an adapted frame field
(e_{\perp},(e_{\bf i}))
to
\mathcal{S}
with respect to
\widetilde{g}
, one has

\widehat{D}_{\bf i}\widetilde{h}_{\bf jk}=\widehat{D}_{\bf i}\widetilde{g}_{\bf jk}=\widehat{\nabla}_{\bf i}\widetilde{g}_{\bf jk}+\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf i}{}^{\bf\perp}{}_{\bf j}\widetilde{g}_{\bf\perp k}+\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf i}{}^{\bf\perp}{}_{\bf k}\widetilde{g}_{\bf j\perp}=\widehat{\nabla}_{\bf i}\widetilde{g}_{\bf jk}=-2(\widehat{\kappa}_{\widetilde{g}})_{\bf i}\widetilde{g}_{\bf jk}=-2(\widehat{\kappa}_{\widetilde{g}})_{\bf i}\widetilde{h}_{\bf jk}\,.\qed

2.6.3
Extrinsic curvature of a Weyl connection

Definition 2.57
.

The
extrinsic curvature
of a Weyl connection
\widehat{\nabla}
on
\mathcal{S}
with respect to a representative
\widetilde{g}\in[\widetilde{g}]
is defined by

\widehat{K}_{\widetilde{g}}(X,Y):=\langle\omega^{\perp},\widehat{\nabla}_{V}W\rangle

for all smooth vector fields
X,Y
on
\mathcal{S}
and where
V,W
are smooth vector fields on
\mathcal{M}
such that
V|_{\mathcal{S}}=X
,
W|_{\mathcal{S}}=Y
,
\omega^{\perp}
is a unit normal covector field to
\mathcal{S}
with respect to
\widetilde{g}
. The extrinsic curvature is a symmetric 2-tensor field on
\mathcal{S}
.

Remarks
.

•

If
g=\Omega^{2}\widetilde{g}\in[\widetilde{g}]
is another representative then
(\widehat{K}_{g})_{ij}=\Omega(\widehat{K}_{\widetilde{g}})_{ij}
.

•

If
\widecheck{\nabla}
is another Weyl connection then

(\widecheck{K}_{\widetilde{g}})_{ij}=(\widehat{K}_{\widetilde{g}})_{ij}-\varepsilon(\widecheck{\kappa}_{\widetilde{g}}-\widehat{\kappa}_{\widetilde{g}})_{\perp}\,\widetilde{h}_{ij}\,,

(2.45)

where
\widetilde{h}
is the induced metric by
\widetilde{g}
on
\mathcal{S}
and
\widecheck{\kappa}_{\widetilde{g}}
,
\widehat{\kappa}_{\widetilde{g}}
are the covector fields associated to
\widecheck{\nabla}
and
\widehat{\nabla}
with respect to
{\widetilde{g}}
.

•

By combining the two precedent remarks, if
\widetilde{g}
and
g=\Omega^{2}\widetilde{g}
are two representatives then the extrinsic curvatures
4
4

4

In this particular instance, the metric is omitted in the notation of the extrinsic curvature for simplicity.

K_{ij}
,
\widetilde{K}_{ij}
of their Levi-Civita connections
\nabla
,
\widetilde{\nabla}
with respect to respectively
g
,
\widetilde{g}
are linked by

K_{ij}=\Omega\widetilde{K}_{ij}-\varepsilon\langle d\ln\Omega,e_{\perp}\rangle\,h_{ij}\,,

(2.46)

where
e_{\perp}
is unitary for the metric
g
. The respective traces
K
and
\widetilde{K}
are thus linked by

K=\Omega^{-1}\widetilde{K}-\varepsilon n\langle d\ln\Omega,e_{\perp}\rangle\,.

(2.47)

•

The indices of
\widehat{K}_{\widetilde{g}}
are raised or lowered with respect to
\widetilde{g}
. For instance,

(\widehat{K}_{\widetilde{g}})_{i}{}^{j}:=\widetilde{g}^{jk}(\widehat{K}_{\widetilde{g}})_{ik}\,.\qed

Lemma 2.58
.

In a frame field
(e_{\perp},(e_{\bf i}))
adapted to
\mathcal{S}
with respect to a representative
\widetilde{g}\in[\widetilde{g}]
, one has on
\mathcal{S}

\displaystyle\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf i}{}^{\perp}{}_{\bf j}

\displaystyle=(\widehat{K}_{\widetilde{g}})_{\bf ij}\,,

\displaystyle\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf i}{}^{\bf j}{}_{\perp}

\displaystyle=-\varepsilon(\widehat{K}_{\widetilde{g}})_{\bf i}{}^{\bf j}\,,

\displaystyle\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf i}{}^{\perp}{}_{\perp}

\displaystyle=(\widehat{\kappa}_{\widetilde{g}})_{\bf i}\,,

where
\widehat{\kappa}_{\widetilde{g}}
is the covector field associated to the Weyl connection
\widehat{\nabla}
with respect to
\widetilde{g}
and
\varepsilon:=\widetilde{g}(e_{\perp},e_{\perp})\in\{\pm 1\}
.

Proof.

The first equation follows straightforwardly from the definition of the extrinsic curvature. Moreover,

\widetilde{g}_{\bf jk}\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf i}{}^{\bf k}{}_{\perp}=\widetilde{g}(e_{\bf k},\widehat{\nabla}_{e_{\bf i}}e_{\perp})=e_{\bf i}(\widetilde{g}_{\bf k\perp})+2(\widehat{\kappa}_{\widetilde{g}})_{\bf i}\widetilde{g}_{\bf k\perp}-\widetilde{g}(\widehat{\nabla}_{e_{\bf i}}e_{\bf k},e_{\perp})=-\varepsilon\langle\omega^{\perp},\widehat{\nabla}_{e_{\bf i}}e_{\bf k})=-\varepsilon(\widehat{K}_{\widetilde{g}})_{\bf ik}\,,

and

\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf i}{}^{\perp}{}_{\perp}=\langle\omega^{\perp},\widehat{\nabla}_{e_{\bf i}}e_{\perp}\rangle=\varepsilon\widetilde{g}(e_{\perp},\widehat{\nabla}_{e_{\bf i}}e_{\perp})=\varepsilon\left(\frac{e_{\bf i}(\widetilde{g}_{\perp\perp})}{2}+(\widehat{\kappa}_{\widetilde{g}})_{\bf i}\widetilde{g}_{\perp\perp}\right)=(\widehat{\kappa}_{\widetilde{g}})_{\bf i}\,.\qed

Definition 2.59
.

The hypersurface
\mathcal{S}
is said to

•

be
totally geodesic for a Weyl connection

\widehat{\nabla}
if the extrinsic curvature
\widehat{K}_{g}
with respect to any (and thus all) representative
g\in[\widetilde{g}]
vanishes identically,

•

be
umbilical
if the extrinsic curvature
\widehat{K}_{g}
of any (and thus all) Weyl connection
\widehat{\nabla}
with respect to any (and thus all) representative
g\in[\widetilde{g}]
is pure trace,

•

have
zero mean curvature for a Weyl connection

\widehat{\nabla}
if the extrinsic curvature
\widehat{K}_{g}
with respect to any (and thus all) representative
g\in[\widetilde{g}]
is trace-free.

Lemma 2.60
.

i)

There exists a Weyl connection
\widehat{\nabla}
such that
\mathcal{S}
has zero mean curvature for
\widehat{\nabla}
.

ii)

\mathcal{S}
is umbilical if and only if there exists a Weyl connection
\widehat{\nabla}
such that
\mathcal{S}
is totally geodesic for
\widehat{\nabla}
.

Proof.

i)
Let
\widetilde{g}\in[\widetilde{g}]
be any representative and
\widehat{\nabla}
be any Weyl connection. Take a smooth covector field
\kappa
on
\mathcal{M}
such that
\kappa_{\perp}=(\widehat{\kappa}_{\widetilde{g}})_{\perp}+\widetilde{h}^{ij}(\widehat{K}_{\widetilde{g}})_{ij}/3
on
\mathcal{S}
. Then the Weyl connection associated to
\kappa
with respect to
\widetilde{g}
by
2.24
works.

ii)
By
i)
, there exists a Weyl connection
\widehat{\nabla}
such that
\mathcal{S}
has zero mean curvature for
\widehat{\nabla}
. Since
\mathcal{S}
is also umbilical, one deduces that it is totally geodesic for
\widehat{\nabla}
.
∎

Lemma 2.61
.

Let
x:I\to\mathcal{S}
be a smooth curve on
\mathcal{S}
and
\widehat{\nabla}
be a Weyl connection on
(\mathcal{M},[\widetilde{g}])
. Denote by
X
the smooth vector field obtained by parallel transport along the curve
x
of a vector
X_{\star}\in T_{x(\tau_{\star})}\mathcal{M}
for some
\tau_{\star}\in I
, that is

\widehat{\nabla}_{\dot{x}}X=0\,,\qquad X|_{\tau_{\star}}=X_{\star}\,.

If
\mathcal{S}
is totally geodesic for
\widehat{\nabla}
and
X_{\star}
is tangent to
\mathcal{S}
then
X
remains tangent to
\mathcal{S}
.

Proof.

Take a representative
g\in[\widetilde{g}]
and a frame field
(e_{\perp},(e_{\bf i}))
adapted to
\mathcal{S}
with respect to
g
. Then

0=(\widehat{\nabla}_{\dot{x}}X)^{\perp}=\dot{x}(X^{\perp})+\langle\widehat{\kappa}_{g},\dot{x}\rangle X^{\perp}+(\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf ij}\dot{x}^{\bf i}X^{\bf j}\,.

Thus, assuming that
\mathcal{S}
is totally geodesic for
\widehat{\nabla}
,
X^{\perp}
vanishes identically along
x
if and only if
X_{\star}^{\perp}=0
.
∎

2.6.4
Decomposition of a Weyl candidate

The following lemma gives a decomposition of Weyl candidates (see
2.46
) at a non-null hypersurface into algebraically independent tensor fields. In the case of the Weyl tensor, this decomposition is closely related to the Codazzi equations as we will see in
2.64
.

Lemma 2.62
.

Let
g\in[\widetilde{g}]
be a representative and
Q^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\rho}
be a Weyl candidate on
(\mathcal{M},g)
. Define the following tensor fields on
\mathcal{M}
, using an adapted frame field
(e_{\perp},(e_{\bf i}))
to
\mathcal{S}
with respect to
g
whose dual frame field is denoted by
(\omega^{\perp},(\omega^{\bf i}))
,

•

E:=Q^{\perp}{}_{\bf i\perp j}\,\omega^{\bf i}\otimes\omega^{\bf j}
which is a symmetric trace-free 2-tensor field,

•

M:=Q^{\perp}{}_{\bf kij}\,\omega^{\bf i}\otimes\omega^{\bf j}\otimes\omega^{\bf k}
which is a Cotton candidate (see
2.47
),

•

P:=\left(Q^{\bf i}{}_{\bf jkl}+\frac{2}{n-2}S_{\bf j[k}{}^{\bf im}E_{\bf l]m}\right)e_{\bf i}\otimes\omega^{\bf j}\otimes\omega^{\bf k}\otimes\omega^{\bf l}
which is a Weyl candidate.

Then the following decomposition holds

Q_{\alpha\beta\mu\rho}=P_{\alpha\beta\mu\rho}+2\left((\omega^{\perp})_{[\alpha}M_{|\mu\rho|\beta]}+(\omega^{\perp})_{[\mu}M_{|\alpha\beta|\rho]}\right)-\frac{2}{n-2}\left(X_{\alpha[\mu}E_{\rho]\beta}-X_{\beta[\mu}E_{\rho]\alpha}\right)\,,

(2.48)

where
X_{\alpha\beta}:=g_{\alpha\beta}-(n-1)\varepsilon(\omega^{\perp})_{\alpha}(\omega^{\perp})_{\beta}
.

Remarks
.

•

The restriction of
E
,
M
and
P
on
\mathcal{S}
are tangent to
\mathcal{S}
and do not depend on the choice of adapted frame field
(e_{\perp},(e_{\bf i}))
. In addition, the restriction of
E
and
P
are conformally invariant, whereas the one of
M
is conformally invariant of weight
1
.

•

Consider the special case
n=3
. One has
P=0
since Weyl candidates vanish identically in dimension 3. To recover the usual Bel decomposition, assume that
\mathcal{M}
is oriented and denote the volume forms on
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{S}
by
\epsilon
. Recall that the dual tensor field
(Q\star)^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\nu}
is also a Weyl candidate, see
Section

2.5.3
. Then one defines the symmetric and trace-free 2-tensor

H:=(Q\star)^{\perp}{}_{\bf i\perp j}\,\omega^{\bf i}\otimes\omega^{\bf j}\,.

The tensor fields
H
and
M
are duals of each other on
\mathcal{S}

H_{\bf ij}=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{\bf i}{}^{\bf kl}M_{\bf klj}\,\quad\text{or equivalently}\quad M_{\bf ijk}=-\varepsilon\epsilon_{\bf ij}{}^{\bf l}H_{\bf lk}\,.

(2.49)

By analogy with the decomposition of the electromagnetic tensor field,
E
and
H
are called the electric part and the magnetic part of the Weyl candidate
Q
on
\mathcal{S}
. Furthermore, since

((Q\star)\star)^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\lambda\mu}=-Q^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\lambda\mu}\,,

the electric and magnetic parts of
Q
and
Q\star
are linked by

E(Q\star)=H(Q)\,,\qquad H(Q\star)=-E(Q)\,.\qed

(2.50)

2.6.5
The Codazzi equations for Weyl connections

We detail here the Codazzi equations for a Weyl connection
\widehat{\nabla}
. In comparison with the Levi-Civita case, care must be taken since the connections do not preserve the metric and the Riemann tensors have fewer symmetries.

Let
\widehat{D}
be the induced Weyl connection on
\mathcal{S}
by a Weyl connection
\widehat{\nabla}
. The Riemann and Ricci tensors of
\widehat{\nabla}
and
\widehat{D}
are linked by the Codazzi equations stated below.

Lemma 2.63
.

The Gauss-Codazzi and Codazzi-Mainardi equations write as follows, in an frame field
(e_{\bf a})=(e_{\perp},(e_{\bf i}))
adapted to
\mathcal{S}
with respect to a representative
g\in[\widetilde{g}]
,

\displaystyle\widehat{R}^{\bf i}{}_{\bf jkl}

\displaystyle=\widehat{r}^{\bf i}{}_{\bf jkl}-2\varepsilon(\widehat{K}_{g})^{\bf i}{}_{\bf[k}(\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf l]j}\,,

(2.51a)

\displaystyle\widehat{R}^{\perp}{}_{\bf jkl}

\displaystyle=2\widehat{D}_{\bf[k}(\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf l]j}+2(\widehat{\kappa}_{g})_{\bf[k}(\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf l]j}\,,

(2.51b)

\displaystyle-\varepsilon\widehat{R}^{\bf i}{}_{\bf\perp kl}

\displaystyle=2\widehat{D}_{\bf[k}(\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf l]}{}^{\bf i}-2(\widehat{\kappa}_{g})_{\bf[k}(\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf l]}{}^{\bf i}\,,

(2.51c)

where
\widehat{R}^{\bf a}{}_{\bf bcd}
,
\widehat{r}^{\bf i}{}_{\bf jkl}
are the frame components of the Riemann tensor of
\widehat{\nabla}
and
\widehat{D}
respectively,
\widehat{K}_{g}
is the extrinsic curvature of
\widehat{\nabla}
with respect to
g
, see
Section

2.6.3
.
Their contractions give

\displaystyle\widehat{R}_{\bf jl}-\widehat{r}_{\bf jl}

\displaystyle=\widehat{R}^{\perp}{}_{\bf j\perp l}-\varepsilon\left((\widehat{K}_{g})^{\bf i}{}_{\bf i}(\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf jl}-(\widehat{K}_{g})^{\bf i}{}_{\bf l}(\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf ij}\right)\,,

(2.52a)

\displaystyle-\widehat{R}^{\perp}{}_{\bf l}=-\varepsilon\widehat{R}_{\bf\perp l}

\displaystyle=\widehat{D}_{\bf i}(\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf l}{}^{\bf i}-\widehat{D}_{\bf l}(\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf i}{}^{\bf i}-(\widehat{\kappa}_{g})_{\bf i}(\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf l}{}^{\bf i}+(\widehat{\kappa}_{g})_{\bf l}(\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf i}{}^{\bf i}\,.

(2.52b)

Proof.

By definition of the Riemann tensor,

\displaystyle\widehat{R}^{\bf i}{}_{\bf jkl}

\displaystyle:=\langle\omega^{\bf i},\widehat{\nabla}_{e_{\bf k}}\widehat{\nabla}_{e_{\bf l}}e_{\bf j}-\widehat{\nabla}_{e_{\bf l}}\widehat{\nabla}_{e_{\bf k}}e_{\bf j}-\widehat{\nabla}_{[e_{\bf k},e_{\bf l}]}e_{\bf j}\rangle\,,

\displaystyle\widehat{R}^{\perp}{}_{\bf jkl}

\displaystyle:=\langle\omega^{\perp},\widehat{\nabla}_{e_{\bf k}}\widehat{\nabla}_{e_{\bf l}}e_{\bf j}-\widehat{\nabla}_{e_{\bf l}}\widehat{\nabla}_{e_{\bf k}}e_{\bf j}-\widehat{\nabla}_{[e_{\bf k},e_{\bf l}]}e_{\bf j}\rangle\,,

\displaystyle\widehat{R}^{\bf i}{}_{\bf\perp kl}

\displaystyle:=\langle\omega^{\bf i},\widehat{\nabla}_{e_{\bf k}}\widehat{\nabla}_{e_{\bf l}}e_{\perp}-\widehat{\nabla}_{e_{\bf l}}\widehat{\nabla}_{e_{\bf k}}e_{\perp}-\widehat{\nabla}_{[e_{\bf k},e_{\bf l}]}e_{\perp}\rangle\,.

Yet

\displaystyle\widehat{\nabla}_{e_{\bf k}}\widehat{\nabla}_{e_{\bf l}}e_{\bf j}

\displaystyle=\widehat{\nabla}_{e_{\bf k}}\left(\widehat{D}_{e_{\bf l}}e_{\bf j}+\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf l}{}^{\perp}{}_{\bf j}e_{\perp}\right)

\displaystyle=\widehat{D}_{e_{\bf k}}\widehat{D}_{e_{\bf l}}e_{\bf j}+\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf l}{}^{\bf m}{}_{\bf j}\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf k}{}^{\perp}{}_{\bf m}e_{\perp}+e_{\bf k}(\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf l}{}^{\perp}{}_{\bf j})e_{\perp}+\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf l}{}^{\perp}{}_{\bf j}\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf k}{}^{\bf a}{}_{\perp}e_{\bf a}

\displaystyle=\widehat{D}_{e_{\bf k}}\widehat{D}_{e_{\bf l}}e_{\bf j}+\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf l}{}^{\bf m}{}_{\bf j}(\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf km}e_{\perp}+e_{\bf k}((\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf lj})e_{\perp}-\varepsilon(\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf lj}(\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf k}{}^{\bf m}e_{\bf m}+(\widehat{\kappa}_{g})_{\bf k}(\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf lj}e_{\perp}

\displaystyle=\widehat{D}_{e_{\bf k}}\widehat{D}_{e_{\bf l}}e_{\bf j}-\varepsilon(\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf lj}(\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf k}{}^{\bf m}e_{\bf m}+\left(e_{\bf k}((\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf lj})+\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf l}{}^{\bf m}{}_{\bf j}(\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf km}+(\widehat{\kappa}_{g})_{\bf k}(\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf lj}\right)e_{\perp}\,,

and

\displaystyle\widehat{\nabla}_{[e_{\bf k},e_{\bf l}]}e_{\bf j}

\displaystyle=\widehat{D}_{[e_{\bf k},e_{\bf l}]}e_{\bf j}+\langle\omega^{\bf m},[e_{\bf k},e_{\bf l}]\rangle\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf m}{}^{\perp}{}_{\bf j}e_{\perp}

\displaystyle=\widehat{D}_{[e_{\bf k},e_{\bf l}]}e_{\bf j}+\left(\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf k}{}^{\bf m}{}_{\bf l}-\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf l}{}^{\bf m}{}_{\bf k}\right)\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf m}{}^{\perp}{}_{\bf j}e_{\perp}

\displaystyle=\widehat{D}_{[e_{\bf k},e_{\bf l}]}e_{\bf j}+\left(\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf k}{}^{\bf m}{}_{\bf l}-\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf l}{}^{\bf m}{}_{\bf k}\right)(\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf mj}e_{\perp}\,.

Equations (
2.51a
) and (
2.51b
) follow. Moreover,

\displaystyle\langle\omega^{\bf i},\widehat{\nabla}_{e_{\bf k}}\widehat{\nabla}_{e_{\bf l}}e_{\perp}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\omega^{\bf i},\widehat{\nabla}_{e_{\bf k}}\left(\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf l}{}^{\bf j}{}_{\perp}e_{\bf j}+\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf l}{}^{\perp}{}_{\perp}e_{\perp}\right)\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\omega^{\bf i},e_{\bf k}(\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf l}{}^{\bf j}{}_{\perp})e_{\bf j}+\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf l}{}^{\bf j}{}_{\perp}\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf k}{}^{\bf a}{}_{\bf j}e_{\bf a}+e_{\bf k}(\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf l}{}^{\perp}{}_{\perp})e_{\perp}+\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf l}{}^{\perp}{}_{\perp}\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf k}{}^{\bf a}{}_{\perp}e_{\bf a}\rangle

\displaystyle=e_{\bf k}(\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf l}{}^{\bf i}{}_{\perp})+\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf l}{}^{\bf j}{}_{\perp}\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf k}{}^{\bf i}{}_{\bf j}+\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf l}{}^{\perp}{}_{\perp}\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf k}{}^{\bf i}{}_{\perp}

\displaystyle=-\varepsilon\left(e_{\bf k}((\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf l}{}^{\bf i})+\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf k}{}^{\bf i}{}_{\bf j}(\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf l}{}^{\bf j}+(\widehat{\kappa}_{g})_{\bf l}(\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf k}{}^{\bf i}\right)\,,

and

\langle\omega^{\bf i},\widehat{\nabla}_{[e_{\bf k},e_{\bf l}]}e_{\perp}\rangle=\langle\omega^{\bf j},[e_{\bf k},e_{\bf l}]\rangle\langle\omega^{\bf i},\widehat{\nabla}_{e_{\bf j}}e_{\perp}\rangle=-\varepsilon\left(\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf k}{}^{\bf j}{}_{\bf l}-\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf l}{}^{\bf j}{}_{\bf k}\right)(\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf j}{}^{\bf i}\,.

Hence equation (
2.51c
).
∎

One can rewrite the Codazzi equations in terms of the Weyl and Schouten tensors instead of the Riemann and Ricci tensors.

Corollary 2.64
.

Let
g\in[\widetilde{g}]
be a representative,
(e_{\perp},(e_{\bf i}))
be a frame field adapted to
\mathcal{S}
with respect to
g
and
(E,M,P)
be the decomposition defined in
2.62
of the Weyl tensor
\widehat{W}^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\nu}
of
\widehat{\nabla}
. Then

\displaystyle\widehat{L}_{\bf lj}-\widehat{l}_{\bf lj}

\displaystyle=\frac{E_{\bf lj}}{n-2}-\varepsilon F_{\bf lj}(\widehat{K}_{g})\,,

(2.53a)

\displaystyle\widehat{w}^{\bf i}{}_{\bf jkl}

\displaystyle=P^{\bf i}{}_{\bf jkl}+\varepsilon\left(2(\widehat{K}_{g})^{\bf i}{}_{\bf[k}(\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf l]j}-2S_{\bf j[k}{}^{\bf im}F_{\bf l]m}(\widehat{K}_{g})\right)\,,

(2.53b)

\displaystyle\widehat{L}_{\bf k\perp}

\displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon}{n-1}h^{\bf jl}G_{\bf klj}(\widehat{K}_{g},\widehat{\kappa}_{g})\,,

(2.53c)

\displaystyle M_{\bf klj}

\displaystyle=G_{\bf klj}(\widehat{K}_{g},\widehat{\kappa}_{g})+\frac{2}{n-1}h_{\bf j[k}h^{\bf ip}G_{\bf l]ip}(\widehat{K}_{g},\widehat{\kappa}_{g})\,,

(2.53d)

where
\widehat{L}_{\bf ab}
,
\widehat{l}_{\bf ij}
are the frame components of the Schouten tensor of
\widehat{\nabla}
and
\widehat{D}
respectively,
\widehat{K}_{g}
is the extrinsic curvature of
\widehat{\nabla}
with respect to
g
and

\displaystyle F_{\bf lj}(\widehat{K}_{g})

\displaystyle:=\frac{(\widehat{K}_{g})^{\bf i}{}_{\bf i}(\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf lj}-(\widehat{K}_{g})^{\bf i}{}_{\bf l}(\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf ij}}{n-2}-\frac{\left((\widehat{K}_{g})^{\bf i}{}_{\bf i}\right)^{2}-(\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf ik}(\widehat{K}_{g})^{\bf ik}}{2(n-1)(n-2)}h_{\bf lj}\,,

(2.54a)

\displaystyle G_{\bf klj}(\widehat{K}_{g},\widehat{\kappa}_{g})

\displaystyle:=2\widehat{D}_{\bf[k}(\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf l]j}+2(\widehat{\kappa}_{g})_{\bf[k}(\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf l]j}\,.

(2.54b)

Remark
.

If
\mathcal{S}
is umbilical, write
(\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf ij}=fh_{\bf ij}
. Then

\displaystyle F_{\bf lj}(\widehat{K}_{g})

\displaystyle=\frac{f^{2}}{2}h_{\bf lj}\,,

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad G_{\bf klj}(\widehat{K}_{g},\widehat{\kappa}_{g})

\displaystyle=-2h_{\bf j[k}\left((df)_{\bf l]}-(\widehat{\kappa}_{g})_{\bf l]}f\right)\,,

\displaystyle P^{\bf i}{}_{\bf jkl}

\displaystyle=\widehat{w}^{\bf i}{}_{\bf jkl}\,,

\displaystyle\qquad E_{\bf lj}

\displaystyle=(n-2)\left(\widehat{L}_{\bf lj}-\widehat{l}_{\bf lj}+\frac{\varepsilon f^{2}}{2}h_{\bf lj}\right)\,,

\displaystyle M_{\bf klj}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\qquad\widehat{L}_{\bf k\perp}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\left((df)_{\bf k}-(\widehat{\kappa}_{g})_{\bf k}f\right)\,.

∎

Proof.

From the decomposition (
2.22
) of the Riemann tensor of a Weyl connection, one has

\displaystyle\widehat{R}^{\bf i}{}_{\bf jkl}

\displaystyle=\widehat{W}^{\bf i}{}_{\bf jkl}+2S_{\bf j[k}{}^{\bf ia}\widehat{L}_{\bf l]a}=\widehat{W}^{\bf i}{}_{\bf jkl}+2S_{\bf j[k}{}^{\bf im}\widehat{L}_{\bf l]m}\,,

(2.55a)

\displaystyle\widehat{r}^{\bf i}{}_{\bf jkl}

\displaystyle=\widehat{w}^{\bf i}{}_{\bf jkl}+2S_{\bf j[k}{}^{\bf ia}\widehat{l}_{\bf l]a}=\widehat{w}^{\bf i}{}_{\bf jkl}+2S_{\bf j[k}{}^{\bf im}\widehat{l}_{\bf l]m}\,,

(2.55b)

\displaystyle\widehat{R}^{\perp}{}_{\bf jkl}

\displaystyle=\widehat{W}^{\perp}{}_{\bf jkl}+2S_{\bf j[k}{}^{\bf\perp a}\widehat{L}_{\bf l]a}=\widehat{W}^{\perp}{}_{\bf jkl}-2h_{\bf j[k}\widehat{L}_{\bf l]}{}^{\perp}\,.

(2.55c)

By combining (
2.51a
) with (
2.55a
)-(
2.55b
), one deduces

\widehat{W}^{\bf i}{}_{\bf jkl}-\widehat{w}^{\bf i}{}_{\bf jkl}+2S_{\bf j[k}{}^{\bf im}\left(\widehat{L}_{\bf l]m}-\widehat{l}_{\bf l]m}\right)=\widehat{R}^{\bf i}{}_{\bf jkl}-\widehat{r}^{\bf i}{}_{\bf jkl}=-2\varepsilon(\widehat{K}_{g})^{\bf i}{}_{\bf[k}(\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf l]j}\,.

By contracting the indices
({\bf i},{\bf k})
and denoting
\widehat{A}_{\bf jl}:=\widehat{L}_{\bf jl}-\widehat{l}_{\bf jl}
, one has

-\widehat{W}^{\perp}{}_{\bf j\perp l}+n\widehat{A}_{\bf[lj]}+(n-2)\widehat{A}_{\bf(lj)}+h_{\bf lj}h^{\bf ik}\widehat{A}_{\bf ik}=-\varepsilon\left((\widehat{K}_{g})^{\bf i}{}_{\bf i}(\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf lj}-(\widehat{K}_{g})^{\bf i}{}_{\bf l}(\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf ij}\right)\,.

Taking the anti-symmetric part gives
\widehat{A}_{\bf[jl]}=0
(this can also be deduced from (
2.24a
) and
2.56
). By taking the trace with respect to
h
,

2(n-1)h^{\bf jl}\widehat{A}_{\bf jl}=-\varepsilon\left(\left((\widehat{K}_{g})^{\bf i}{}_{\bf i}\right)^{2}-(\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf jl}(\widehat{K}_{g})^{\bf jl}\right)\,.

Injecting the traces back leads to (
2.53a
) and then (
2.53b
).

Furthermore, by combining (
2.51b
) with (
2.55c
), one has

\widehat{W}^{\perp}{}_{\bf jkl}-2h_{\bf j[k}\widehat{L}_{\bf l]}{}^{\perp}=\widehat{R}^{\perp}{}_{\bf jkl}=2\widehat{D}_{\bf[k}(\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf l]j}+2(\widehat{\kappa}_{g})_{\bf[k}(\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf l]j}\,.

Taking the trace with respect to
h
on the pair
({\bf j},{\bf l})
gives

\varepsilon(n-1)\widehat{L}_{\bf k\perp}=h^{\bf jl}\left(2\widehat{D}_{\bf[k}(\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf l]j}+2(\widehat{\kappa}_{g})_{\bf[k}(\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf l]j}\right)\,.

Hence (
2.53c
) and then (
2.53d
) follows by injecting the trace back.
∎

3
Asymptotically Anti-de Sitter spaces

This section is dedicated to defining asymptotically Anti-de Sitter (aAdS) spaces and presenting their standard properties. We start by introducing the definition used in this article and comparing it to the literature in
Section

3.1
.

We then recall in
Section

3.2
the notorious expansion of aAdS metrics satisfying the vacuum Einstein equations with a negative cosmological constant derived by Fefferman and Graham in
[
31
]
. This result was obtained in order to solve the so-called Poincaré problem, equivalent to the ambient space problem, which can be interpreted as a certain boundary value problem for aAdS spaces. Their method gives a formal series, described in
3.8
, with two free data: the boundary metric and the trace-free and divergence-free part of the boundary stress-energy tensor. These are discussed in detail and a special effort is made to justify their geometric interpretation as an equivalence class for boundaries of odd dimensions.
Section

3.2
concludes with the asymptotic expansions of some curvature tensors obtained from the expansion of the metric. In particular, one important result of this section, equation (
3.22
), is crucial to interpret geometrically the analytic boundary conditions in
Section

4.3
.

For illustrative purposes, several examples of aAdS spaces are provided in
Section

3.3
. Finally, we introduced potential boundary stress-energy tensors inferred from these examples and discuss the interactions between boundary stress-energy tensors and conformal Killing fields in
Section

3.4
.

3.1
Definitions

Let us state the definition of asymptotically Anti-de Sitter spaces using manifolds with corners introduced in
Section

2.2
.

Definition 3.1
.

An
asymptotically Anti-de Sitter (aAdS) space
is a triple
(\mathcal{M},\mathfrak{I},\widetilde{g})
where

•

\mathcal{M}
is a (n+1)-dimensional smooth manifold with corners,

•

\mathfrak{I}
is a non-empty set of disjoint boundary hypersurfaces of
\mathcal{M}
,

•

\widetilde{g}
is a smooth Lorentzian metric on
\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathfrak{I}
,

such that there exists a boundary defining function
x
of
\mathfrak{I}
verifying

i)

the associated
rescaled metric

x^{2}\widetilde{g}
extends as a
\mathcal{C}^{2}
-metric up to
\mathfrak{I}
,

ii)

one has
(x^{2}\widetilde{g})^{-1}(dx,dx)=1
on
\mathfrak{I}
.

Each boundary hypersurface
\mathfrak{S}\in\mathfrak{I}
is equipped with the conformal class
[\mathfrak{h}]
, where
\mathfrak{h}
is the metric on
\mathfrak{S}
induced by
x^{2}\widetilde{g}
, to form a Lorentzian conformal structure. The set
\mathfrak{I}
is called the
conformal boundary
of
(\mathcal{M},\mathfrak{I},\widetilde{g})
.

Remarks
.

•

In the definition and in the whole article, the union
\bigcup_{\mathfrak{S}\in\mathfrak{I}}\mathfrak{S}\subset\mathcal{M}
is identified to
\mathfrak{I}
and identically denoted for simplicity.

•

3.1
is independent of the choice of a boundary defining function of
\mathfrak{I}
. Indeed, assume
i)-ii)
hold for a boundary defining function
x
of
\mathfrak{I}
and let
x^{\prime}
be another boundary defining function of
\mathfrak{I}
. By
2.12
, one can write
x^{\prime}=x\Theta
with
\Theta\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M},\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\star})
. Thus
(x^{\prime})^{2}\widetilde{g}=\Theta^{2}x^{2}\widetilde{g}
also extends as a
\mathcal{C}^{2}
-metric on
\mathcal{M}
and
((x^{\prime})^{2}\widetilde{g})^{-1}(dx^{\prime},dx^{\prime})=(x^{2}\widetilde{g})^{-1}(dx,dx)=1
on
\mathfrak{I}
. Moreover, the induced metric on
\mathfrak{S}
verifies

\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}=\Theta|_{\mathfrak{S}}^{2}\,\mathfrak{h}\,.

(3.1)

This explains why
\mathfrak{S}
is equipped with the conformal class
[\mathfrak{h}]
rather than the metric
\mathfrak{h}
which is dependent on the boundary defining function.

•

All rescaled metrics of an aAdS space have the same regularity thanks to
2.12
. The common regularity classes are
\mathcal{C}^{m}(\mathcal{M})
for
m\geq 2
and
\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathcal{M})\cap\mathcal{C}^{m}_{phg}(\mathcal{M}\,|\,\mathfrak{I},E)
for
m\geq 0
and
E
an index set, see
Section

2.2.3
for the definitions.

•

If
(\mathcal{M},\mathfrak{I},\widetilde{g})
is an aAdS space then
(\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathfrak{I},\widetilde{g})
is approximately Einstein near
\mathfrak{I}
for the normalised negative cosmological constant
\Lambda=-n(n-1)/2
. Indeed, on any compact neighbourhood of a point
p\in\mathfrak{S}
,

\Ric(\widetilde{g})+n\widetilde{g}=\mathcal{O}\left({\frac{1}{x}}\right)\,.

(3.2)

This implies that
\widetilde{R}=-n(n+1)+\mathcal{O}\left({x}\right)
where
\widetilde{R}
is the scalar curvature of
\widetilde{g}
.

•

3.1
allows for various topologies and geometries at the conformal boundary, not only that of AdS. In this sense, it is more general than the definition of asymptotically flat spacetimes. See
[
48
]
for a definition imposing the same topology and conformal class on the boundary as AdS.

•

This definition is based on
[
30
, Definition 3.1]
. Moreover, if the rescaled metrics are smooth on
\mathcal{M}
then it is similar to
[
88
, Definition 1]
with the weak decay rates.

•

Former definitions of aAdS spaces such as Friedrich’s
[
38
]
are based on the vacuum Einstein equations and conformal embeddings, in the lines of Penrose’s asymptotic simplicity
[
75
]
. However, multiple non-equivalent conformal embeddings of a space exist since the differential structure at the conformal boundary thereby created is a priori not imposed. Furthermore,
3.1
allows for coupling of the Einstein equations with matter fields as long as they are compatible with the existence of a smooth conformal boundary.

•

Even though one cannot define the physical extrinsic curvature of a connected component
\mathfrak{S}
of the conformal boundary
\mathfrak{I}
since the metric
\widetilde{g}
is singular on
\mathfrak{S}
, the transformation rule (
2.46
) implies that the physical mean curvature of
\mathfrak{S}
would be equal to
-n
(for the outward-pointing normal choice). ∎

Anticipating the initial data problem, one can define asymptotically hyperbolic spaces in a similar manner. Most of the above remarks also apply in this context.

Definition 3.2
.

An
asymptotically Hyperbolic (aH) space
is a triple
(\mathcal{S},\not{\mathfrak{I}},\widetilde{h})
where

•

\mathcal{S}
is a n-dimensional smooth manifold with corners,

•

\not{\mathfrak{I}}
is a non-empty set of disjoint boundary hypersurfaces of
\mathcal{S}
,

•

\widetilde{h}
is a smooth Riemannian metric on
\mathcal{S}\setminus\not{\mathfrak{I}}
,

such that there exists a boundary defining function
x
of
\not{\mathfrak{I}}
verifying

i)

the associated
rescaled metric

x^{2}\widetilde{h}
extends as a
\mathcal{C}^{2}
-metric up to
\not{\mathfrak{I}}
,

ii)

one has
(x^{2}\widetilde{h})^{-1}(dx,dx)=1
on
\not{\mathfrak{I}}
.

Each boundary hypersurface
\mathfrak{S}\in\not{\mathfrak{I}}
is equipped with the conformal class
[\not{\mathfrak{h}}]
, where
\not{\mathfrak{h}}
is the metric on
\mathfrak{S}
induced by
x^{2}\widetilde{h}
, to form a Riemannian conformal structure. The set
\not{\mathfrak{I}}
is called the
conformal boundary
of
(\mathcal{S},\not{\mathfrak{I}},\widetilde{h})
.

3.2
Asymptotic expansions at the conformal boundary

The definition of aAdS spaces is designed so that the metric
\widetilde{g}
is approximately Einstein near the conformal boundary, see (
3.2
). Fefferman and Graham
[
31
]
, see also their comprehensive book
[
32
]
, derived iteratively an asymptotic expansion of the rescaled metric
g:=x^{2}\widetilde{g}
ensuring that the Einstein equations with (the normalised) negative cosmological constant
\Lambda=-n(n-1)/2
are verified by
\widetilde{g}
nearby the conformal boundary at any given order. This expansion will be referred to as the Fefferman-Graham expansion (FG expansion).

We present first the gauge used to obtain the FG expansion in
Section

3.2.1
before stating and commenting the FG expansion in
Section

3.2.2
. The Einstein equations fully impose all the terms of the expansion apart from two which are therefore called the free data on the conformal boundary. We review in length how to geometrically interpret these free data from their gauge-dependent definition as the conformal class and the boundary stress-energy tensor class. Finally, asymptotic expansions for some curvature tensor fields implied by the FG expansion are derived in
Section

3.2.3
. This enables us to identify the boundary stress-energy tensor in the asymptotic expansions of (part of) the Weyl tensor, see equation (
3.22
), which will help us to pass from analytic to geometric boundary conditions in
Section

4.3
.

In this whole section, we consider an aAdS space
(\mathcal{M},\mathfrak{I},\widetilde{g})
of dimension
n+1\geq 3
.

3.2.1
Fefferman-Graham gauges

Definition 3.3
.

A
Fefferman-Graham gauge
(FG gauge) is a chart
(\phi,\mathcal{U},1)
with
\mathcal{U}\cap\mathfrak{I}\neq\varnothing
and whose coordinates are denoted by
\phi:=(x,y^{0},\dots,y^{n-1})
such that

i)

x
is a local boundary defining function of
\mathfrak{I}
on
\mathcal{U}
,

ii)

the rescaled metric has the following form on
\mathcal{U}

g:=x^{2}\widetilde{g}=dx^{2}+\underline{\mathfrak{h}}(x)

(3.3)

with
\underline{\mathfrak{h}}:x\mapsto\underline{\mathfrak{h}}(x)
a
\mathcal{C}^{2}
-map with values into smooth Lorentzian metrics on the level sets of
x
.

Remarks
.

•

The FG gauge is a geodesic gauge or a
1+n
decomposition with respect to the spacelike coordinate
x
with zero shift and lapse
1
for the rescaled metric
g=x^{2}\widetilde{g}
.

•

The frame field
(\partial_{x},(\partial_{y^{i}}))
is an adapted frame field to the level sets of
x
on
\mathcal{U}
with respect to the rescaled metric
g
, see
Section

2.6.1
.

•

The construction of FG gauges for aAdS spaces can be derived from
[
45
, Section 5]
. Let us recall the proof which decomposes into three steps.

a)

Take any local boundary defining function
x
of
\mathfrak{I}
and any smooth function
\omega
on
\mathfrak{I}
.

b)

Let
\theta
be a smooth solution on a neighbourhood of
\mathfrak{I}
to the following non-characteristic first order hyperbolic partial differential equation, with
\omega
as initial data on
\mathfrak{I}
,

2(\grad_{g}x)\cdot\theta+xg^{-1}(d\theta,d\theta)=\frac{1-g^{-1}(dx,dx)}{x}\,,

where
\grad_{g}x
is the gradient of
x
with respect to the rescaled metric
g:=x^{2}\widetilde{g}
. Note that the function on the right hand side is well-defined up to the conformal boundary by definition of aAdS spaces. If the rescaled metrics are smooth then
\theta
always exist.

c)

Define
x^{\prime}:=\exp(\theta)x
. Take a local chart
(y^{0},\dots,y^{n-1})
in the neighbourhood of
p
in
\mathfrak{I}
and extend it away from the conformal boundary by taking the functions constant on the integral curves of
\grad_{g}x^{\prime}
.

Then
(x^{\prime},y^{0},\dots,y^{n-1})
is a FG gauge. Note that not all local boundary defining functions of
\mathfrak{I}
can become the first coordinate of a FG gauge, only those verifying

(x^{2}\widetilde{g})^{-1}(dx,dx)=1

(3.4)

in a neighbourhood of a point
p\in\mathfrak{I}
in
\mathcal{M}
.

•

Let
(x,y^{0},\dots,y^{n-1})
be a FG gauge on an open set
\mathcal{U}
. Then for all
\mathfrak{S}\in\mathfrak{I}
such that
\mathfrak{S}\cap\mathcal{U}\neq\varnothing
,

\underline{\mathfrak{h}}=\mathfrak{h}\quad\text{on }\mathfrak{S}\cap\mathcal{U}\,,

where
\mathfrak{h}
is the Lorentzian metric on
\mathfrak{S}
associated to
x
by
3.1
. ∎

Definition 3.4
.

A
transformation preserving the FG gauge
is a diffeomorphism
\chi
on an open set
\mathcal{U}
such that there exists two FG gauges
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
on
\mathcal{U}
verifying
\chi=\phi_{2}\circ(\phi_{1})^{-1}
.

Remark
.

Let
\phi_{1}=(x,y^{0},\dots,y^{n-1})
and
\phi_{2}=(x^{\prime},z^{0},\dots,z^{n-1})
be two FG gauges on an open set
\mathcal{U}
. Then the map
\psi:(y^{0},\dots,y^{n-1})\in\Pi\circ\phi_{1}(\mathfrak{I}\cap\mathcal{U})\mapsto(z^{0},\dots,z^{n-1})\in\Pi\circ\phi_{2}(\mathfrak{I}\cap\mathcal{U})
is a diffeomorphism, where
\Pi:(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\mapsto y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
∎

The relation between the families of Lorentzian metrics
\underline{\mathfrak{h}}
of two FG gauges is given by the following lemma.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
\phi_{1}=(x,y^{0},\dots,y^{n-1})
and
\phi_{2}=(x^{\prime},z^{0},\dots,z^{n-1})
be two FG gauges defined on a common open set
\mathcal{U}
. Define
\Theta:=x^{\prime}/x\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{U},\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\star})
thanks to
2.12
. Then

\displaystyle\left(\underline{\mathfrak{h}}^{\prime}_{kl}\circ\phi_{2}\circ(\phi_{1})^{-1}\right)\,\frac{\partial z^{k}}{\partial x}\frac{\partial z^{l}}{\partial x}

\displaystyle=\left(1-\left(1+\frac{x}{\Theta}\frac{\partial\Theta}{\partial x}\right)^{2}\right)\Theta^{2}\,,

(3.5a)

\displaystyle\left(\underline{\mathfrak{h}}^{\prime}_{kl}\circ\phi_{2}\circ(\phi_{1})^{-1}\right)\,\frac{\partial z^{k}}{\partial x}\frac{\partial z^{l}}{\partial y^{i}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{x}{\Theta}\frac{\partial\Theta}{\partial y^{i}}\left(1+\frac{x}{\Theta}\frac{\partial\Theta}{\partial x}\right)\Theta^{2}\,,

(3.5b)

\displaystyle\left(\underline{\mathfrak{h}}^{\prime}_{kl}\circ\phi_{2}\circ(\phi_{1})^{-1}\right)\,\frac{\partial z^{k}}{\partial y^{i}}\frac{\partial z^{l}}{\partial y^{j}}

\displaystyle=\left(\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{ij}-\frac{x^{2}}{\Theta^{2}}\frac{\partial\Theta}{\partial y^{i}}\frac{\partial\Theta}{\partial y^{j}}\right)\Theta^{2}\,.

(3.5c)

Proof.

One has

\frac{\partial x^{\prime}}{\partial x}=\Theta\left(1+\frac{x}{\Theta}\frac{\partial\Theta}{\partial x}\right)\,,\qquad\frac{\partial x^{\prime}}{\partial y^{i}}=x\frac{\partial\Theta}{\partial y^{i}}\,.

Equations (
3.5
) are found by comparing (
3.3
) in the two FG gauges.
∎

Corollary 3.6
.

There exists an open neighbourhood
\mathcal{V}
of
\mathfrak{I}\cap\mathcal{U}
in
\mathcal{U}
such that the functions

1+\frac{x}{\Theta}\frac{\partial\Theta}{\partial x}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\det\left(\frac{\partial z}{\partial y}\right)

nowhere vanish on
\mathcal{V}
. Furthermore, the following differential equations hold on
\mathcal{V}

\displaystyle\frac{\partial z^{k}}{\partial x}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\frac{x}{\Theta}}{\sqrt{1-\frac{x^{2}}{\Theta^{2}}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}^{pq}\frac{\partial\Theta}{\partial y^{p}}\frac{\partial\Theta}{\partial y^{q}}}}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}^{ij}\frac{\partial\Theta}{\partial y^{j}}\frac{\partial z^{k}}{\partial y^{i}}\,,

(3.6a)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\Theta}{\partial x}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Theta}{x}\left(\sqrt{1-\frac{x^{2}}{\Theta^{2}}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}^{ij}\frac{\partial\Theta}{\partial y^{i}}\frac{\partial\Theta}{\partial y^{j}}}-1\right)\,.

(3.6b)

Remark
.

If
\Theta
is independent of
x
then (
3.6b
) implies that the restriction of
\Theta
on the level set
\{x=c>0\}
is an eikonal function for
\underline{\mathfrak{h}}(c)
. By continuity, this also holds for
c=0
, that is on
\mathfrak{I}\cap\mathcal{U}
.
∎

Proof.

The two functions nowhere vanish on
\mathfrak{I}\cap\mathcal{U}
thus the existence of
\mathcal{V}
is given by continuity. By dimension counting, there exists a map
(\lambda,\mu^{0},\dots,\mu^{n-1}):\mathcal{V}\to\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\setminus\{0\}
such that

\lambda u+\mu^{i}v_{i}=0

(3.7)

where

z:=(z^{0},\dots,z^{n-1})\,,\;\;u:=\frac{\partial z}{\partial x}\,,\;\;v_{i}:=\frac{\partial z}{\partial y^{i}}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{V},\mathbb{R}^{n})\,.

Taking
\lambda
(
3.5a
)
+\mu^{i}
(
3.5b
), one obtains

\frac{x}{\Theta}\left(1+\frac{x}{\Theta}\frac{\partial\Theta}{\partial x}\right)\,\mu^{i}\frac{\partial\Theta}{\partial y^{i}}=\lambda\left(1-\left(1+\frac{x}{\Theta}\frac{\partial\Theta}{\partial x}\right)^{2}\right)\,.

(3.8)

In the same way,
\lambda
(
3.5b
)
+\mu^{j}
(
3.5c
) gives

\lambda\frac{x}{\Theta}\left(1+\frac{x}{\Theta}\frac{\partial\Theta}{\partial x}\right)\frac{\partial\Theta}{\partial y^{i}}=\mu^{j}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{ij}-\frac{x^{2}}{\Theta^{2}}\,\mu^{j}\frac{\partial\Theta}{\partial y^{i}}\frac{\partial\Theta}{\partial y^{j}}\,.

(3.9)

Injecting (
3.8
) in (
3.9
) gives

\left(1+\frac{x}{\Theta}\frac{\partial\Theta}{\partial x}\right)\mu^{i}=\lambda\,\frac{x}{\Theta}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}^{ij}\frac{\partial\Theta}{\partial y^{j}}\,.

It follows that
\lambda\neq 0
. By injecting the above formula for
\mu^{i}
and simplifying by
\lambda
, (
3.7
) and (
3.8
) rewrite as

\displaystyle\frac{\partial z^{k}}{\partial x}=-\frac{\frac{x}{\Theta}}{1+\frac{x}{\Theta}\frac{\partial\Theta}{\partial x}}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}^{ij}\frac{\partial\Theta}{\partial y^{j}}\frac{\partial z^{k}}{\partial y^{i}}\,,

\displaystyle\left(1+\frac{x}{\Theta}\frac{\partial\Theta}{\partial x}\right)^{2}=1-\frac{x^{2}}{\Theta^{2}}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}^{ij}\frac{\partial\Theta}{\partial y^{i}}\frac{\partial\Theta}{\partial y^{j}}\,.

Hence the result.
∎

Recall that conformal rescalings of the conformal boundary are linked to changes of the boundary defining function, see equation (
3.1
). A remarkable fact is that they are also linked to transformations preserving the FG gauge as stated in the next lemma, even though the boundary defining functions of FG gauges verify (
3.4
).

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
\phi_{1}=(x,y^{0},\dots,y^{n-1})
and
\phi_{2}=(x^{\prime},z^{0},\dots,z^{n-1})
be two FG gauges on an open set
\mathcal{U}
. Define
\Theta:=x^{\prime}/x\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{U})
and
\psi:(y^{0},\dots,y^{n-1})\mapsto(z^{0},\dots,z^{n-1})
the diffeomorphism on
\mathfrak{I}\cap\mathcal{U}
. Then, for all
\mathfrak{S}\in\mathfrak{I}
such that
\mathfrak{S}\cap\mathcal{U}\neq\varnothing
, the diffeomorphism
\psi
induces a confomorphism on
\mathfrak{S}\cap\mathcal{U}
with

\psi^{\star}\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}=\Theta^{2}\mathfrak{h}\quad\text{on }\mathfrak{S}\cap\mathcal{U}\,,

(3.10)

where
\mathfrak{h}
and
\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}
are the metrics on
\mathfrak{S}
associated to
x
and
x^{\prime}
respectively. Reciprocally, all local confomorphisms of
\mathfrak{S}\in\mathfrak{I}
can be induced this way.

Remarks
.

•

The reciprocal can for instance be found expressed in infinitesimal form in
[
60
]
.

•

The particular case
\Theta=1
is equivalent to a diffeomorphism on
\mathfrak{I}
since then
x^{\prime}=x
and the functions
z^{k}
are independent of
x
by (
3.6a
). ∎

Proof.

By evaluating (
3.5c
) on
\mathfrak{S}\cap\mathcal{U}
, one obtains (
3.10
) straightforwardly.

Let us turn to the reciprocal. Let
p\in\mathfrak{S}\in\mathfrak{I}
be a point on the conformal boundary,
(x,y^{0},\dots,y^{n-1})
be a FG gauge on an open neighbourhood
\mathcal{U}
of
p
in
\mathcal{M}
and
\Omega\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{S}\cap\mathcal{U},\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\star})
be a local conformal factor. One can construct another FG gauge
(x\Theta,z^{0},\dots,z^{n-1})
on a neighbourhood of
p
in
\mathcal{U}
as in the proof of the existence of FG gauges, with initial data on the boundary
\omega:=\ln\Omega
and a chart
(z^{0},\dots,z^{n-1})
of
\mathfrak{I}
around
p
.
∎

3.2.2
Fefferman-Graham expansions

Let us now detail the Fefferman and Graham expansions derived in the celebrated work
[
31
]
. A study of these expansions at a finite order can be found in
[
81
]
.

Theorem 3.8

(Fefferman and Graham
[
31
, Theorem 2.3]
and
[
32
, Theorem 4.5]
)
.

Assume that the aAdS space
(\mathcal{M},\mathfrak{I},\widetilde{g})
is such that

i)

the rescaled metrics
g
are of class
\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathcal{M})\cap\mathcal{C}^{m}_{\text{phg}}(\mathcal{M}\,|\,\mathfrak{I},E)
with
m\geq n
and some index set
E
containing
(0,0)
and
(n,1)
,

ii)

(\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathfrak{I},\widetilde{g})
is a solution to the (
VE
) with
\Lambda=-n(n-1)/2
.

Let
(x,y^{0},\dots,y^{n-1})
be a FG gauge on an open set
\mathcal{U}
,
p
be a point in
\mathfrak{I}\cap\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{K}
be a compact neighbourhood of
p
in
\mathcal{U}
. Then, according to the parity of
n
, the following asymptotic expansion in
x
– called the
Fefferman-Graham expansion
(FG expansion) – holds on
\mathcal{K}
:

•

if
n
is odd,

\underline{\mathfrak{h}}(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor m/2\rfloor}\mathfrak{h}^{(2k)}\,x^{2k}+\sum_{k=(n-1)/2}^{\lfloor(m-1)/2\rfloor}\mathfrak{h}^{(2k+1)}\,x^{2k+1}+o(x^{m})

(3.11)

where
\mathfrak{h}^{(k)}
are smooth symmetric 2-tensor fields on
\mathfrak{I}
. Let us emphasise that the second sum starts at
k=(n-1)/2
. Furthermore, introducing

➤

\mathfrak{h}:=\mathfrak{h}^{(0)}
the induced metric of the rescaled metric
g:=x^{2}\widetilde{g}
on
\mathfrak{I}
,

➤

\mathfrak{t}:=\mathfrak{h}^{(n)}
,

one has that
\mathfrak{t}
is trace-free and divergence-free with respect to
\mathfrak{h}
and that there exists universal functions
\mathcal{F}_{n,k}
such that

\displaystyle\forall\;1\leq k\leq(n-1)/2,\quad\mathfrak{h}^{(2k)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{n,2k}(\mathfrak{h},\dots,\partial^{2k}\mathfrak{h})\,,

\displaystyle\forall\;n+1\leq k\leq m,\quad\mathfrak{h}^{(k)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{n,k}(\mathfrak{h},\dots,\partial^{k}\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{t},\dots,\partial^{k-n}\mathfrak{t})\,.

•

if
n
is even,

\underline{\mathfrak{h}}(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor m/2\rfloor}\mathfrak{h}^{(2k)}\,x^{2k}+\sum_{k=n/2}^{\lfloor m/2\rfloor}\mathfrak{h}^{(\star 2k)}\,x^{2k}\log x+o(x^{m})

(3.12)

where
\mathfrak{h}^{(k)}
and
\mathfrak{h}^{(\star 2k)}
are smooth symmetric 2-tensors on
\mathfrak{I}
. Let us emphasise that the second sum starts at
k=n/2
. Furthermore, introducing

➤

\mathfrak{h}:=\mathfrak{h}^{(0)}
the induced metric of the rescaled metric
g:=x^{2}\widetilde{g}
on
\mathfrak{I}
,

➤

\mathfrak{t}
the divergence-free and trace-free part of
\mathfrak{h}^{(n)}
with respect to
\mathfrak{h}
,

there exists universal functions
\mathcal{F}_{n,2k}
,
\mathcal{F}_{n,\star 2k}
,
\mathcal{F}_{n,n}^{\text{tr}}
and
\mathcal{F}_{n,n}^{\text{div}}
such that

\displaystyle\forall\;1\leq k\leq(n-2)/2,\quad\mathfrak{h}^{(2k)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{n,2k}(\mathfrak{h},\dots,\partial^{2k}\mathfrak{h})\,,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}^{(\star n)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{n,\star n}(\mathfrak{h},\dots,\partial^{n}\mathfrak{h})\,,

\displaystyle\trace_{\mathfrak{h}}\mathfrak{h}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{n,n}^{\text{tr}}(\mathfrak{h},\dots,\partial^{n}\mathfrak{h})\,,

\displaystyle\diver_{\mathfrak{h}}\mathfrak{h}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{n,n}^{\text{div}}(\mathfrak{h},\dots,\partial^{n+1}\mathfrak{h})\,,

\displaystyle\forall\;(n+2)/2\leq k\leq\lfloor m/2\rfloor,\quad\mathfrak{h}^{(\star 2k)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{n,\star 2k}(\mathfrak{h},\dots,\partial^{2k}\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{t},\dots,\partial^{2k-n}\mathfrak{t})\,,

\displaystyle\forall\;(n+2)/2\leq k\leq\lfloor m/2\rfloor,\quad\mathfrak{h}^{(2k)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{n,2k}(\mathfrak{h},\dots,\partial^{2k}\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{t},\dots,\partial^{2k-n}\mathfrak{t})\,.

Remarks
.

•

The pair
(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{t})
is called the
free data
on the boundary as its value is not imposed by the vacuum Einstein equations.

•

The tensor field
\mathfrak{h}^{(n)}
is called in the physical literature the
boundary stress-energy tensor
or holographic stress-energy tensor. For more details, see for example
[
28
,
82
]
. We will also refer to
\mathfrak{t}
as the boundary stress-energy tensor for
n
odd because it coincide with
\mathfrak{h}^{(n)}
in this case.

•

The first coefficients
\mathfrak{h}^{(2k)}
are given by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}^{(2)}_{ij}

\displaystyle=-\mathfrak{l}_{ij}

\displaystyle\qquad\text{if }n\geq 3\,,

(3.13a)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}^{(4)}_{ij}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4(n-4)}\mathfrak{b}_{ij}+\frac{1}{4}\mathfrak{l}_{ik}\mathfrak{l}^{k}{}_{j}

\displaystyle\qquad\text{if }n\geq 5\,,

(3.13b)

where
\mathfrak{l}_{ij}
and
\mathfrak{b}_{ij}
are the Schouten and Bach tensors of
\mathfrak{h}
.

•

When
n
is even, logarithmic terms may arise in the expansion (
3.12
). The first coefficient of the logarithmic terms denoted by
\mathfrak{h}^{(\star n)}
is proportional to the
n
th obstruction tensor, see
[
31
,
44
]
for more details on the obstruction tensors. If it vanishes then all the other coefficients
\mathfrak{h}^{(\star 2k)}
also vanish, leaving no logarithmic terms in the expansion. In particular, this is the case if
\mathfrak{h}
is locally conformally flat or conformally Einstein (note that it is always true for
n=2
by
2.37
).

•

The absence of a first order term in (
3.11
) and (
3.12
) implies that the boundary hypersurfaces
\mathfrak{S}\in\mathfrak{I}
are totally geodesic for the Levi-Civita connection
\nabla
of the rescaled metric
g:=x^{2}\widetilde{g}
, when
x
is a boundary defining function verifying
(x^{2}\widetilde{g})^{-1}(dx,dx)=1
in a neighbourhood of the conformal boundary. In view of
2.60
, this implies that all
\mathfrak{S}\in\mathfrak{I}
are umbilical in
(\mathcal{M},[g])
. ∎

The fact that the coefficients
\mathfrak{h}^{(k)}
and
\mathfrak{h}^{(\star k)}
are tensor fields on the conformal boundary is not immediate. As noted before, diffeomorphisms of the conformal boundary are associated to transformations preserving the FG gauge with
x^{\prime}=x
or equivalently
\Theta=1
. Yet, in this case the other coordinates
z^{k}
of the FG gauge
(x^{\prime},z^{0},\dots,z^{n-1})
are independent of
x
by (
3.6a
). Thus the comparison between the FG expansions associated to two such FG gauges is direct and gives the tensorial covariance of the coefficients. Another important consequence is that these tensors
\mathfrak{h}^{(k)}
and
\mathfrak{h}^{(\star k)}
do not depend on the full FG gauge
(x,y^{0},\dots,y^{n-1})
but only on the boundary defining function
x
.

The behaviours of
\mathfrak{h}^{(k)}
and
\mathfrak{h}^{(\star k)}
under transformations preserving the FG gauge but inducing non-trivial conformal rescalings on the conformal boundary still are to be derived. Comparing the FG expansions is not as easy as above since now the function
\Theta
depends a priori on
x
. It is nonetheless crucial to understand these behaviours, and especially these of the free data
(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{t})
, to obtain a gauge-independent meaning.

This was addressed in the physics literature under infinitesimal form
[
60
,
82
]
. For our purposes, it is sufficient to only look at the free data behaviour. That of
\mathfrak{h}
is already given by
3.7
. That of
\mathfrak{t}
for odd
n
is the object of the following lemma.

Lemma 3.9
.

Assume
n
is odd and the hypotheses of
3.8
hold. Let
\phi_{1}=(x,y^{0},\dots,y^{n-1})
and
\phi_{2}=(x^{\prime},z^{0},\dots,z^{n-1})
be two FG gauges on an open set
\mathcal{U}
. Pose
\Theta:=x^{\prime}/x\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{U},\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\star})
given by
2.12
and
\psi:=(y^{0},\dots,y^{n-1})\mapsto(z^{0},\dots,z^{n-1})
. For all
\mathfrak{S}\in\mathfrak{I}
such that
\mathfrak{S}\cap\mathcal{U}\neq\varnothing
,

\psi^{\star}\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}=\Theta^{2-n}\,\mathfrak{t}\quad\text{on }\mathfrak{S}\cap\mathcal{U}\,,

(3.14)

where
\mathfrak{t}
and
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
are the boundary stress-energy tensors associated to the two FG gauges by
3.8
.

Proof.

Take the open neighbourhood
\mathcal{V}
of
\mathfrak{I}\cap\mathcal{U}
in
\mathcal{U}
given by
3.6
. From the FG expansion (
3.11
), the functions
\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{ij}
verify locally

\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{ij}(x)=P_{ij}(x^{2})+\mathcal{O}\left({x^{n}}\right)\,,

for some suitable polynomials
P_{ij}
of degree at most
(n-1)/2
. Write locally

\Theta(x,.)=Q(x^{2})+\mathcal{O}\left({x^{s}}\right)\,,

where
s\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
is odd and maximal,
Q
is a polynomial of degree at most
(s-1)/2
with values into
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R})
. From the differential equation (
3.6b
) on
\Theta
, one deduces that
s\geq 2+\min(s,n)
and thus
s\geq n+2
. Using (
3.5c
) and the FG expansions (
3.11
) for the two FG gauges, the result follows by identifying the term of order
n
in
x
.
∎

For
n
odd, the previous lemma enables to extend the definition of the boundary stress-energy tensor for all boundary defining functions, not only those verifying (
3.4
).

Definition 3.10
.

Assume
n
is odd and the hypotheses of
3.8
hold. Let
x^{\prime}
be a boundary defining function of
\mathfrak{I}
. Define its boundary stress-energy tensor by

\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}:=\exp(\theta)^{2-n}\mathfrak{t}\,,

where
\theta
is a smooth function on a neighbourhood of
\mathfrak{I}
such that
x:=\exp(-\theta)x^{\prime}
is a boundary defining function of
\mathfrak{I}
verifying (
3.4
) on this neighbourhood and
\mathfrak{t}
is the boundary stress-energy tensor associated to
x
.

Remark
.

The existence of such a function
\theta
is ensured in a neighbourhood of the conformal boundary, see the proof of the existence of FG gauges. Although
\theta
is not unique, the definitions for two different functions
\theta
coincide by
3.9
.
∎

For
n
odd,
3.7
and
3.9
lead to the following definitions.

Definition 3.11
.

Let
n\geq 3
be an odd integer. Two triples
(\mathfrak{S},\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{t})
and
(\mathfrak{S}^{\prime},\mathfrak{h}^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
where

•

(\mathfrak{S},\mathfrak{h})
and
(\mathfrak{S}^{\prime},\mathfrak{h}^{\prime})
are pseudo-Riemannian manifolds of dimension
n
,

•

\mathfrak{t}
and
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
are trace-free and divergence-free symmetric 2-tensors on
(\mathfrak{S},\mathfrak{h})
and
(\mathfrak{S}^{\prime},\mathfrak{h}^{\prime})
respectively,

are said to be equivalent if there exists a confomorphism
\psi:(\mathfrak{S},\mathfrak{h})\to(\mathfrak{S}^{\prime},\mathfrak{h}^{\prime})
such that

\qquad\psi^{\star}\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}=\Omega^{2-n}\mathfrak{t}\,,

where
\Omega
is the conformal factor associated to
\psi
. The classes for this equivalence relation are denoted by
[(\mathfrak{S},\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{t})]
, or for simplicity
[(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{t})]
.

Definition 3.12
.

Assume
n
is odd. The free data class of a connected component
\mathfrak{S}
of
\mathfrak{I}
is the class
[(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{t})]
where
(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{t})
is the free data on
\mathfrak{S}
associated to any boundary defining function
x
of
\mathfrak{I}
.

3.2.3
Asymptotics of curvature tensors

Fix a FG gauge
(x,y^{0},\dots,y^{n-1})
on an open subset
\mathcal{U}
. Recall that the rescaled metric is in this case given by

g:=x^{2}\widetilde{g}=dx^{2}+\underline{\mathfrak{h}}(x)\,.

The goal of this section is to derive the asymptotics of the curvature tensors of
g
near the conformal boundary from the FG expansion presented in the previous section. Remind that since
g
extends to a
\mathcal{C}^{2}
metric on
\mathcal{M}
, its Weyl and Schouten tensors are continuous up to the conformal boundary
\mathfrak{I}
.

Proposition 3.13
.

Assume
n
is odd and the hypotheses of
3.8
hold. Then the Weyl and Schouten tensor of the rescaled metric
g
verify the following asymptotic developments in
x
on any compact neighbourhood of a point
p\in\mathfrak{I}\cap\mathcal{U}

\displaystyle L_{ij}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{l}_{ij}-\frac{n}{2}x^{n-2}\mathfrak{t}_{ij}+\mathcal{O}\left({x^{2}}\right)\,,

(3.15a)

\displaystyle L_{ix}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\left({x^{3}}\right)\,,

(3.15b)

\displaystyle L_{xx}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathfrak{l}_{i}{}^{i}}{n}+\mathcal{O}\left({x^{2}}\right)\,,

(3.15c)

\displaystyle W^{x}{}_{ixj}

\displaystyle=-\frac{n(n-2)}{2}x^{n-2}\mathfrak{t}_{ij}+\mathcal{O}\left({x^{2}}\right)\,,

(3.15d)

\displaystyle W^{x}{}_{ijk}

\displaystyle=x\mathfrak{c}_{jki}-nx^{n-1}\mathfrak{D}_{[j}\mathfrak{t}_{k]i}+\mathcal{O}\left({x^{3}}\right)\,,

(3.15e)

\displaystyle W^{i}{}_{jkl}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{w}^{i}{}_{jkl}+nx^{n-2}\left(\delta^{i}{}_{[k}\mathfrak{t}_{l]j}-\mathfrak{h}_{j[k}\mathfrak{t}_{l]i}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left({x^{2}}\right)\,,

(3.15f)

where
(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{t})
is the free data associated to the boundary defining function
x
and
\mathfrak{l}_{ij}
,
\mathfrak{c}_{ijk}
and
\mathfrak{w}^{i}{}_{jkl}
are respectively the Schouten, Cotton and Weyl tensors of
\mathfrak{h}
.

Remark
.

The coefficients of order
n-2
in (
3.15a
), (
3.15d
), (
3.15f
) and order
n-1
in (
3.15e
) are exact, even for odd dimensions
n\geq 5
for which these terms can be included in the
\mathcal{O}
.
∎

Proof.

Denote the Levi-Civita connection, Christoffel symbols, Riemman tensor, Ricci tensor and scalar curvature of
\underline{\mathfrak{h}}(x)
respectively by
\underline{\mathfrak{D}}
,
\underline{\mathfrak{G}}_{i}{}^{k}{}_{j}
,
\underline{\mathfrak{r}}^{i}{}_{jkl}
,
\underline{\mathfrak{r}}_{ij}
,
\underline{\mathfrak{r}}
. The Christoffel symbols of the rescaled metric
g
are given by

\displaystyle\Gamma_{x}{}^{x}{}_{x}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{x}{}^{x}{}_{i}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{x}{}^{i}{}_{x}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{i}{}^{x}{}_{j}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{ij}\,,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{i}{}^{j}{}_{x}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}^{jk}\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{ik}\,,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{i}{}^{k}{}_{j}

\displaystyle=\underline{\mathfrak{G}}_{i}{}^{k}{}_{j}\,.

Therefore the Riemann tensor of
g
is

\displaystyle R^{x}{}_{ixj}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}^{2}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{ij}+\frac{1}{4}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}^{kl}\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{ik}\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{jl}\,,

(3.16a)

\displaystyle R^{x}{}_{ijk}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\left(\underline{\mathfrak{D}}_{j}(\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{ki})-\underline{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}(\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{ji})\right)\,,

(3.16b)

\displaystyle R^{i}{}_{jkl}

\displaystyle=\underline{\mathfrak{r}}^{i}{}_{jkl}-\frac{1}{4}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}^{ip}\left(\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{pk}\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{jl}-\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{jk}\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{pl}\right)\,,

(3.16c)

where one defines

\underline{\mathfrak{D}}_{j}(\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{ki}):=\partial_{j}\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{ki}-\underline{\mathfrak{G}}_{j}{}^{l}{}_{k}\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{li}-\underline{\mathfrak{G}}_{j}{}^{l}{}_{i}\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{kl}\,.

It follows that

\displaystyle R_{xx}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}^{ij}\partial_{x}^{2}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{ij}+\frac{1}{4}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}^{ij}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}^{kl}\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{ik}\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{jl}\,,

(3.17a)

\displaystyle R_{xi}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\underline{\mathfrak{D}}^{j}\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{ji}-\underline{\mathfrak{D}}_{i}\left(\underline{\mathfrak{h}}^{jk}\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{jk}\right)\right)\,,

(3.17b)

\displaystyle R_{ij}

\displaystyle=\underline{\mathfrak{r}}_{ij}-\frac{1}{4}\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{ij}\left(\underline{\mathfrak{h}}^{kl}\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{kl}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}^{kl}\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{ik}\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{jl}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}^{2}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{ij}\,,

(3.17c)

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle=\underline{\mathfrak{r}}-\frac{1}{4}\left(\underline{\mathfrak{h}}^{kl}\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{kl}\right)^{2}+\frac{3}{4}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}^{ij}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}^{kl}\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{ik}\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{jl}-\underline{\mathfrak{h}}^{ij}\partial_{x}^{2}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{ij}\,.

(3.17d)

From (
3.17
) and the definition of the Schouten tensor (
2.5
), one deduces

\displaystyle L_{ij}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n-1}\Bigg(\underline{\mathfrak{r}}_{ij}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}^{2}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{ij}-\frac{1}{4}\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{ij}\left(\underline{\mathfrak{h}}^{kl}\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{kl}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}^{kl}\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{ik}\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{jl}

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{ij}}{2n}\left(\underline{\mathfrak{r}}-\underline{\mathfrak{h}}^{kl}\partial_{x}^{2}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{kl}-\frac{1}{4}\left(\underline{\mathfrak{h}}^{kl}\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{kl}\right)^{2}+\frac{3}{4}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}^{pq}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{kl}\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{pk}\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{ql}\right)\Bigg)\,,

(3.18a)

\displaystyle L_{ix}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2(n-1)}\left(\underline{\mathfrak{D}}^{j}\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{ji}-\underline{\mathfrak{D}}_{i}\left(\underline{\mathfrak{h}}^{jk}\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{jk}\right)\right)\,,

(3.18b)

\displaystyle L_{xx}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2n(n-1)}\Big(-\underline{\mathfrak{r}}+\frac{1}{4}\left(\underline{\mathfrak{h}}^{kl}\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{kl}\right)^{2}+\frac{2n-3}{4}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}^{ij}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}^{kl}\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{ik}\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{jl}

\displaystyle\quad-(2n-1)\underline{\mathfrak{h}}^{ij}\partial_{x}^{2}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{ij}\Big)\,.

(3.18c)

By combining the decomposition of the Riemann tensor (
2.6
) with equations (
3.16
) and (
3.18
), one has

\displaystyle W^{x}{}_{ixj}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n-1}\tf_{\underline{\mathfrak{h}}(x)}\Big(-\underline{\mathfrak{r}}_{ij}-\frac{n-2}{2}\partial_{x}^{2}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{ij}+\frac{(n-3)}{4}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}^{kl}\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{ik}\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{jl}+\frac{1}{4}\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{ij}\left(\underline{\mathfrak{h}}^{kl}\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{kl}\right)\Big)\,,

(3.19a)

\displaystyle W^{x}{}_{ijk}

\displaystyle=-\underline{\mathfrak{D}}_{[j}\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{k]i}+\frac{1}{n-1}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{i[j}\left(\underline{\mathfrak{D}}^{l}\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{k]l}-\underline{\mathfrak{D}}_{k]}\left(\underline{\mathfrak{h}}^{lp}\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{lp}\right)\right)\,,

(3.19b)

\displaystyle W^{i}{}_{jkl}

\displaystyle=\underline{\mathfrak{r}}^{i}{}_{jkl}-\frac{2}{n-1}\Big(\delta^{i}{}_{[k}\left(\underline{\mathfrak{r}}_{l]j}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}^{2}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{l]j}\right)-\underline{\mathfrak{h}}^{ip}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{j[k}\left(\underline{\mathfrak{r}}_{l]p}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}^{2}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{l]p}\right)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{n}\delta^{i}{}_{[k}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{l]j}\left(\underline{\mathfrak{r}}-\underline{\mathfrak{h}}^{pq}\partial_{x}^{2}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{pq}\right)\Big)+\mathcal{F}(\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}})\,,

(3.19c)

where
\tf_{\underline{\mathfrak{h}}(x)}
is the trace-free part with respect to
\underline{\mathfrak{h}}(x)
and
\mathcal{F}(\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}})
represents terms quadratic in
\partial_{x}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}
. By assumption, the FG expansion (
3.11
) holds. Using (
3.13
), one has

\displaystyle\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{ij}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{h}^{(0)}_{ij}+\mathfrak{h}^{(2)}_{ij}x^{2}+\mathfrak{h}^{(n)}_{ij}x^{n}+\mathcal{O}\left({x^{4}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{h}_{ij}-\mathfrak{l}_{ij}x^{2}+\mathfrak{t}_{ij}x^{n}+\mathcal{O}\left({x^{4}}\right)\,,

and

\displaystyle\underline{\mathfrak{h}}^{ij}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{h}^{(0)ij}-\mathfrak{h}^{(2)ij}x^{2}-\mathfrak{h}^{(n)ij}x^{n}+\mathcal{O}\left({x^{4}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{h}^{ij}+\mathfrak{l}^{ij}x^{2}-\mathfrak{t}^{ij}x^{n}+\mathcal{O}\left({x^{4}}\right)\,,

where the indices are raised with the metric
\mathfrak{h}
. Plugging these expansions in (
3.18
) gives

\displaystyle L_{ij}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n-1}\left(\mathfrak{r}_{ij}+\mathfrak{l}_{ij}-\frac{n(n-1)}{2}\mathfrak{t}_{ij}x^{n-2}-\frac{\mathfrak{r}+2\mathfrak{l}_{k}{}^{k}}{2n}\mathfrak{h}_{ij}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left({x^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{l}_{ij}-\frac{n}{2}\mathfrak{t}_{ij}x^{n-2}+\mathcal{O}\left({x^{2}}\right)\,,

\displaystyle L_{ix}

\displaystyle=-\frac{x}{n-1}\mathfrak{c}_{ji}{}^{j}+\frac{nx^{n-1}}{2(n-1)}\left(\mathfrak{D}^{j}\mathfrak{t}_{ij}-\mathfrak{D}_{i}(\mathfrak{h}^{kl}\mathfrak{t}_{kl})\right)+\mathcal{O}\left({x^{3}}\right)=\mathcal{O}\left({x^{3}}\right)\,,

\displaystyle L_{xx}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2n(n-1)}\left(-\mathfrak{r}+4(n-1)\mathfrak{l}_{i}{}^{i}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left({x^{2}}\right)=\frac{\mathfrak{l}_{i}{}^{i}}{n}+\mathcal{O}\left({x^{2}}\right).

In the same way with (
3.19
), one finds

\displaystyle W^{x}{}_{ixj}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n-1}\tf_{\mathfrak{h}}\left(-\mathfrak{r}_{ij}+(n-2)\mathfrak{l}_{ij}-\frac{(n-2)n(n-1)}{2}\mathfrak{t}_{ij}x^{n-2}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left({x^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{n(n-2)}{2}x^{n-2}\mathfrak{t}_{ij}+\mathcal{O}\left({x^{2}}\right)\,,

\displaystyle W^{x}{}_{ijk}

\displaystyle=x\mathfrak{c}_{jki}-nx^{n-1}\mathfrak{D}_{[j}\mathfrak{t}_{k]i}+\mathcal{O}\left({x^{3}}\right)\,,

\displaystyle W^{i}{}_{jkl}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{r}^{i}{}_{jkl}-2S_{j[k}{}^{ip}\mathfrak{l}_{l]p}+nx^{n-2}S_{j[k}{}^{ip}\mathfrak{t}_{l]p}+\mathcal{O}\left({x^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{w}^{i}{}_{jkl}+nx^{n-2}\left(\delta^{i}{}_{[k}\mathfrak{t}_{l]j}-\mathfrak{h}_{j[k}\mathfrak{t}_{l]i}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left({x^{2}}\right)\,.

Hence the results.
∎

One can generalise the results of
3.13
by considering any boundary defining function (and thus leaving the FG gauge) up to the cost of restricting to the leading term of the expansions. This is particularly interesting for the Weyl components which is the object of the next corollary.

Corollary 3.14
.

Assume
n
is odd and the hypotheses of
3.8
hold. Let
x
be a local boundary defining function of the conformal boundary
\mathfrak{I}
on an open set
\mathcal{U}
and
(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{t})
be its associated free data. Then

\displaystyle\left.\left(\lim_{x\to 0^{+}}W\right)\right|_{T^{\star}\mathfrak{I}\times(T\mathfrak{I})^{3}}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{w}\,,

(3.21a)

\displaystyle\left.\left(\lim_{x\to 0^{+}}x^{-1}W(dx,.,.,.)\right)\right|_{(T\mathfrak{I})^{3}}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{c}\,,

(3.21b)

where
W
is the Weyl tensor of the rescaled metric
g:=x^{2}\widetilde{g}
,
\mathfrak{w}
and
\mathfrak{c}
are the Weyl and Cotton tensors of
\mathfrak{h}
. Furthermore, if
n=3
then one also has

-\frac{2}{3}\left.\left(\lim_{x\to 0^{+}}x^{-1}W(dx,.,\grad_{g}x,.)\right)\right|_{(T\mathfrak{I})^{2}}=\mathfrak{t}\,,

(3.22)

where
\grad_{g}
is the gradient with respect to the rescaled metric
g
.

Remarks
.

•

In fact, the proofs of the limits (
3.21
) hold for all dimensions
n\geq 2
.

•

For
n\geq 5
,
\mathfrak{t}
is screened by lower order terms in (
3.15d
) and thus cannot be retrieved as in (
3.22
).

•

Equation (
3.22
) is key to interpret geometrically certain boundary conditions for the local existence of aAdS spaces. It will be used in
Section

4.3.3
. On the other hand, equations (
3.21
) are somehow already encoded in the extended conformal Einstein equations, see
Section

4.1.5
. ∎

Proof.

Let
(x^{\prime},y^{0},\dots,y^{n-1})
be a FG gauge on
\mathcal{U}
and pose
\Theta:=x/x^{\prime}
. One has
\Theta\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{U},\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\star})
by
2.12
. Since the Weyl tensor is conformally invariant, the Weyl tensors
W
and
W^{\prime}
of the rescaled metrics
g:=x^{2}\widetilde{g}
and
g^{\prime}:=(x^{\prime})^{2}\widetilde{g}
coincide, and so do the Weyl tensors
\mathfrak{w}
and
\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}
of
\mathfrak{h}
and
\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}
. Using (
3.15f
), one deduces

\left.\left(\lim_{x\to 0^{+}}W\right)\right|_{T^{\star}\mathfrak{I}\times(T\mathfrak{I})^{3}}=\left.\left(\lim_{x^{\prime}\to 0^{+}}W^{\prime}\right)\right|_{T^{\star}\mathfrak{I}\times(T\mathfrak{I})^{3}}=\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{w}\,.

Moreover, since
dx=x^{\prime}d\Theta+\Theta dx^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle x^{-1}W(dx,.,.,.)

\displaystyle=(x^{\prime})^{-1}W^{\prime}(dx^{\prime},.,.,.)+\Theta^{-1}W^{\prime}(d\Theta,.,.,.)

\displaystyle=\left(1+\frac{x^{\prime}}{\Theta}\frac{\partial\Theta}{\partial x^{\prime}}\right)(x^{\prime})^{-1}W^{\prime}(dx^{\prime},.,.,.)+\frac{1}{\Theta}\frac{\partial\Theta}{\partial y^{i}}W^{\prime}(dy^{i},.,.,.)\,.

With (
3.15e
) and (
3.15f
), one deduces

\left.\left(\lim_{x\to 0^{+}}x^{-1}W(dx,.,.,.)\right)\right|_{(T\mathfrak{I})^{3}}=\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}+\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}(d\ln\Omega,.,.,.)\,,

where
\Omega:=\Theta|_{\mathfrak{I}\cap\mathcal{U}}
. The transformation law of the Cotton tensor (
2.28b
) gives in particular

\mathfrak{c}=\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}+\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}(d\ln\Omega,.,.,.)\,,

since the Levi-Civita connections of
\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{h}
are the Weyl connections associated to the covector fields
0
and
d\ln\Omega
with respect to
\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}
. Hence the second limit.

The limit (
3.22
) when
n=3
is derived analogously. First, remark that

\grad_{g}x=\Theta^{-2}\grad_{g^{\prime}}x=\Theta^{-1}\grad_{g^{\prime}}x^{\prime}+x^{\prime}\Theta^{-2}\grad_{g^{\prime}}\Theta\,.

Thus

\displaystyle x^{-1}W(dx,.,\grad_{g}x,.)

\displaystyle=\left(1+\frac{x^{\prime}}{\Theta}\frac{\partial\Theta}{\partial x^{\prime}}\right)\Theta^{-1}(x^{\prime})^{-1}W^{\prime}(dx^{\prime},.,\grad_{g^{\prime}}x^{\prime},.)

\displaystyle\quad+\left(1+\frac{x^{\prime}}{\Theta}\frac{\partial\Theta}{\partial x^{\prime}}\right)\Theta^{-2}W^{\prime}(dx^{\prime},.,\grad_{g^{\prime}}\Theta,.)

\displaystyle\quad+\Theta^{-2}\frac{\partial\Theta}{\partial y^{i}}W^{\prime}(dy^{i},.,\grad_{g^{\prime}}x^{\prime},.)

\displaystyle\quad+\Theta^{-3}\frac{\partial\Theta}{\partial y^{i}}x^{\prime}W^{\prime}(dy^{i},.,\grad_{g^{\prime}}\Theta,.)\,.

Thanks to the symmetries of the Weyl tensor and the asymptotic developments (
3.15d
)-(
3.15f
), one deduces

\left.\left(\lim_{x\to 0^{+}}x^{-1}W(dx,.,\grad_{g}x,.)\right)\right|_{(T\mathfrak{I})^{2}}=-\frac{3}{2}\Omega^{-1}\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}+0+0+0\,.

Yet
\mathfrak{t}:=\Omega^{-1}\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
by
3.10
with
n=3
. Hence the result.
∎

3.3
Examples of aAdS spaces

In this section, we illustrate the previous definitions by looking at known aAds spaces and describe their free data class on their conformal boundary. More particularly, apart from the pure AdS space, a few aAdS black holes and aAdS spaces arising from simple free data classes are presented.

This illustrative endeavour is especially helpful to gain certain insight on potential boundary stress-energy tensor and to provide non-trivial spaces satisfying our boundary conditions (
1.1
).

3.3.1
Anti-de Sitter

Let us start, naturally, with the AdS space.

Definition 3.15
.

The
Anti-de Sitter (AdS) space
of dimension
n+1
and radius
l>0
is the smooth manifold without boundary
\mathcal{M}_{AdS}:=\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
equipped with the Lorentzian metric given in the spherical coordinate system
(t,r,\phi)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\star}\times\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
by

\widetilde{g}_{AdS}(l):=-f(r)dt^{2}+f(r)^{-1}dr^{2}+r^{2}d\sigma_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}^{2}\,,

where

f(r):=1+\frac{r^{2}}{l^{2}}\,.

Remarks
.

•

(\mathcal{M}_{AdS},\widetilde{g}_{AdS}(l))
is solution to the (VE) with
\Lambda=-n(n-1)/(2l^{2})
.

•

One has
