\widetilde{g}_{AdS}(l)=l^{2}\widetilde{g}_{AdS}(1)
by the change of variables
(t,r)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\star}\mapsto(t/l,r/l)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\star}
. In what follows, it will be assumed that
l=1
. ∎

With the change of variables
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\star}\mapsto\arctan r\in(0,\pi/2)
, of inverse
\psi\in(0,\pi/2)\mapsto\tan\psi\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\star}
, one obtains

\widetilde{g}_{AdS}=\frac{1}{\cos^{2}\psi}\left(-dt^{2}+d\sigma_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}^{2}\right)

(3.23)

where
\psi
is the polar angle in the hyperspherical coordinates on the sphere
\mathbb{S}^{n}
, the north pole being identified to the point
r=0
. Thus, the AdS space is, up to a confomorphism, the open hemisphere
\{\psi<\pi/2\}
of the Einstein cylinder
(\mathcal{M}_{EC},g_{EC})
defined by (
2.32
).

This confomorphism highlights the existence of a timelike conformal boundary, namely the equator
\{\psi=\pi/2\}\simeq\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
of the Einstein cylinder, since the conformal factor
\Omega=\cos\psi
of the confomorphism extends naturally by zero on it. In order to attach the conformal boundary, one extends the above coordinate system on the equator. Introducing the function
1-\cos\psi
as a radius, this defines a smooth manifold with boundary
\mathcal{N}_{AdS}\simeq\mathbb{R}\times\overline{B}\vphantom{B}^{n}
, where
\overline{B}\vphantom{B}^{n}
is the closed unit ball of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. It follows that
(\mathcal{N}_{AdS},\partial\mathcal{N}_{AdS},\widetilde{g}_{AdS})
is an aAdS space in the sense of
3.1
. The conformal class
[\mathfrak{h}]
on the
n
-dimensional conformal boundary
\partial\mathcal{N}_{AdS}\simeq\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
is
[g_{EC}]
.

One can easily construct a FG gauge for the AdS space. Define

x(r):=\exp\left(-\int_{0}^{r}f(s)^{-1/2}ds\right)=\exp(-\sinh^{-1}(r))=\frac{1}{r+\sqrt{f(r)}}\,.

The map
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\star}\mapsto x(r)\in(0,1)
is a change of variables of inverse
x\in(0,1)\mapsto(1-x^{2})/(2x)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\star}
. Furthermore,
x
is a boundary defining function of the conformal boundary since

x=\frac{\cos\psi}{1+\sin\psi}\,.

It follows that

x^{2}\widetilde{g}_{AdS}=dx^{2}-\frac{(1+x^{2})^{2}}{4}dt^{2}+\frac{(1-x^{2})^{2}}{4}d\sigma_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}^{2}\,.

Thus the chart
(x,t,\phi)
is a FG gauge of associated free data
\mathfrak{h}=g_{EC}/4
and
\mathfrak{t}=0
. Consequently, the free data class of AdS is given by

[(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{t})]=[(g_{EC},0)]\,.

(3.24)

3.3.2
Schwarzschild-Anti-de Sitter

Let us turn to aAdS black holes, the simplest of which is the Schwarzschild-Anti-de Sitter space. As we are interested in the free data on the conformal boundary, only the exterior region is presented.

Definition 3.16
.

The exterior of the
Schwarzschild-Anti-de Sitter space
of dimension
n+1\geq 3
, radius
l>0
and mass
m>0
is the smooth manifold without boundary
\mathcal{M}_{SAdS}:=\mathbb{R}\times(r_{\star},+\infty)\times\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
equipped with the Lorentzian metric given in the spherical coordinate system
(t,r,\phi)\in\mathbb{R}\times(r_{\star},+\infty)\times\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
by

\widetilde{g}_{SAdS}(l,m):=-f_{m}(r)dt^{2}+f_{m}(r)^{-1}dr^{2}+r^{2}d\sigma_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}^{2}\,,

where

f_{m}(r):=1+\frac{r^{2}}{l^{2}}-\frac{2m}{r^{n-2}}

and
r_{\star}=r_{\star}(n,l,m)>0
is the only real positive root of the polynomial
X^{n}+l^{2}X^{n-2}-2ml^{2}
(assuming that
m>1/2
if
n=2
).

Remarks
.

•

(\mathcal{M}_{SAdS},\widetilde{g}_{SAdS}(l,m))
is solution to the (VE) with
\Lambda=-n(n-1)/(2l^{2})
.

•

One has
\widetilde{g}_{SAdS}(l,m)=l^{2}\widetilde{g}_{SAdS}(1,m/l^{n-2})
by the change of variables
(t,r)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\star}\mapsto(t/l,r/l)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\star}
. In what follows, it will be assumed that
l=1
. ∎

Contrary to AdS, no natural confomorphism arises. Let us imitate the construction of a FG gauge for AdS conducted above by introducing the new variable

x_{m}(r):=\exp\left(-\int_{r_{\star}}^{r}f_{m}(s)^{-1/2}ds\right)\,.

This is a well defined decreasing diffeomorphism from
(r_{\star},+\infty)
to
(0,1)
.

Example
.

One have explicit formulae when

•

n=2

x_{m}(r)=\exp\left(-\left[\ln\left(\sqrt{f_{m}(r)}+r\right)\right]_{r_{\star}}^{r}\right)=\frac{r_{\star}}{\sqrt{f_{m}(r)}+r}\,,

of inverse function

r(x_{m})=r_{\star}\frac{1+x_{m}^{2}}{2x_{m}}\,,

•

n=4

x_{m}(r)=\exp\left(-\left[\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(2r\sqrt{f_{m}(r)}+2r^{2}+1\right)\right]_{r_{\star}}^{r}\right)=\sqrt{\frac{2r_{\star}^{2}+1}{2r\sqrt{f_{m}(r)}+2r^{2}+1}}\,,

of inverse function

r(x_{m})=\frac{2mx_{m}^{4}+\sqrt{(2mx_{m}^{4})^{2}+(2r_{\star}^{2}+1)x_{m}^{2}(2r_{\star}^{2}+1-x_{m}^{2})^{2}}}{2(2r_{\star}^{2}+1)x_{m}^{2}}\,.\qed

Since

\frac{x_{m}(r)}{x(r)}=\exp\left(-\int_{0}^{r_{\star}}f(s)^{-1/2}ds\right)\exp\left(-\int_{r_{\star}}^{r}f_{m}(s)^{-1/2}-f(s)^{-1/2}ds\right)\,,

and

0\leq f_{m}(s)^{-1/2}-f(s)^{-1/2}\;\underset{+\infty}{\sim}\;ms^{-(n+1)}\,,

one deduces that

\lim_{r\to+\infty}\frac{x_{m}(r)}{x(r)}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\star}\,.

Thus
x_{m}
and
x
have the same behaviour at infinity, which is not surprising since Schwarzschild-Anti-de Sitter can be seen as a fast decaying perturbation of AdS.

This leads us to extend
\mathcal{M}_{SAdS}\simeq\mathbb{R}\times(0,1)\times\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
into the smooth manifold with boundary
\mathcal{N}_{SAdS}:=\mathbb{R}\times[0,1)\times\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
whose differential structure at the boundary
\partial\mathcal{N}_{SAdS}\simeq\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
is imposed by requiring that
x_{m}
is a smooth function. Then
(\mathcal{N}_{SAdS},\partial\mathcal{N}_{SAdS},\widetilde{g}_{SAdS})
is an aAdS space in the sense of
3.1
. The conformal class on the boundary is the same as the one of AdS, that is
[\mathfrak{h}]=[g_{EC}]
. Furthermore, the global chart
(x_{m},t,\phi)
is a FG gauge.

Let us now determine the boundary stress-energy tensor
\mathfrak{t}
. Identifying it from the definition of the metric
\widetilde{g}_{SAdS}
in the FG gauge
(x_{m},t,\phi)
would require an expansion of
r
and
f_{m}(r)
in term of
x_{m}
. We will instead use the more convenient asymptotic expansion (
3.15d
) which holds for
n
odd. The relevant components of the Weyl tensor
\widetilde{W}
of
\widetilde{g}_{SAdS}
in the spherical coordinates
(t,r,\phi)
are given by

\displaystyle\widetilde{W}^{r}{}_{trt}

\displaystyle=\frac{-(n-1)(n-2)m}{r^{n}}f_{m}(r)\,,

\displaystyle\widetilde{W}^{r}{}_{trA}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\widetilde{W}^{r}{}_{ArB}

\displaystyle=\frac{-(n-2)m}{r^{n}}r^{2}(d\sigma_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}^{2})_{AB}\,,

where
A,B
correspond to the sphere indices. Note that the smooth function
1/r=2x/(1-x^{2})
is a boundary defining function of the conformal boundary. However it does not verify (
3.4
) and thus (
3.15d
) does not directly apply. The key point to overcome this difficulty is that the leading term in the above expressions for the Weyl tensor are of order
n-2
in
1/r
. Hence the term of order
n-2
must be the leading term in the expansion (
3.15d
) for all odd
n
. It follows that (
3.22
) holds for all odd
n
up to replacing the factor
2/3
by
2/(n(n-2))
. Therefore, the boundary stress-energy tensor associated to
1/r
for
n
odd is

\mathfrak{t}=\frac{2m}{n}\Big((n-1)dt^{2}+d\sigma_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}^{2}\Big)\,.

(3.25)

Consequently, for
n
odd, the free data class of the Schwarzschild-Anti-de Sitter space is given by

[(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{t})]=\left[\left(g_{EC},\frac{2m}{n}\Big((n-1)dt^{2}+d\sigma_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}^{2}\Big)\right)\right]\,.

(3.26)

Let us mention that the first
n
terms of the FG expansion are explicitly given by
[
81
, Equation (3.93)]
for all dimensions. In particular, it shows that (
3.25
) remains valid for all dimensions
n\geq 3
.

3.3.3
Birmingham-Anti-de Sitter

The Birmingham spaces
5
5

5

I would like to thanks P. Chruściel for pointing out the existence of these spaces to my advisor.
form a generalisation of the Schwarzschild spaces. They have been introduced by Birmingham in
[
10
]
, see also
[
24
, Section 5.5]
.

Definition 3.17
.

Let
(\mathcal{P},\not{h})
be an Einstein Riemannian manifold of dimension
n-1\geq 2
, whose (constant) scalar curvature is denoted by
\not{r}
. The exterior of the associated
Birmingham-Anti-de Sitter space
of dimension
n+1
, radius
l>0
and mass
m\geq 0
is the smooth manifold without boundary
\mathcal{M}_{BAdS}:=\mathbb{R}\times(r_{\star},+\infty)\times\mathcal{P}
equipped with the Lorentzian metric given by

\widetilde{g}_{BAdS}(l,m,\not{h}):=-f_{m,\not{h}}(r)dt^{2}+f_{m,\not{h}}(r)^{-1}dr^{2}+r^{2}\not{h}\,,

where

f_{m,\not{h}}(r):=\frac{\not{r}}{(n-2)(n-1)}+\frac{r^{2}}{l^{2}}-\frac{2m}{r^{n-2}}

and
r_{\star}
is the only real positive root of the polynomial

X^{n}+\frac{\not{r}l^{2}}{(n-2)(n-1)}X^{n-2}-2ml^{2}\,.

Remarks
.

•

(\mathcal{M}_{BAdS},\widetilde{g}_{BAdS}(l,m,\not{h}))
is solution to the (VE) with
\Lambda=-n(n-1)/(2l^{2})
.

•

For all
k>0
and
p,q\in\mathbb{R}
, one has

\widetilde{g}_{BAdS}(l,m,\not{h})=k^{2p}\widetilde{g}_{BAdS}(k^{-p}l,k^{2q-(p+q)(n-2)}m,k^{2q}\not{h})

with the change of variables
(t,r)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\star}\mapsto(t/k^{p+q},r/k^{p+q})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\star}
. In what follows, it will be assumed that
l=1
and
\not{r}=\text{sign}(\not{r})(n-2)(n-1)
. ∎

In a similar way to Schwarzschild-Anti-de Sitter, one defines a function
x_{m,\not{h}}
with values into
(0,1)
and extend the manifold into the smooth manifold with boundary
\mathcal{N}_{BAdS}:=\mathbb{R}\times[0,1)\times\mathcal{P}
. Then one obtains an aAdS space in the sense of
3.1
. Using the boundary defining function
1/r
such as for Schwarzschild-Anti-de Sitter, one finds

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}

\displaystyle=-dt^{2}+\not{h}\,,

(3.27a)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{2m}{n}\Big((n-1)dt^{2}+\not{h}\Big)\,.

(3.27b)

Consequently, for
n
odd, the free data class of Birmingham-Anti-de Sitter on its conformal boundary
\mathfrak{I}\simeq\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}
is given by

[(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{t})]=\left[\left(-dt^{2}+\not{h},\frac{2m}{n}\Big((n-1)dt^{2}+\not{h}\Big)\right)\right]\,.

(3.28)

3.3.4
Kerr-Anti-de Sitter

The Kerr black holes are a generalisation of the Schwarzschild black holes by allowing them to rotate. We will only consider the case
n=3
.

Definition 3.18
.

The exterior of the
Kerr-Anti-de Sitter space
of dimension
4
, radius
l>0
, mass
m>0
and angular momentum per mass
a\in\mathbb{R}
with
|a|<l
is the smooth manifold without boundary
\mathcal{M}_{KAdS}:=\mathbb{R}\times(r_{\star},+\infty)\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
equipped with the Lorentzian metric given in the Boyer-Lindquist chart
(t,r,\theta,\varphi)\in\mathbb{R}\times(r_{\star},+\infty)\times(0,\pi)\times(0,2\pi)
by

\displaystyle\widetilde{g}_{KAdS}(l,m,a)

\displaystyle:=\rho^{2}\left(\frac{dr^{2}}{\Delta_{r}}+\frac{d\theta^{2}}{\Delta_{\theta}}\right)+\frac{\Delta_{\theta}\sin^{2}\theta}{\rho^{2}\Xi^{2}}\left(adt-(r^{2}+a^{2})d\varphi\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\Delta_{r}}{\rho^{2}\Xi^{2}}\left(dt-a\sin^{2}\theta d\varphi\right)^{2}\,,

where

\displaystyle\Delta_{r}

\displaystyle:=(r^{2}+a^{2})\left(1+\frac{r^{2}}{l^{2}}\right)-2mr\,,

\displaystyle\qquad\Delta_{\theta}

\displaystyle:=1-\frac{a^{2}}{l^{2}}\cos^{2}\theta\,,

\displaystyle\rho^{2}

\displaystyle:=r^{2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta\,,

\displaystyle\qquad\Xi

\displaystyle:=1-\frac{a^{2}}{l^{2}}\,.

and
r_{\star}
is the greatest real positive root of the polynomial
\Delta_{r}
if it admits at least one or
0
otherwise.

Remarks
.

•

(\mathcal{M}_{KAdS},\widetilde{g}_{KAdS}(l,m,a))
is solution to the (VE) with
\Lambda=-3/l^{2}
.

•

One has
\widetilde{g}_{KAdS}(l,m,a)=l^{2}\widetilde{g}_{KAdS}(1,m/l,a/l)
by the change of variables
(t,r)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\star}\mapsto(t/l,r/l)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\star}
. In what follows, it will be assumed that
l=1
. ∎

With the benefit of hindsight, let us use
1/r
as a boundary defining function and attach to the manifold the cylinder at infinity
\mathfrak{I}\simeq\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
. The free data associated to
1/r
is given by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\Xi^{2}}\left(dt-a\sin^{2}\theta d\varphi\right)^{2}+\frac{d\theta^{2}}{\Delta_{\theta}}+\frac{\sin^{2}\theta\Delta_{\theta}}{\Xi^{2}}d\varphi^{2}\,,

(3.29a)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{2m}{3}\left(\frac{2}{\Xi^{2}}\left(dt-a\sin^{2}\theta d\varphi\right)^{2}+\frac{d\theta^{2}}{\Delta_{\theta}}+\frac{\sin^{2}\theta\Delta_{\theta}}{\Xi^{2}}d\varphi^{2}\right)\,.

(3.29b)

Equation (
3.29a
) follows straightforwardly from the expression of the metric
\widetilde{g}_{KAdS}
. Note that
\mathfrak{h}
is locally conformally flat but not Einstein. We used Mathematica to compute the Weyl tensor of Kerr-Anti-de Sitter and take the limit (
3.22
) in order to obtain (
3.29b
).

3.3.5
Real analytic free data

Another way to construct aAdS spaces is to determine cases for which the FG expansion given in
3.8
converges. The simplest case is when the free data
(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{t})
is real analytic. For
n
odd, this implies that the formal series converges and that the resulting metric is real analytic as well in a neighbourhood of the boundary. One can refer to
[
31
, Theorem 2.3]
in the case
\mathfrak{t}=0
and to
[
32
, Theorem 4.8 and the following remarks]
in the general case. For
n
even, the convergence of the formal series, which contains logarithmic terms, for real analytic free data can be derived from
[
64
]
.

Let us also mention the work of LeBrun
[
66
]
, previous to Fefferman and Graham
[
31
]
, in which the holomorphic case for
n=3
and with the additional assumption of self-duality is investigated. This last assumption amounts to set the boundary stress-energy tensor proportional to the Cotton-York tensor by a fixed imaginary number by adapting the discussion in
[
32
, Section 5]
to the Lorentzian case. Therefore, it can be seen as a special case of our family of boundary conditions (
1.1
) in a complex setting.

3.3.6
Conformally Einstein or locally conformally flat boundaries

Two remarkable cases, discussed for example in
[
32
, Section 7]
, give rise to a finite FG expansion. First, consider a smooth Lorentzian manifold
(\mathfrak{I},\mathfrak{h})
of dimension
n\geq 3
which is solution to the (VE) for a cosmological constant
\Lambda\in\mathbb{R}
. Define
\mathcal{M}:=[0,x_{\max})\times\mathfrak{I}
where

x_{\max}=\begin{cases}\sqrt{\frac{2(n-1)(n-2)}{\Lambda}}&\text{if }\Lambda>0\,,\\
\quad\quad\;+\infty&\text{otherwise}\,,\end{cases}

and equip the interior
\mathring{\mathcal{M}}=(0,x_{\max})\times\mathfrak{I}
with the smooth Lorentzian metric

\widetilde{g}:=\frac{dx^{2}+\underline{\mathfrak{h}}(x)}{x^{2}}\quad\text{where}\quad\underline{\mathfrak{h}}(x)=\left(1-\frac{\Lambda x^{2}}{2(n-1)(n-2)}\right)^{2}\mathfrak{h}\,.

By construction,
(\mathcal{M},\mathfrak{I},\widetilde{g})
is an aAdS space. Its free data class is
[(\mathfrak{h},0)]
.

Secondly, consider a smooth Lorentzian conformal structure
(\mathfrak{I},[\mathfrak{h}])
of dimension
n\geq 3
which is locally conformally flat. Fix a representative
\mathfrak{h}
of the conformal class whose Schouten tensor is denoted by
\mathfrak{l}_{ij}
. Define the following Lorentzian metric

\widetilde{g}:=\frac{dx^{2}+\underline{\mathfrak{h}}(x)}{x^{2}}\quad\text{where}\quad\underline{\mathfrak{h}}\vphantom{h}_{ij}(x)=\mathfrak{h}_{ij}-\mathfrak{l}_{ij}x^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\mathfrak{l}_{i}{}^{k}\mathfrak{l}_{kj}x^{4}\,,

on a neighbourhood
\mathcal{M}
of
\{0\}\times\mathfrak{I}
in
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathfrak{I}
. Then
(\mathcal{M},\mathfrak{I},\widetilde{g})
is an aAdS space and its free data class is
[(\mathfrak{h},0)]
. It was shown in
[
83
]
that, in this case,
\widetilde{g}
is locally conformally flat as well.

Remark
.

Since
\widetilde{g}
is locally conformally flat, the Weyl tensor of
\widetilde{g}
(and thus of any rescaled metric
g
) vanishes identically. This implies that if one introduces a non-zero boundary stress-energy tensor
\mathfrak{t}
, the leading term of the asymptotic expansion (
3.15d
) of
W^{x}{}_{ixj}
is only due to
\mathfrak{t}
. Hence, (
3.22
) holds for all odd dimensions
n\geq 3
if the factor
2/3
is replaced by
2/(n(n-2))
. This apply to the Schwarzschild-Anti-de Sitter and Birmingham-Anti-de Sitter spaces, already presented in
Section

3.3.2
and
Section

3.3.3
.
∎

3.3.7
Conformally pp-wave boundaries

We recall here results obtained by Anderson, Leistner and Nurowski in
[
67
,
1
]
. Consider a smooth Lorentzian conformal structure
(\mathfrak{I},[\mathfrak{h}])
of dimension
n\geq 3
which contains a pp-wave metric, that is to say such that there exists a representative
\mathfrak{h}
and a smooth null vector field
\mathfrak{K}
on
\mathfrak{I}
such that

\mathfrak{D}_{i}\mathfrak{K}_{j}=0\,.

Note that this is equivalent to say that
\mathfrak{K}
is a Killing field for
\mathfrak{h}
(and thus a conformal Killing field for the conformal structure) which is locally a gradient. The pp-wave representative
\mathfrak{h}
can be written in a local coordinate system
(u,y,z^{1},\dots,z^{n-2})
under the form

\mathfrak{h}=2\mathfrak{f}(u,z)du^{2}+du\otimes dy+dy\otimes du+dz^{2}\,,

(3.30)

where
\mathfrak{f}
is a smooth function of
u
and
z
. With these coordinates, one has
\mathfrak{K}:=\partial_{y}
. The inverse metric is given by

\mathfrak{h}^{-1}=\partial_{u}\otimes\partial_{y}+\partial_{y}\otimes\partial_{u}-2\mathfrak{f}(u,z)\partial_{y}\otimes\partial_{y}+\partial_{z}\otimes\partial_{z}\,.

By
[
67
, Section 3]
, the only non-vanishing components of the Ricci, Cotton, Weyl and Bach tensors of
\mathfrak{h}
are given by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{r}_{uu}

\displaystyle=-\Delta_{z}\mathfrak{f}\,,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}_{Auu}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\partial_{z^{A}}\Delta_{z}\mathfrak{f}}{n-2}\,,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{w}_{AuBu}

\displaystyle=\partial_{z^{A}}\partial_{z^{B}}\mathfrak{f}-\frac{\Delta_{z}\mathfrak{f}}{n-2}\delta_{AB}\,,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{b}_{uu}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\Delta^{2}_{z}\mathfrak{f}}{n-2}\,.

where
\Delta_{z}
is the Laplacian in the
z=(z^{1},\dots,z^{n-2})
coordinate and the indices
A
,
B
ranges from
1
to
n-2
.

In this setting, the FG expansions simplify greatly. For
n
odd, the formal series (
3.11
) reduces to

\underline{\mathfrak{h}}(x):=2\mathfrak{F}(u,z,x)du^{2}+du\otimes dy+dy\otimes du+dz^{2}\,,

(3.31)

where

\mathfrak{F}(u,z,x):=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\Delta_{z}^{2k}\mathfrak{f}}{k!\,2^{k}\prod_{j=1}^{k}(n-2j)}x^{2k}+\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{k}\Delta_{z}^{2k}\mathfrak{a}}{k!2^{k}\prod_{j=1}^{k}(n+2j)}x^{n+2k}

(3.32)

with
\mathfrak{a}
a smooth function of
u
and
z
, see
[
1
, Theorem 3.1]
. This function
\mathfrak{a}
determines entirely the boundary stress-energy tensor through

\mathfrak{t}=2\mathfrak{a}\,du^{2}\,,

(3.33)

or in covariant form

\mathfrak{t}=2\mathfrak{a}\;\mathfrak{K}\otimes\mathfrak{K}\,.

(3.34)

In particular, if
\mathfrak{f}
and
\mathfrak{a}
are real analytic functions then the formal series (
3.32
) converges and the resulting function
\mathfrak{F}
is real analytic as well. This is a special case of
Section

3.3.5
. One also retrieves subcases of
Section

3.3.6
for special choices of
\mathfrak{f}
. For example,
\mathfrak{h}
Einstein is equivalent to
\Delta_{z}\mathfrak{f}=0
and
\mathfrak{h}
locally conformally flat is equivalent for
n=3
to
\partial_{z}^{3}\mathfrak{f}=0
.

Outside the real analytic class, one can also construct various aAdS spaces with a pp-wave conformal boundary. For instance, consider the functions
\mathfrak{f}
of the form
\mathfrak{f}(u,z)=\mathfrak{P}(z)\mathfrak{e}(u)
with
\mathfrak{P}
a polynomial function and
\mathfrak{e}
a smooth function. Then, taking
\mathfrak{P}
of order 3 and
\mathfrak{a}=0
yields an aAds space with a conformally Bach-flat but not locally conformally flat boundary. Imposing
\mathfrak{a}(u,z)=\mu^{\prime}(\partial_{z}^{3}\mathfrak{P})(z)\mathfrak{e}(u)
with
\mu^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
yields aAdS spaces verifying our boundary conditions (
1.1
).

3.4
Boundary stress-energy tensors

This section focuses on boundary stress-energy tensors. First, a few potential boundary stress-energy tensor classes are inferred in
Section

3.4.1
from the examples of aAdS spaces presented in
Section

3.3
. This could represent a first step towards treating the geometric
inhomogeneous Robin
boundary conditions. In particular, some of these potential boundary stress-energy tensors arise from conformal Killing fields of the boundary. The interactions between boundary stress-energy tensors and conformal Killing fields are then highlighted in the general case in
Section

3.4.2
.

3.4.1
Examples of boundary stress-energy tensor classes

Let
(\mathfrak{S},[\mathfrak{h}])
be a smooth Lorentzian conformal structure of dimension
n\geq 3
. The goal of this section is to construct boundary stress-energy tensor candidates for the conformal structure
(\mathfrak{S},[\mathfrak{h}])
, that is to say to construct trace-free and divergence-free symmetric 2-tensor fields which are conformally invariant of weight
n-2
(in contravariant form). Let us begin with our family of boundary conditions (
1.1
).

Lemma 3.19
.

Assume that
n=3
and
(\mathfrak{S},[\mathfrak{h}])
is oriented. Then for all
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
, one can construct a boundary stress-energy tensor class by defining for any representative
\mathfrak{h}\in[\mathfrak{h}]

\mathfrak{t}_{ij}:=\mu\,\mathfrak{y}_{ij}\,,

(3.35)

where
\mathfrak{y}_{ij}
is the Cotton-York tensor of
\mathfrak{h}
.

Remark
.

If
\mu
is not constant then one has to require that

\mathfrak{y}_{ij}\mathfrak{D}^{j}\mu=0

for (
3.35
) to define a suitable boundary stress-energy tensor candidate. This is a non-trivial condition on the Cotton-York tensor, and thus on the conformal class
[\mathfrak{h}]
. In particular, we expect that the Cauchy problem with the geometric boundary condition (
3.35
) for a non-constant
\mu
is ill-posed, since it would both impose the boundary stress-energy tensor and restrict the conformal class. This is coherent with the fact that (
3.35
) with a non-constant
\mu
cannot be propagated on the conformal boundary as in
Section

4.3.5
.
∎

Proof.

This is a suitable choice thanks to
2.49
.
∎

The two following classes give rise to fluid-like and null-dust-like boundary stress-energy tensors.

Lemma 3.20
.

Let
\mathfrak{X}
be a smooth vector field on
\mathfrak{S}
. If the equation

\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{X}}\mathfrak{X}+\frac{1}{2}\grad_{\mathfrak{h}}\mathfrak{h}(\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{X})=\frac{2\diver\mathfrak{X}}{n}\mathfrak{X}

(3.36)

holds for a representative
\mathfrak{h}\in[\mathfrak{h}]
then it holds for any representative. Furthermore, if equation (
3.36
) is verified and
\mathfrak{X}
is nowhere null then one can construct a boundary stress-energy tensor class by defining for any representative
\mathfrak{h}\in[\mathfrak{h}]

\mathfrak{t}^{ij}:=\frac{\pm 1}{|\mathfrak{h}(\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{X})|^{n/2+1}}\left(\mathfrak{X}^{i}\mathfrak{X}^{j}-\frac{\mathfrak{h}(\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{X})}{n}\mathfrak{h}^{ij}\right)\,.

(3.37)

Remarks
.

•

Equation (
3.36
) implies

\mathfrak{X}\cdot\mathfrak{h}(\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{X})=\frac{2\diver\mathfrak{X}}{n}\mathfrak{h}(\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{X})\,.

(3.38)

•

In particular, all conformal Killing fields of
(\mathfrak{S},[\mathfrak{h}])
verify (
3.36
).

•

Since
\mathfrak{X}
is nowhere null, a representative
\mathfrak{h}_{ij}
and its associated boundary stress-energy tensor
\mathfrak{t}_{ij}
defined by (
3.37
) are co-orthogonalisable in the sense of symmetric bilinear forms.

•

The boundary stress-energy tensors of Schwarzschild-Anti-de Sitter (
3.25
), Bir-

mingham-Anti-de Sitter (
3.27b
) and Kerr-Anti-de Sitter (
3.29b
) are of the form (
3.37
) with
\mathfrak{X}=\sqrt{\frac{2m(n-1)}{n}}\partial_{t}
. ∎

Proof.

Equation (
3.36
) can be rewritten as

Z_{i}{}^{jk}{}_{l}\mathfrak{X}^{i}\mathfrak{D}_{k}\mathfrak{X}^{l}=0\,,

where
Z_{i}{}^{jk}{}_{l}
is defined by (
2.29
). The conformal invariance of the equation is thus similar to that of the conformal Killing equation, see
Section

2.4.6
.

By definition, the tensor field
\mathfrak{t}
is symmetric, trace-free and is conformally invariant of weight
n-2
. Let us compute its divergence using (
3.36
) and (
3.38
)

\displaystyle\pm|\mathfrak{h}(\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{X})|^{n/2+1}\mathfrak{D}_{i}\mathfrak{t}^{ij}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{X}}\mathfrak{X}^{j}+\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{D}^{j}\mathfrak{h}(\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{X})+\left(-\frac{n+2}{2}\frac{\mathfrak{X}\cdot\mathfrak{h}(\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{X})}{\mathfrak{h}(\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{X})}+\diver\mathfrak{X}\right)\mathfrak{X}^{j}

\displaystyle=-\frac{n+2}{2}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{X}\cdot\mathfrak{h}(\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{X})}{\mathfrak{h}(\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{X})}-\frac{\diver\mathfrak{X}}{n}\right)\mathfrak{X}^{j}

\displaystyle=0\,.\qed

Lemma 3.21
.

Let
(\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{a})
be a pair constituted of a smooth null vector field
\mathfrak{X}
and a smooth scalar function
\mathfrak{a}
on
\mathfrak{S}
verifying

\mathfrak{a}\,\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{X}}\mathfrak{X}+\diver(\mathfrak{a}\mathfrak{X})\mathfrak{X}=0\,,

(3.39)

for the Levi-Civita connection
\mathfrak{D}
of a representative
\mathfrak{h}\in[h]
. Equation (
3.39
) is verified for all representatives of the conformal class
[\mathfrak{h}]
if one assumes that
\mathfrak{X}
and
\mathfrak{a}
are conformally invariant of weight
p
and
q\in\mathbb{R}
with
2p+q=n+2
. Furthermore, one can construct a boundary stress-energy tensor class by

\mathfrak{t}^{ij}=\mathfrak{a}\,\mathfrak{X}^{i}\mathfrak{X}^{j}\,.

(3.40)

Remark
.

The boundary stress-energy tensor of a conformally pp-wave boundary (
3.34
) is of the form (
3.40
) with the pair
(\mathfrak{K},2\mathfrak{a})
.
∎

Finally, note that the boundary stress-energy tensor classes above can be added together to create other classes if the hypotheses permit it.

3.4.2
Conformal Killing fields on the boundary

Let
(\mathcal{M},\mathfrak{I},\widetilde{g})
be an aAdS space. Recall that from the FG expansion, one deduces that all
\mathfrak{S}\in\mathfrak{I}
are umbilical in the conformal structure
(\mathcal{M},[g])
, where
[g]
is the conformal class of rescaled metrics. This implies that the tangent part on
\mathfrak{S}\in\mathfrak{I}
of conformal Killing fields of
(\mathcal{M},[g])
are conformal Killing fields of the induced conformal structure
(\mathfrak{S},[\mathfrak{h}])
. This fact leads to the following known result.

Lemma 3.22
.

Let
K
be a Killing field of
(\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathfrak{I},\widetilde{g})
. If
K
extends smoothly on
\mathfrak{I}
then it is tangent to
\mathfrak{I}
and its restriction on
\mathfrak{S}\in\mathfrak{I}
is a conformal Killing field of
(\mathfrak{S},[\mathfrak{h}])
.

Proof.

Let
K
be a Killing field of
(\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathfrak{I},\widetilde{g})
extending smoothly on
\mathfrak{I}
. Then for any boundary defining function
x
of
\mathfrak{I}
, one has

x\mathcal{L}_{K}g=2(K\cdot x)g\,,

where
g:=x^{2}\widetilde{g}
is the rescaled metric associated to
x
. Hence
K\cdot x=0
on
\mathfrak{I}
and thus
K
is tangent to the conformal boundary. Moreover,
K
is a conformal Killing field of
(\mathcal{M},[g])
by continuity. It follows from the above discussion that its restriction on
\mathfrak{S}\in\mathfrak{I}
is a conformal Killing field of
(\mathfrak{S},[\mathfrak{h}])
.
∎

Thus symmetries in the bulk imply symmetries on the conformal boundary. The converse was studied for stationary Killing fields by Anderson
[
2
]
and for holographic Killing fields of a domain of the conformal boundary by Holzegel and Shao
[
53
]
.

Now that we know how to derive conformal Killing fields on the conformal boundary, let us remind that they lead to conserved currents on the boundary by association with the boundary stress-energy tensor. Indeed, if
\mathfrak{K}
is a conformal Killing field of
(\mathfrak{S},[\mathfrak{h}])
then for any representative
(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{t})
of the free data class, the covector field

\mathfrak{J}_{i}:=\mathfrak{t}_{ij}\mathfrak{K}^{j}

is divergence-free for the Levi-Civita connection
\mathfrak{D}
of
\mathfrak{h}
. Note that this covector field is conformally invariant of weight
n-2
.

4
Local existence and uniqueness of aAdS spaces in dimension 4

This section contains the precise statement of our main theorem,
4.53
, and its proof. For this, we rely on the framework established by Friedrich in
[
38
]
. Recall that a local existence and uniqueness theory for the vacuum Einstein equations (
VE
) with a negative cosmological constant
\Lambda<0
requires to consider an initial boundary value problem due to the presence of a timelike conformal boundary for generic solutions. Applying the standard method for solving the (
VE
) by means of generalised wave coordinates would, in the context of aAdS spaces, lead to two main difficulties:

a)

The Ricci tensor is not regular up to the conformal boundary
\mathscr{I}
. Thus one has to deal with a system of partial differential equations with singular coefficients for the metric components.

b)

No information is a priori known on the localisation of the conformal boundary. Consequently, this is a free boundary problem.

As established in
[
38
]
, Friedrich’s strategy to overcome these two problems is as follows:

1.

Recast the (
VE
) into an enlarged tensorial system which is regular up to the conformal boundary and includes a boundary defining function of the conformal boundary.

2.

Add some gauge invariance to the previous system by considering Weyl connections instead of only the Levi-Civita connection. This enables more freedom in the gauge choice. The resulting system is called the extended conformal vacuum Einstein equations, see (
4.1
).

3.

Choose a gauge adapted to the conformal boundary for which the tensorial system reduces to a symmetric hyperbolic system of partial differential equations and for which the conformal boundary is straightened out.

The only limitations of this method are that it constructs Einstein manifolds with a regular conformal extension and that it requires the spacetime dimension to be equal to 4. The former is not restrictive given our definition of aAdS spaces, see
3.1
. However, as a consequence of the latter, we will always assume that
n=3
in this section.

In
Section

4.1
, the general framework of the extended conformal vacuum Einstein equations is introduced. A particular emphasis is put on describing the gauge invariances as they determine whether an object is analytic (gauge-dependent) or geometric (gauge-independent). The presentation includes a noticeable extension of the Friedrich scalar definition to all Weyl connections, see
Section

4.1.2
. Let us also point out that the relevance of the (CVE) in the context of 4-dimensional aAdS spaces verifying the vacuum Einstein equations is detailed in
Section

4.1.7
.

The core of the analytic part of the proof is contained in
Section

4.2
. In particular, the gauge is constructed in
Section

4.2.2
and the evolution problem is addressed in
Section

4.2.4
. The analytic initial boundary value problem is then solved in
Section

4.2.4
using maximal dissipative boundary conditions for symmetric hyperbolic systems, see
4.32
, and we prove the propagation of the constraints in
Section

4.2.5
.

Section

4.3
is dedicated to the geometric interpretation of a subset of the analytic boundary conditions. The first step, conducted in
Section

4.3.1
, consists in rewriting them as tensorial boundary conditions on a 2-dimensional distribution. Thanks to the asymptotic expansions of the curvature tensors based on the FG expansion found in
Section

3.2.3
, the analytic boundary conditions with constant coefficients are expressed in terms of the boundary stress-energy tensor and the Cotton-York tensor of the conformal boundary in
Section

4.3.3
. Most of the reflective analytic boundary conditions are then interpreted as the geometric Dirichlet boundary conditions in
Section

4.3.4
or as the geometric homogeneous Robin boundary conditions in
Section

4.3.5
. This last step relies on auxiliary evolution systems on the conformal boundary. In the case of the Dirichlet boundary conditions, this was done by Friedrich in
[
38
]
while the treatment of the homogeneous Robin boundary conditions is new, see
4.45
.

The geometric initial data problem is discussed in
Section

4.4
. Its precise formulation is given in
Section

4.4.2
. An adapted version of the conformal method established by Andersson and Chruściel
[
3
,
4
]
allows for a partial resolution of the geometric initial data problem in certain cases. This method is outlined in
Section

4.4.3
. Finally,
Section

4.4.4
focuses on the smoothness of the unphysical fields, a requirement of the geometric initial data problem. In the case of initial data constructed by the conformal method and under certain hypotheses, Kánnár derived necessary and sufficient condition for this requirement to be satisfied in
[
62
]
. We extend this result by deriving the necessary and sufficient conditions in all generality, see
4.51
and its
4.52
.

The last section,
Section

4.5
, is comprised of the precise statement of the main theorem,
4.53
, as well as further remarks.

4.1
The extended conformal vacuum Einstein equations

This section introduces the general framework of the extended Conformal Vacuum Einstein equations (CVE). We start by stating the equations and its gauge invariances in
Section

4.1.1
. We then introduce the extended Friedrich scalar in
Section

4.1.2
. It generalises the Friedrich scalar, used by Friedrich in
[
38
]
, to all Weyl connections.

In
Section

4.1.3
, we derive the constraints implied by the (CVE) on any non-null hypersurface. These are then rewritten into an intrinsic framework in
Section

4.1.4
. In particular, the constraints greatly simplify on a particular subset of a solution to the (CVE), called the conformal boundary. As its name suggests, this subset is closely related to the conformal boundary of Einstein spacetimes. More generally, the explicit relations between the (VE) and the (CVE) are given in
Section

4.1.6
, as well as the equivalent relations at the level of the constraints. The specific case of 4-dimensional aAdS spaces satisfying the (VE) is further discussed in
Section

4.1.7
, justifying the use of the (CVE) to solve our problem.

4.1.1
The equations and gauge invariances

Definition 4.1
.

Denote by
\mathscr{E}
the set of all quintuples
(\mathcal{M},g,V,\Theta,\kappa)
where

•

(\mathcal{M},g)
is a 4-dimensional smooth Lorentzian manifold with corners (see
Section

2.2
),

•

V
is a Weyl candidate on
\mathcal{M}
(see
2.46
),

•

\Theta
is a smooth function on
\mathcal{M}
,

•

\kappa
is a smooth covector field on
\mathcal{M}
.

Definition 4.2
.

A quintuple
(\mathcal{M},g,V,\Theta,\kappa)\in\mathscr{E}
is said to be a solution to the
extended Conformal Vacuum Einstein equations (CVE)
if the following tensorial equations are satisfied on
\mathcal{M}

\displaystyle\widehat{\nabla}_{\alpha}V^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\kappa_{\alpha}V^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\nu}\,,

(CVE.1)

\displaystyle\widehat{R}^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\Theta V^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\nu}+2S_{\beta[\mu}{}^{\alpha\xi}\widehat{L}_{\nu]\xi}\,,

(CVE.2)

\displaystyle 2\widehat{\nabla}_{[\alpha}\widehat{L}_{\beta]\gamma}

\displaystyle=(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{\mu}V^{\mu}{}_{\gamma\alpha\beta}\,,

(CVE.3)

\displaystyle\Theta^{2}\widehat{L}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=-\Theta\widehat{\nabla}_{\alpha}(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{\beta}+(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{\beta}(d\Theta)_{\alpha}-\frac{1}{2}S_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\mu\nu}(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{\mu}(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{\nu}+\frac{\Lambda}{6}g_{\alpha\beta}\,.

(CVE.4)

where

•

\widehat{\nabla}
is the Weyl connection on the conformal structure
(\mathcal{M},[g])
associated to
\kappa
with respect to
g
given by
2.24
,

•

\widehat{R}^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\nu}
and
\widehat{L}_{\mu\nu}
are the Riemann and Schouten tensors of
\widehat{\nabla}
(see
2.25
and
2.27
),

•

S_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\mu\nu}
is the tensor field defined by (
2.7
),

•

\widehat{\zeta}_{g}
is the smooth covector field on
\mathcal{M}
defined by

(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{\alpha}:=\Theta\,\kappa_{\alpha}+(d\Theta)_{\alpha}\,,

(4.2)

•

\Lambda\in\mathbb{R}
is the cosmological constant.

Remark
.

By definition of the Cotton tensor (
2.8
) and the decomposition of the Riemann tensor (
2.22
) of a Weyl connection, equations (
CVE.2
) and (
CVE.3
) mean that the Cotton and Weyl tensors of
\widehat{\nabla}
are given by

\widehat{W}^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\nu}=\Theta V^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\nu}\,,\qquad\qquad\widehat{C}_{\alpha\beta\gamma}=(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{\mu}V^{\mu}{}_{\gamma\alpha\beta}\,.\qed

To describe the gauge invariances of the (CVE), let us introduce the following transformations on quintuples.

Definition 4.3
.

Let
(\mathcal{M},g,V,\Theta,\kappa)\in\mathscr{E}
. Define

•

for any confomorphism
\phi:(\mathcal{M},g)\to(\overline{\mathcal{M}},\overline{g})
(see
2.20
),

\Conf_{\phi}\left[(\mathcal{M},g,V,\Theta,\kappa)\right]:=\left(\overline{\mathcal{M}},\overline{g},\phi_{\star}(\Omega^{-1}V),\phi_{\star}(\Omega\Theta),\phi_{\star}(\kappa-d\ln\Omega)\right)\in\mathscr{E}\,,

where
\Omega\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M},\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\star})
is the conformal factor of
\phi
, that is
\phi^{\star}\overline{g}=\Omega^{2}g
,

•

for any smooth covector field
\omega
on
\mathcal{M}
,

\Weylchg_{\omega}\left[(\mathcal{M},g,V,\Theta,\kappa)\right]:=\left(\mathcal{M},g,V,\Theta,\kappa+\omega\right)\in\mathscr{E}\,,

•

the sign change operator by

\Signchg\left[(\mathcal{M},g,V,\Theta,\kappa)\right]:=\left(\mathcal{M},g,-V,-\Theta,\kappa\right)\in\mathscr{E}\,.

Remark
.

Under the above transformations, the covector field
\widehat{\zeta}_{g}
transforms as follows

\widehat{\zeta}_{g}\overset{\Conf_{\phi}}{\longmapsto}\phi_{\star}(\Omega\widehat{\zeta}_{g})\,,\qquad\widehat{\zeta}_{g}\overset{\Weylchg_{\omega}}{\longmapsto}\widehat{\zeta}_{g}+\Theta\omega\,,\qquad\widehat{\zeta}_{g}\overset{\Signchg}{\longmapsto}-\widehat{\zeta}_{g}\,.\qed

(4.3)

Proposition 4.4
.

The (
4.1
) enjoy the following gauge invariances

i)

invariance under confomorphisms
: for any quintuple
(\mathcal{M},g,V,\Theta,\kappa)\in\mathscr{E}
and any confomorphism
\phi
of
(\mathcal{M},g)
,
(\mathcal{M},g,V,\Theta,\kappa)
is a solution if and only if
\Conf_{\phi}\big[(\mathcal{M},g,V,\Theta,\kappa)\big]
is a solution,

ii)

invariance under Weyl connection changes
: for any quintuple
(\mathcal{M},g,V,\Theta,\kappa)\in\mathscr{E}
and any smooth covector field
\omega
on
\mathcal{M}
,
(\mathcal{M},g,V,\Theta,\kappa)
is a solution if and only if
\Weylchg_{\omega}\left[(\mathcal{M},g,V,\Theta,\kappa)\right]
is a solution,

iii)

invariance under sign change
: a quintuple
(\mathcal{M},g,V,\Theta,\kappa)\in\mathscr{E}
is a solution if and only if
\Signchg[(\mathcal{M},g,V,\allowbreak\Theta,\kappa)]
is a solution.

Remark
.

The local statement of invariance under confomorphisms includes, in particular, invariance under changes of coordinates which expresses the tensorial nature of the equations.
∎

Proof.

The invariance of the (CVE) under confomorphisms and under sign change is trivial. The invariance under Weyl connection changes can be proven using the transformation rules of the Weyl, Schouten and Cotton tensors given by
2.32
.
∎

Definition 4.5
.

Two quintuples in
\mathscr{E}
are said to be
gauge-equivalent
if they are related by a composition of confomorphisms
\Conf_{\phi}
, Weyl connection changes
\Weylchg_{\omega}
and sign changes
\Signchg
.

Remark
.

From the properties

\displaystyle S^{2}

\displaystyle=\text{Id}\,,

\displaystyle\qquad\quad T_{\omega_{1}}T_{\omega_{2}}

\displaystyle=T_{\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}}\,,

\displaystyle\qquad\quad C_{\phi_{1}}C_{\phi_{2}}

\displaystyle=C_{\phi_{1}\circ\phi_{2}}\,,

\displaystyle[S,T_{\omega}]

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\qquad[S,C_{\phi}]

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\qquad C_{\phi}T_{\omega}

\displaystyle=T_{\phi_{\star}\omega}C_{\phi}\,,

one deduces that if
(\mathcal{M},g,V,\Theta,\kappa)
,
\left(\overline{\mathcal{M}},\overline{g},\overline{V},\overline{\Theta},\overline{\kappa}\right)\in\mathscr{E}
are two gauge-equivalent quintuples then there exists a confomorphism
\phi:(\mathcal{M},g)\to\left(\overline{\mathcal{M}},\overline{g}\right)
, a smooth covector field
\omega
on
\mathcal{M}
and
k\in\{0,1\}
such that

\left(\overline{\mathcal{M}},\overline{g},\overline{V},\overline{\Theta},\overline{\kappa}\right)=C_{\phi}T_{\omega}S^{k}\left[(\mathcal{M},g,V,\Theta,\kappa)\right]\,.\qed

4.1.2
The extended Friedrich scalar

In this section, we introduce a new quantity, called the extended Friedrich scalar, which extends the original Friedrich scalar to all Weyl connections. This scalar field enables us to split equation (
CVE.4
) into two equations that behave better where
\Theta
vanishes, see
4.8
below. This modification enables us to obtain a propagation system for the constraints free of singularities contrary to Friedrich’s work. The (extended) Friedrich scalar is also linked to the properties of the conformal boundary as we will see in
Section

4.1.5
.

Definition 4.6
.

The
extended Friedrich scalar
of a quintuple
(\mathcal{M},g,V,\Theta,\kappa)\in\mathscr{E}
is defined by

\widehat{s}_{g}:=\frac{g^{\alpha\beta}}{4}\left(\widehat{\nabla}_{\alpha}(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{\beta}+\kappa_{\alpha}(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{\beta}+\Theta\widehat{L}_{\alpha\beta}\right)\,,

(4.4)

where
\widehat{\zeta}_{g}
is the covector field given by (
4.2
) and
\widehat{\nabla}
is the Weyl connection associated to
\kappa
with respect to
g
.

Remark
.

When
\kappa=0
, that is for the Levi-Civita connection
\nabla
of
g
, the extended Friedrich scalar reduces to the Friedrich scalar given by

s_{g}=\frac{1}{4}\left(\Box_{g}\Theta+L\Theta\right)\,,

where
L:=L_{\alpha}{}^{\alpha}
is the trace of the Schouten tensor of
\nabla
. Note that
\Box_{g}+L
is not the linear scalar conformal wave operator which writes
\Box_{g}-L
in dimension
4
.
∎

Example
.

On the Einstein cylinder
(\mathcal{M}_{EC},g_{EC})
defined by (
2.32
) and with
\Theta=\cos\psi
, one has

s_{g_{EC}}=\frac{1}{4}\left(\Box_{g_{EC}}\cos\psi+\cos\psi\right)=\frac{1}{4}\left(-3\cos\psi+\cos\psi\right)=-\frac{\Theta}{2}\,.\qed

Proposition 4.7
.

Under the transformations on quintuples defined in
4.3
, the extended Friedrich scalar transforms as follows

\widehat{s}_{g}\overset{\Conf_{\phi}}{\longmapsto}\phi_{\star}(\Omega^{-1}\widehat{s}_{g})\,,\qquad\widehat{s}_{g}\overset{\Weylchg_{\omega}}{\longmapsto}\widehat{s}_{g}+g^{-1}(\omega,\widehat{\zeta}_{g})+\frac{\Theta}{2}g^{-1}(\omega,\omega)\,,\qquad\widehat{s}_{g}\overset{\Signchg}{\longmapsto}-\widehat{s}_{g}\,.

(4.5)

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
(\mathcal{M},g,V,\Theta,\kappa)\in\mathscr{E}
. Equation (
CVE.4
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle\widehat{\nabla}_{\alpha}(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{\beta}+\kappa_{\alpha}(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{\beta}+\Theta\widehat{L}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\widehat{s}_{g}\,g_{\alpha\beta}\,,

(4.6a)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(g^{-1}(\widehat{\zeta}_{g},\widehat{\zeta}_{g})+\frac{\Lambda}{3}\right)

\displaystyle=\widehat{s}_{g}\Theta\,.

(4.6b)

Furthermore, if (
4.6a
) and (
4.6b
) hold then

(d\widehat{s}_{g})_{\alpha}-\kappa_{\alpha}\widehat{s}_{g}=-\widehat{L}_{\alpha\beta}g^{\beta\gamma}(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{\gamma}\,.

(4.7)

Proof.

By definition of
\widehat{\zeta}_{g}
, see equation (
4.2
), (
CVE.4
) rewrites as

\Theta\left(\widehat{\nabla}_{\alpha}(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{\beta}+\kappa_{\alpha}(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{\beta}+\Theta\widehat{L}_{\alpha\beta}\right)=\frac{g_{\alpha\beta}}{2}\left(g^{-1}(\widehat{\zeta}_{g},\widehat{\zeta}_{g})+\frac{\Lambda}{3}\right)\,.

Taking the trace with respect to the metric
g
gives (
4.6b
). Then

\Theta\left(\widehat{\nabla}_{\alpha}(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{\beta}+\kappa_{\alpha}(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{\beta}+\Theta\widehat{L}_{\alpha\beta}-\widehat{s}_{g}\,g_{\alpha\beta}\right)=0\,.

Thus, (
4.6a
) holds on
\{p\in\mathcal{M}\mid\Theta(p)\neq 0\}
, and by continuity on

\supp\Theta:=\overline{\{p\in\mathcal{M}\mid\Theta(p)\neq 0\}}\,.

If
p\notin\supp\Theta
then there exists an open neighbourhood
\mathcal{U}
of
p
on which
\Theta
vanishes identically. Then
\widehat{\zeta}_{g}
also vanishes on
\mathcal{U}
and (
4.6a
) becomes trivial. Hence, equation (
4.6a
) is verified everywhere on
\mathcal{M}
.

Furthermore, by taking the covariant derivative of (
4.6b
) and using (
4.6a
),

\displaystyle\Theta(d\widehat{s}_{g})_{\alpha}+\widehat{s}_{g}(d\Theta)_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\kappa_{\alpha}g^{-1}(\widehat{\zeta}_{g},\widehat{\zeta}_{g})+g^{-1}(\widehat{\nabla}_{\alpha}\widehat{\zeta}_{g},\widehat{\zeta}_{g})

\displaystyle=-\Theta\widehat{L}_{\alpha\beta}g^{\beta\gamma}(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{\gamma}+\widehat{s}_{g}(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{\alpha}\,.

Thus

\Theta\left((d\widehat{s}_{g})_{\alpha}-\kappa_{\alpha}\widehat{s}_{g}+\widehat{L}_{\alpha\beta}g^{\beta\gamma}(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{\gamma}\right)=0\,.

Consequently, (
4.7
) is verified on
\supp\Theta
. As above, one can show that it also holds on the complementary set. Hence the result.
∎

4.1.3
The constraint equations on non-null hypersurfaces

Let
(\mathcal{M},g,V,\Theta,\kappa)\in\mathscr{E}
be a solution to the (CVE) and
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathcal{M}
be a non-null hypersurface. The (CVE) induce some tensorial equations on
\mathcal{S}
called the constraint equations. To state them, introduce

•

e_{\perp}
a unit-normal vector field on
\mathcal{S}
with respect to
g
,

•

\varepsilon:=g(e_{\perp},e_{\perp})\in\{\pm 1\}
the
g
pseudo-norm of
e_{\perp}
,

•

h
the metric induced by
g
on
\mathcal{S}
,

•

\widehat{D}
the Weyl connection induced by
\widehat{\nabla}
on
\mathcal{S}
(see
Section

2.6.2
),

•

\widehat{K}_{g}
the extrinsic curvature of
\widehat{\nabla}
with respect to
g
on
\mathcal{S}
(see
Section

2.6.3
).

Proposition 4.9
.

The constraint equations induced by the (
4.1
) on the non-null hypersurface
\mathcal{S}
are

\displaystyle\widehat{D}_{i}M_{jk}{}^{i}

\displaystyle=2(\widehat{K}_{g})^{i}{}_{[j}E_{k]i}+2\kappa_{i}M_{jk}{}^{i}\,,

(4.8a)

\displaystyle\widehat{D}_{i}E^{i}{}_{j}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon(\widehat{K}_{g})^{ik}M_{jki}+3\kappa_{i}E^{i}{}_{j}\,,

(4.8b)

\displaystyle\widehat{L}_{ij}-\widehat{l}_{ij}

\displaystyle=\Theta E_{ij}-\varepsilon F_{ij}(\widehat{K}_{g})\,,

(4.8c)

\displaystyle 2\widehat{L}_{k\perp}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon h^{ij}G_{kij}(\widehat{K}_{g},\kappa)\,,

(4.8d)

\displaystyle\Theta M_{klj}

\displaystyle=G_{klj}(\widehat{K}_{g},\kappa)+h_{j[k}h^{ip}G_{l]ip}(\widehat{K}_{g},\kappa)\,,

(4.8e)

\displaystyle 2\widehat{D}_{[i}\widehat{L}_{j]k}

\displaystyle=2(\widehat{K}_{g})_{k[i}\widehat{L}_{j]\perp}+zM_{ijk}-2(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{l}S_{k[i}{}^{lm}E_{j]m}\,,

(4.8f)

\displaystyle 2\widehat{D}_{[i}\widehat{L}_{j]\perp}

\displaystyle=-2\varepsilon(\widehat{K}_{g})^{k}{}_{[i}\widehat{L}_{j]k}+2\kappa_{[i}\widehat{L}_{j]\perp}-\varepsilon M_{ij}{}^{k}(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{k}\,,

(4.8g)

\displaystyle\widehat{D}_{i}(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{j}+\kappa_{i}(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{j}

\displaystyle=z(\widehat{K}_{g})_{ij}-\Theta\widehat{L}_{ij}+\widehat{s}_{g}h_{ij}\,,

(4.8h)

\displaystyle(dz)_{i}

\displaystyle=-\varepsilon(\widehat{K}_{g})_{i}{}^{j}(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{j}-\Theta\widehat{L}_{i\perp}\,,

(4.8i)

\displaystyle(d\widehat{s}_{g})_{i}-\kappa_{i}\widehat{s}_{g}

\displaystyle=-\varepsilon z\widehat{L}_{i\perp}-\widehat{L}_{ij}h^{jk}(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{k}\,,

(4.8j)

\displaystyle\widehat{s}_{g}\Theta

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(h^{ij}(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{i}(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{j}+\varepsilon z^{2}+\frac{\Lambda}{3}\right)\,,

(4.8k)

where

•

(E_{ij},M_{ijk})
is the decomposition of the Weyl candidate
V
with respect to
\mathcal{S}
given by
2.62
(the indices of
E
and
M
are raised with the metric
h
),

•

z:=(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{\perp}
is seen as a smooth scalar field on
\mathcal{S}
,

•

\widehat{L}_{i\perp}
is seen as smooth vector fields on
\mathcal{S}
,

•

F_{ij}(\widehat{K}_{g})
and
G_{ijk}(\widehat{K}_{g},\kappa)
are defined by equations (
2.54
).

Remark
.

The fields
M
,
z
,
\widehat{L}_{i\perp}
and
\widehat{K}_{g}
are defined up to a sign unless an orientation on
\mathcal{M}
is specified so that
e_{\perp}
can be defined uniquely.
∎

Proof.

Let us use a frame field
(e_{\perp},(e_{\bf i}))
adapted to
\mathcal{S}
with respect to
g
, see
Section

2.6.1
. The constraints on
\mathcal{S}
arise from the equations in the (CVE) with no normal covariant derivatives. Namely,

•

Equation (
CVE.1
). One has

\displaystyle\widehat{\nabla}_{\bf i}V^{\bf i}{}_{\perp\bf jk}

\displaystyle=\widehat{D}_{\bf i}V^{\bf i}{}_{\perp\bf jk}-\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf i}{}^{\perp}{}_{\perp}V^{\bf i}{}_{\bf\perp jk}-\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf i}{}^{\bf l}{}_{\perp}V^{\bf i}{}_{\bf ljk}-\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf i}{}^{\perp}{}_{\bf j}V^{\bf i}{}_{\perp\bf\perp k}-\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf i}{}^{\perp}{}_{\bf k}V^{\bf i}{}_{\perp\bf j\perp}

\displaystyle=-\varepsilon\widehat{D}_{\bf i}M_{\bf jk}{}^{\bf i}+\varepsilon\kappa_{\bf i}M_{\bf jk}{}^{\bf i}-2\varepsilon(\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf i}{}^{\bf l}S_{\bf l[j}{}^{\bf im}E_{\bf k]m}+2\varepsilon(\widehat{K}_{g})^{\bf i}{}_{\bf[j}E_{\bf k]i}

\displaystyle=-\varepsilon\left(\widehat{D}_{\bf i}M_{\bf jk}{}^{\bf i}-\kappa_{\bf i}M_{\bf jk}{}^{\bf i}\right)+2\varepsilon(\widehat{K}_{g})^{\bf i}{}_{\bf[j}E_{\bf k]i}\,,

\displaystyle\widehat{\nabla}_{\bf i}V^{\bf i}{}_{\perp\bf j\perp}

\displaystyle=\widehat{D}_{\bf i}V^{\bf i}{}_{\perp\bf j\perp}-2\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf i}{}^{\perp}{}_{\perp}V^{\bf i}{}_{\bf\perp j\perp}-\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf i}{}^{\bf k}{}_{\perp}V^{\bf i}{}_{\bf kj\perp}-\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf i}{}^{\bf k}{}_{\perp}V^{\bf i}{}_{\bf\perp jk}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\widehat{D}_{\bf i}E^{\bf i}{}_{\bf j}-2\varepsilon\kappa_{\bf i}E^{\bf i}{}_{\bf j}-(\widehat{K}_{g})^{\bf ik}M_{\bf ikj}-(\widehat{K}_{g})^{\bf ik}M_{\bf jki}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\left(\widehat{D}_{\bf i}E^{\bf i}{}_{\bf j}-2\kappa_{\bf i}E^{\bf i}{}_{\bf j}\right)-(\widehat{K}_{g})^{\bf ik}M_{\bf jki}\,,

and

\displaystyle\kappa_{\bf a}V^{\bf a}{}_{\bf\perp jk}

\displaystyle=-\varepsilon\kappa_{\bf i}M_{\bf jk}{}^{\bf i}\,,

\displaystyle\kappa_{\bf a}V^{\bf a}{}_{\bf\perp j\perp}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\kappa_{\bf i}E^{\bf i}{}_{\bf j}\,.

Hence (
4.8a
) and (
4.8b
).

•

Equation (
CVE.2
). This leads to a slightly modified version of the Codazzi equations (
2.53
) where the Weyl tensor
\widehat{W}^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\rho}
is replaced by
\Theta V^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\rho}
. Since
\dim\mathcal{S}=n=3
, Weyl candidates vanish identically. Therefore, equation (
2.53b
) trivially holds and is discarded. Hence (
4.8c
)-(
4.8e
).

•

Equation (
CVE.3
). From

\displaystyle\widehat{\nabla}_{\bf i}\widehat{L}_{\bf jk}

\displaystyle=\widehat{D}_{\bf i}\widehat{L}_{\bf jk}-\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf i}{}^{\perp}{}_{\bf j}\widehat{L}_{\perp\bf k}-\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf i}{}^{\perp}{}_{\bf k}\widehat{L}_{\bf j\perp}

\displaystyle=\widehat{D}_{\bf i}\widehat{L}_{\bf jk}-(\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf ij}\widehat{L}_{\bf k\perp}-(\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf ik}\widehat{L}_{\bf j\perp}\,,

\displaystyle\widehat{\nabla}_{\bf i}\widehat{L}_{\bf j\perp}

\displaystyle=\widehat{D}_{\bf i}\widehat{L}_{\bf j\perp}-\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf i}{}^{\perp}{}_{\bf j}\widehat{L}_{\perp\perp}-\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf i}{}^{\bf k}{}_{\perp}\widehat{L}_{\bf jk}-\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf i}{}^{\perp}{}_{\perp}\widehat{L}_{\bf j\perp}

\displaystyle=\widehat{D}_{\bf i}\widehat{L}_{\bf j\perp}-(\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf ij}\widehat{L}_{\perp\perp}+\varepsilon(\widehat{K})_{\bf i}{}^{\bf k}\widehat{L}_{\bf jk}-\kappa_{\bf i}\widehat{L}_{\bf j\perp}\,,

one deduces

\displaystyle 2\widehat{\nabla}_{\bf[i}\widehat{L}_{\bf j]k}

\displaystyle=2\widehat{D}_{\bf[i}\widehat{L}_{\bf j]k}-2(\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf k[i}\widehat{L}_{\bf j]\perp}\,,

\displaystyle 2\widehat{\nabla}_{\bf[i}\widehat{L}_{\bf j]\perp}

\displaystyle=2\widehat{D}_{\bf[i}\widehat{L}_{\bf j]\perp}+2\varepsilon(\widehat{K}_{g})^{\bf k}{}_{\bf[i}\widehat{L}_{\bf j]k}-2\kappa_{\bf[i}\widehat{L}_{\bf j]\perp}\,.

Moreover

\displaystyle(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{\bf d}V^{\bf d}{}_{\bf kij}

\displaystyle=(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{\perp}V^{\perp}{}_{\bf kij}+(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{\bf l}V^{\bf l}{}_{\bf kij}

\displaystyle=(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{\perp}M_{\bf ijk}-2(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{\bf l}S_{\bf k[i}{}^{\bf lm}E_{\bf j]m}\,,

\displaystyle(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{\bf d}V^{\bf d}{}_{\bf\perp ij}

\displaystyle=(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{\bf l}V^{\bf l}{}_{\bf\perp ij}

\displaystyle=-\varepsilon M_{\bf ij}{}^{\bf k}(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{\bf k}\,.

Hence (
4.8f
) and (
4.8g
).

•

Equation (
4.6a
). From

\displaystyle\widehat{\nabla}_{\bf i}(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{\bf j}

\displaystyle=\widehat{D}_{\bf i}(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{\bf j}-\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf i}{}^{\perp}{}_{\bf j}(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{\perp}

\displaystyle=\widehat{D}_{\bf i}(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{\bf j}-(\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf ij}(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{\perp}\,,

\displaystyle\widehat{\nabla}_{\bf i}(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{\perp}

\displaystyle=(d(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{\perp})_{\bf i}-\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf i}{}^{\perp}{}_{\perp}(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{\perp}-\widehat{\Gamma}_{\bf i}{}^{\bf k}{}_{\perp}(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{\bf k}

\displaystyle=(d(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{\perp})_{\bf i}-\kappa_{\bf i}{}(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{\perp}+\varepsilon(\widehat{K}_{g})_{\bf i}{}^{\bf k}(\widehat{\zeta}_{g})_{\bf k}\,,

one obtains (
4.8h
) and (
4.8i
).

•

Equation (
4.7
) gives (
4.8j
).

•

Equation (
4.6b
) gives directly (
4.8k
). ∎

4.1.4
The extended conformal vacuum constraint equations

An intrinsic framework for the constraint equations (
4.8
), that is one independent of the (CVE), can be introduced as done below.

Definition 4.10
.

Denote by
\mathscr{D}
the set of all 10-tuples
(\mathcal{S},h,E,M,\Psi,\kappa,K,z,T,Q)
where

•

(\mathcal{S},h)
is a 3-dimensional smooth Riemannian or Lorentzian manifold with corners (see
Section

2.2
),

•

E
is a symmetric trace-free 2-tensor and
M
is a Cotton candidate (see
2.47
) on
(\mathcal{S},h)
,

•

\Psi
and
z
are smooth functions on
\mathcal{S}
,

•

\kappa
and
T
are smooth covector fields on
\mathcal{S}
,

•

K
is a smooth symmetric 2-tensor on
\mathcal{S}
,

•

Q
is a smooth 2-tensor on
\mathcal{S}
.

Definition 4.11
.

A 10-tuple
(\mathcal{S},h,E,M,\Psi,\kappa,K,z,T,Q)\in\mathscr{D}
is said to be a solution to the
extended Conformal Vacuum Constraint equations (CVC)
if the following tensorial equations are satisfied on
\mathcal{S}

\displaystyle\widehat{D}_{i}M_{jk}{}^{i}

\displaystyle=2K^{i}{}_{[j}E_{k]i}+2\kappa_{i}M_{jk}{}^{i}\,,

(CVC.1)

\displaystyle\widehat{D}_{i}E^{i}{}_{j}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon K^{ik}M_{jki}+3\kappa_{i}E^{i}{}_{j}\,,

(CVC.2)

\displaystyle Q_{ij}-\widehat{l}_{ij}

\displaystyle=\Psi E_{ij}-\varepsilon F_{ij}(K)\,,

(CVC.3)

\displaystyle 2T_{k}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon h^{ij}G_{kij}(K,\kappa)\,,

(CVC.4)

\displaystyle\Psi M_{klj}

\displaystyle=G_{klj}(K,\kappa)+h_{j[k}h^{ip}G_{l]ip}(K,\kappa)\,,

(CVC.5)

\displaystyle 2\widehat{D}_{[i}Q_{j]k}

\displaystyle=2K_{k[i}T_{j]}+zM_{ijk}-2(\widehat{\zeta}_{h})_{l}S_{k[i}{}^{lm}E_{j]m}\,,

(CVC.6)

\displaystyle 2\widehat{D}_{[i}T_{j]}

\displaystyle=-2\varepsilon K^{k}{}_{[i}Q_{j]k}+2\kappa_{[i}T_{j]}-\varepsilon M_{ij}{}^{k}(\widehat{\zeta}_{h})_{k}\,,

(CVC.7)

\displaystyle\widehat{D}_{i}(\widehat{\zeta}_{h})_{j}+\kappa_{i}(\widehat{\zeta}_{h})_{j}

\displaystyle=zK_{ij}-\Psi Q_{ij}+\widehat{\sigma}h_{ij}\,,

(CVC.8)

\displaystyle(dz)_{i}

\displaystyle=-\varepsilon K_{i}{}^{j}(\widehat{\zeta}_{h})_{j}-\Psi T_{i}\,,

(CVC.9)

\displaystyle(d\widehat{\sigma})_{i}-\kappa_{i}\widehat{\sigma}

\displaystyle=-\varepsilon zT_{i}-Q_{ij}h^{jk}(\widehat{\zeta}_{h})_{k}\,,

(CVC.10)

\displaystyle\widehat{\sigma}\Psi

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(h^{ij}(\widehat{\zeta}_{h})_{i}(\widehat{\zeta}_{h})_{j}+\varepsilon z^{2}+\frac{\Lambda}{3}\right)\,,

(CVC.11)

where

•

\varepsilon=-1
(respectively
+1
) if
(\mathcal{S},h)
is Riemannian (respectively Lorentzian),

•

\widehat{D}
is the Weyl connection associated to the covector field
\kappa
with respect to
h
,

•

\widehat{l}_{ij}
is the Schouten tensor of
\widehat{D}
,

•

F_{ij}(K)
and
G_{ijk}(K,\kappa)
are defined by equations (
2.54
),

•

one defines the following fields

\displaystyle(\widehat{\zeta}_{h})_{i}

\displaystyle:=\Psi\kappa_{i}+(d\Psi)_{i}\,,

(4.10a)

\displaystyle\widehat{s}_{h}

\displaystyle:=\frac{h^{ij}}{3}\left(\widehat{D}_{i}(\widehat{\zeta}_{h})_{j}+\kappa_{i}(\widehat{\zeta}_{h})_{j}+\Psi\widehat{l}_{ij}\right)\,,

(4.10b)

\displaystyle\widehat{\sigma}

\displaystyle:=\widehat{s}_{h}-\frac{z}{3}K_{k}{}^{k}-\varepsilon\frac{\Psi}{12}\left(\left(K_{k}{}^{k}\right)^{2}-K^{kl}K_{kl}\right)\,.

(4.10c)

Remarks
.

•

The correspondence between the intrinsic fields of the (CVC) and the fields of (
4.8
) are as follows:
h
is the metric induced by
g
on
\mathcal{S}
,
(E,M)
is still the restriction of the electromangetic decomposition of
V
with respect to
\mathcal{S}
,
\Psi
is the restriction of
\Theta
on
\mathcal{S}
,
\kappa_{i}
is the tangent part on
\mathcal{S}
of the 4-dimensional covector field
\kappa_{\mu}
,
K_{ij}
is the extrinsic curvature of the connection
\widehat{\nabla}
with respect to
h
,
z
is the scalar field
(\widehat{\zeta}_{h})_{\perp}
,
T_{i}
is the covector field
\widehat{L}_{i\perp}
and
Q_{ij}
is the field
\widehat{L}_{ij}
on
\mathcal{S}
.

•

Equations (
4.10a
) and (
4.10b
) are the 3-dimensional analogue of equations (
4.2
) and (
4.4
). Hence,
\widehat{s}_{h}
is called the 3-dimensional (extended) Friedrich scalar. Its non-extended version, that is when
\kappa=0
, is given by

s_{h}:=\frac{1}{3}\left(\Delta_{h}\Psi+\Psi h^{ij}l_{ij}\right)=\frac{1}{3}\left(\Delta_{h}\Psi+\frac{r}{4}\Psi\right)\,.\qed

(4.11)

Let us detail below the gauges invariances of the (CVC), inherited from these of the (CVE) which have been presented in
Section

4.1.1
. The invariance under Weyl connection changes split into two gauge invariances by distinguishing tangent and normal directions.

Definition 4.12
.

Let
(\mathcal{S},h,E,M,\Psi,\kappa,K,z,T,Q)\in\mathscr{D}
. Define

•

for any confomorphism
\phi:(\mathcal{S},h)\to(\overline{\mathcal{S}},\overline{h})
(see
2.20
),

\displaystyle\Conf_{\phi}\left[(\mathcal{S},h,E,M,\Psi,\kappa,K,z,T,Q)\right]:=\big(\overline{\mathcal{S}},\overline{h},\phi_{\star}(\Omega^{-1}E),\phi_{\star}M,\phi_{\star}(\Omega\Psi),\phi_{\star}(\kappa-d\ln\Omega),

\displaystyle\phi_{\star}(\Omega K),\phi_{\star}z,\phi_{\star}(\Omega^{-1}T),\phi_{\star}Q\big)\in\mathscr{D}\,,

where
\Omega\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{S},\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\star})
is the conformal factor of
\phi
, that is
\phi^{\star}\overline{h}=\Omega^{2}h
,

•

for any smooth covector field
\omega\in T^{\star}\mathcal{S}
,

\displaystyle\Weylchg_{\omega}^{\parallel}\left[(\mathcal{S},h,E,M,\Psi,\kappa,K,z,T,Q)\right]:=\Big(\mathcal{S},h,E,M,\Psi,\kappa+\omega,K,z,T_{i}-\varepsilon K_{i}{}^{j}\omega_{j},

\displaystyle Q_{ij}-\widehat{D}_{i}\omega_{j}+\frac{1}{2}S_{ij}{}^{kl}\omega_{k}\omega_{l}\Big)\in\mathscr{D}\,,

•

for any smooth function
\chi\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{S},\mathbb{R})
,

\displaystyle\Weylchg_{\chi}^{\perp}\left[(\mathcal{S},h,E,M,\Psi,\kappa,K,z,T,Q)\right]:=\Big(\mathcal{S},h,E,M,\Psi,\kappa,K-\varepsilon\chi h,z+\chi\Psi,T-d\chi+\chi\kappa,

\displaystyle Q+\chi K-\frac{\varepsilon\chi^{2}}{2}h\Big)\in\mathscr{D}\,,

•

the sign change operator by

\displaystyle\Signchg\left[(\mathcal{S},h,E,M,\Psi,\kappa,K,z,T,Q)\right]:=(\mathcal{S},h,-E,-M,-\Psi,\kappa,K,-z,T,Q)\in\mathscr{D}\,,

Remark
.

Under the transformations defined above, the scalar field
\widehat{s}_{h}
transforms as follows

\displaystyle\widehat{s}_{h}\overset{\Conf_{\phi}}{\longmapsto}\phi_{\star}(\Omega^{-1}\widehat{s}_{h})\,,\qquad

\displaystyle\widehat{s}_{h}

\displaystyle\overset{\Weylchg_{\omega}^{\parallel}}{\longmapsto}\widehat{s}_{h}+h^{-1}(\omega,\widehat{\zeta}_{h})+\frac{\Psi}{2}h^{-1}(\omega,\omega)\,,

(4.12a)

\displaystyle\widehat{s}_{h}\overset{\Signchg}{\longmapsto}-\widehat{s}_{h}\,.\qquad

\displaystyle\widehat{s}_{h}

\displaystyle\overset{\Weylchg_{\chi}^{\perp}}{\longmapsto}\widehat{s}_{h}\,,

(4.12b)

and the scalar field
\widehat{\sigma}
as follows

\displaystyle\widehat{\sigma}\overset{\Conf_{\phi}}{\longmapsto}\phi_{\star}(\Omega^{-1}\widehat{\sigma})\,,\qquad

\displaystyle\widehat{\sigma}

\displaystyle\overset{\Weylchg_{\omega}^{\parallel}}{\longmapsto}\widehat{\sigma}+h^{-1}(\omega,\widehat{\zeta}_{h})+\frac{\Psi}{2}h^{-1}(\omega,\omega)\,,

(4.13a)

\displaystyle\widehat{\sigma}\overset{\Signchg}{\longmapsto}-\widehat{\sigma}\,,\qquad

\displaystyle\widehat{\sigma}

\displaystyle\overset{\Weylchg_{\chi}^{\perp}}{\longmapsto}\widehat{\sigma}+\varepsilon\left(\chi z+\frac{\Psi}{2}\chi^{2}\right)\,.

(4.13b)

∎

Definition 4.13
.

Two 10-tuples in
\mathscr{D}
are said to be
gauge-equivalent
if they are related by a composition of confomorphisms
\Conf_{\phi}
, tangent Weyl connection changes
\Weylchg^{\parallel}_{\omega}
, normal Weyl connection changes
\Weylchg^{\perp}_{\chi}
and sign changes
\Signchg
.

Proposition 4.14
.

The (CVC) are invariant under the transformations of
4.12
.

4.1.5
The conformal boundary

One main advantage of the (CVE) compared with the (VE) is that they allow to directly include the conformal boundary of spacetimes within the manifold by removing all singularities on it. In fact, the conformal boundary is simply encoded through the following definition.

Definition 4.15
.

The
conformal boundary
of a solution
(\mathcal{M},g,V,\Theta,\kappa)\in\mathscr{E}
of the (CVE) is defined as the gauge-invariant subset

\mathscr{I}:=\{p\in\mathcal{M}\mid\Theta(p)=0\}\,.

(4.14)

The geometry and causal nature of
\mathscr{I}
is given by the following lemma.

Lemma 4.16
.

Let
(\mathcal{M},g,V,\Theta,\kappa)\in\mathscr{E}
be a solution to the (CVE) with
\mathscr{I}\neq\varnothing
. Then

g^{-1}(d\Theta,d\Theta)=-\frac{\Lambda}{3}\quad\text{on }\mathscr{I}\,.

(4.15)

In particular, if
\Lambda<0
(respectively
\Lambda>0
) then
\mathscr{I}
is a union of disjoint timelike (respectively spacelike) hypersurfaces.

Proof.

The result is straightforward from (
4.2
) and (
4.6b
).
∎

Corollary 4.17
.

Let
(\mathcal{M},g,V,\Theta,\kappa)\in\mathscr{E}
be a solution to the (CVE) for a cosmological constant
\Lambda\neq 0
such that
\mathscr{I}\neq\varnothing
. Then for any connected component
\mathfrak{S}
of
\mathscr{I}
, the 10-tuple
(\mathfrak{S},\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{E},\mathfrak{M},\Psi,\kappa,\mathfrak{K},\mathfrak{z},\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{Q})
induced by
(\mathcal{M},g,V,\Theta,\kappa)
on
\mathfrak{S}
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathfrak{z}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{|\Lambda|}{3}\,,

(4.16a)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{K}_{ij}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\widehat{\sigma}}{\mathfrak{z}}\mathfrak{h}_{ij}\,,

(4.16b)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i}\mathfrak{E}^{i}{}_{j}

\displaystyle=3\kappa_{i}\mathfrak{E}^{i}{}_{j}\,,

(4.16c)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{z}\mathfrak{M}_{ijk}

\displaystyle=\widehat{\mathfrak{c}}_{ijk}=\mathfrak{c}_{ijk}\,,

(4.16d)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{T}_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sign(\Lambda)}{\mathfrak{z}}\left((d\widehat{\sigma})_{i}-\kappa_{i}\widehat{\sigma}\right)\,,

(4.16e)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Q}_{ij}

\displaystyle=\widehat{\mathfrak{l}}_{ij}+\frac{3\widehat{\sigma}^{2}}{2\Lambda}\mathfrak{h}_{ij}\,,

(4.16f)

where
\widehat{\sigma}
is the scalar field defined by (
4.10c
),
\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}
is the Weyl connection associated to
\kappa
with respect to
\mathfrak{h}
(it is also the connection induced by
\widehat{\nabla}
on
\mathfrak{S}
by
2.56
),
\widehat{\mathfrak{l}}_{ij}
and
\widehat{\mathfrak{c}}_{ijk}
are respectively the Schouten and Cotton tensors of
\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}
and
\mathfrak{c}_{ijk}
is the Cotton tensor of the Levi-Civita connection
\mathfrak{D}
of
\mathfrak{h}
. Note that since
\mathfrak{S}
is 3-dimensional, the Cotton tensors of any two Weyl connection coincide, see the transformation law (
2.28b
).

Remark
.

Under the hypotheses of the corollary,
\mathscr{I}
is a union of disjoint non-null hypersurfaces by
4.16
. Thus, a connected component
\mathfrak{S}
of
\mathscr{I}
is a hypersurface of
(\mathcal{M},g)
.
∎

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{S}
be a connected component of
\mathscr{I}
. Since
\Psi=\Omega|_{\mathfrak{S}}=0
, the (CVC) on
\mathfrak{S}
simplify into

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i}\mathfrak{M}_{jk}{}^{i}

\displaystyle=2\mathfrak{K}^{i}{}_{[j}\mathfrak{E}_{k]i}+2\kappa_{i}\mathfrak{M}_{jk}{}^{i}\,,

(4.17a)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i}\mathfrak{E}^{i}{}_{j}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\mathfrak{K}^{ik}\mathfrak{M}_{jki}+3\kappa_{i}\mathfrak{E}^{i}{}_{j}\,,

(4.17b)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Q}_{ij}-\widehat{\mathfrak{l}}_{ij}

\displaystyle=-\varepsilon F_{ij}(\mathfrak{K})\,,

(4.17c)

\displaystyle 2\mathfrak{T}_{k}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\mathfrak{h}^{ij}G_{kij}(\mathfrak{K},\kappa)\,,

(4.17d)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=G_{klj}(\mathfrak{K},\kappa)+\mathfrak{h}_{j[k}\mathfrak{h}^{ip}G_{l]ip}(\mathfrak{K},\kappa)\,,

(4.17e)

\displaystyle 2\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{[i}\mathfrak{Q}_{j]k}

\displaystyle=2\mathfrak{K}_{k[i}\mathfrak{T}_{j]}+\mathfrak{z}\mathfrak{M}_{ijk}\,,

(4.17f)

\displaystyle 2\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{[i}\mathfrak{T}_{j]}

\displaystyle=-2\varepsilon\mathfrak{K}^{k}{}_{[i}\mathfrak{Q}_{j]k}+2\kappa_{[i}\mathfrak{T}_{j]}\,,

(4.17g)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{z}\mathfrak{K}_{ij}+\widehat{\sigma}\mathfrak{h}_{ij}\,,

(4.17h)

\displaystyle(d\mathfrak{z})_{i}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(4.17i)

\displaystyle(d\widehat{\sigma})_{i}-\kappa_{i}\widehat{\sigma}

\displaystyle=-\varepsilon\mathfrak{z}\mathfrak{T}_{i}\,,

(4.17j)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\mathfrak{z}^{2}+\frac{\Lambda}{3}\,.

(4.17k)

Equation (
4.17i
) implies that
\mathfrak{z}
is constant. Then (
4.17k
) gives the value of
\mathfrak{z}^{2}
and
\varepsilon=-\sign(\lambda)
. Then equation (
4.17h
) gives (
4.16b
). It follows from (
4.17c
) and (
4.17d
) or (
4.17j
) that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Q}_{ij}

\displaystyle=\widehat{\mathfrak{l}}_{ij}-\frac{\varepsilon\widehat{\sigma}^{2}}{2\mathfrak{z}^{2}}\mathfrak{h}_{ij}=\widehat{\mathfrak{l}}_{ij}+\frac{3\widehat{\sigma}^{2}}{2\Lambda}\mathfrak{h}_{ij}\,,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{T}_{i}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\varepsilon}{\mathfrak{z}}\left((d\widehat{\sigma})_{i}-\kappa_{i}\widehat{\sigma}\right)=\frac{\sign(\Lambda)}{\mathfrak{z}}\left((d\widehat{\sigma})_{i}-\kappa_{i}\widehat{\sigma}\right)\,.

Then (
4.17f
) and (
4.17b
) resume to

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathfrak{c}}_{ijk}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{z}\mathfrak{M}_{ijk}\,,

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i}\mathfrak{E}^{i}{}_{j}

\displaystyle=3\kappa_{i}\mathfrak{E}^{i}{}_{j}\,.

Finally, (
4.17a
) is just the contracted fourth Bianchi identity (
2.27
), and equations (
4.17e
), (
4.17g
) hold.
∎

Equation (
4.16b
) means that the extrinsic curvature
\widehat{\mathfrak{K}}_{g}
of
\widehat{\nabla}
with respect to
g
is given by

(\widehat{\mathfrak{K}}_{g})_{ij}=-\frac{\widehat{s}_{g}|_{\mathfrak{S}}}{\mathfrak{z}}\mathfrak{h}_{ij}\,,

where
\widehat{s}_{g}
is the extended Friedrich scalar defined by (
4.4
). This implies that all connected components
\mathfrak{S}
of the conformal boundary
\mathscr{I}
are umbilical in the conformal structure
(\mathcal{M},[g])
, see
2.59
. Furthermore, the extended Friedrich scalar controls the extrinsic curvature and thus plays an important role to determine whether a conformal geodesic starting on the conformal boundary stay on it or not, as stated in the lemma below. This lemma is given here for comparison purpose since it was used in Friedrich’s proof but will not be necessary in our work.

Lemma 4.18

(Extended version of Friedrich
[
38
, Lemma 4.1]
)
.

Let
(\mathcal{M},g,V,\Theta,\kappa)\in\mathscr{E}
be a solution to the (CVE),
\widehat{\nabla}
be the Weyl connection associated to
\kappa
with respect to
g
given by
2.24
and
(x,\widehat{\beta})
be a
\widehat{\nabla}
-conformal geodesic of initial data
(x_{\star},\dot{x}_{\star},\widehat{\beta}_{\star})
with
x_{\star}\in\mathfrak{S}
, where
\mathfrak{S}
is a connected component of
\mathscr{I}
. If the initial data satisfy

\langle d\Theta,\dot{x}_{\star}\rangle=0\quad\text{and}\quad g^{-1}(\widehat{\beta}_{\star},d\Theta)=-\widehat{s}_{g}(x_{\star})\,,

(4.18)

then

i)

the curve
x
stays on
\mathfrak{S}
,

ii)

along the curve
x
, the normal part of
\widehat{\beta}
is fully imposed by

g^{-1}(\widehat{\beta},d\Theta)=-\widehat{s}_{g}\,,

iii)

(x,\Tan\widehat{\beta})
is a
\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}
-conformal geodesic where
\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}
is the Weyl connection induced by
\widehat{\nabla}
on
\mathfrak{S}
, see
Section

2.6.2
.

Proof.

We adapt the proof provided in
[
38
, Section 4.4]
to Weyl connections. Let us divide it into three steps.

•

Step 1
: Reduction to the case
\widehat{s}_{g}=0
on
\mathfrak{S}

Take a smooth covector field
\omega
on
\mathcal{M}
such that

\omega=\frac{3\widehat{s}_{g}}{\Lambda}d\Theta\quad\text{on }\mathfrak{S}\,.

Then, using (
4.15
),

g^{-1}(\omega,d\Theta)=-\widehat{s}_{g}\quad\text{on }\mathfrak{S}\,.

Let
\widecheck{\nabla}
be the Weyl connection associated to
\widecheck{\kappa}_{g}:=\kappa+\omega
with respect to
g
. By the transformation laws (
4.5
) of the extended Friedrich scalar, one has
\widecheck{s}_{g}=0
on
\mathfrak{S}
. By
2.41
, the
\widehat{\nabla}
-conformal geodesic
(x,\widehat{\beta})
corresponds to the
\widecheck{\nabla}
-conformal geodesic
(x,\widecheck{\beta})
where
\widecheck{\beta}:=\widehat{\beta}-\omega
along
x
.

•

Step 2
: Gauge construction

Fix
p\in\mathfrak{S}
. Let
(y^{i})
be a local chart on an open neighbourhood
\mathfrak{U}
of
p
in
\mathfrak{S}
and
(e_{\bf i})
be a smooth frame field on
\mathfrak{U}
.

Consider
\widecheck{\nabla}
-geodesic curves starting from
\mathfrak{U}
with initial vector a unit normal vector
e_{\perp}
to
\mathfrak{S}
with respect to
\mathfrak{h}
, the metric induced by
g
on
\mathfrak{S}
. The geodesics form a congruence on a neighbourhood
\mathcal{U}
of
p
in
\mathcal{M}
. Let us denote the tangent vector field to the geodesics by
e_{\perp}
and the parameter of the geodesics initialised at
0
on
\mathfrak{U}
by
y^{\perp}
. Thus

\widecheck{\nabla}_{e_{\perp}}e_{\perp}=0\,,\quad e_{\perp}{}^{\perp}:=\langle dy^{\perp},e_{\perp}\rangle=1\,,\quad\mathfrak{S}\cap\mathcal{U}=\{q\in\mathcal{U}\mid y^{\perp}(q)=0\}\,.

Extend the functions
(y^{i})
from
\mathfrak{U}
to
\mathcal{U}
by taking them constant along the geodesics and the vector fields
(e_{\bf i})
by parallel transport along the geodesics. That is to say

e_{\perp}{}^{i}:=\langle dy^{i},e_{\perp}\rangle=0\,,\qquad\widecheck{\nabla}_{e_{\perp}}e_{\bf i}=0\,.

Then
(y^{\alpha}):=(y^{\perp},(y^{i}))
are Gaussian coordinates associated to the congruence of geodesics and
(e_{\bf a}):=(e_{\perp},(e_{\bf i}))
is a frame field adapted to
\mathfrak{S}
with respect to
g
on
\mathcal{U}
with
g(e_{\perp},e_{\bf i})=0
on
\mathcal{U}
.

•

Step 3
: Analysis

Denote by
I
the (maximal) open interval (containing the initial parameter
\tau_{\star}
) on which the conformal geodesic
(x,\widecheck{\beta})
is defined. Define the subinterval

I_{\mathfrak{S}}:=\left\{\tau\in I\,\big|\,x(\tau)\in\mathfrak{S},\dot{x}(\tau)\text{ tangent to }\mathfrak{S},\widecheck{\beta}(\tau)\text{ tangent to }\mathfrak{S}\right\}\,.

Thanks to hypotheses (
4.18
) and step 1, one has
\tau_{\star}\in I_{\mathfrak{S}}
. By smoothness of the conformal geodesic
(x,\widecheck{\beta})
,
I_{\mathfrak{S}}
is a closed subset of
I
.

Let
\tau\in I_{\mathfrak{S}}
and
((y^{\alpha}),(e_{\bf a}))
be the gauge constructed on a neighbourhood
\mathcal{U}
of
x(\tau)\in\mathfrak{S}
in
\mathcal{M}
by step 2. As
\widecheck{s}_{g}=0
on
\mathfrak{S}
, the constraint equations (
4.16
) on
\mathfrak{S}
give in particular

(\widecheck{\mathfrak{K}}_{g})_{\bf ij}=0\,,\qquad\widecheck{L}_{\bf i\perp}=0\,,\qquad\widecheck{L}_{\perp\bf i}=0\,,\qquad\widecheck{L}_{\bf ij}=\widecheck{\mathfrak{l}}_{\bf ij}\,.

By writing the
\widecheck{\nabla}
-conformal geodesic equations in the frame field
(e_{\bf a})
and coordinates
(y^{\alpha})
, one finds that the normal components verify

\displaystyle\dot{x}(y^{\perp}\circ x)

\displaystyle=\dot{x}^{\perp}\,,

\displaystyle\dot{x}(\dot{x}^{\perp})

\displaystyle=-2\langle\widecheck{\beta},\dot{x}\rangle\dot{x}^{\perp}+g(\dot{x},\dot{x})g(e_{\perp},e_{\perp})\widecheck{\beta}_{\perp}\,,

\displaystyle\dot{x}(\widecheck{\beta}_{\perp})

\displaystyle=\langle\widecheck{\beta},\dot{x}\rangle\widecheck{\beta}_{\perp}-\frac{1}{2}g^{-1}(\widecheck{\beta},\widecheck{\beta})g(e_{\perp},e_{\perp})\dot{x}^{\perp}+\widecheck{L}_{\perp\perp}\dot{x}^{\perp}\,,

This is a homogeneous system in
(y^{\perp}\circ x,\dot{x}^{\perp},\widecheck{\beta}_{\perp})
. Since the latter triple vanishes at
\tau\in I_{\mathfrak{S}}
, it vanishes on an open neighbourhood of
\tau
in
I
. Thus
I_{\mathfrak{S}}
is an open subset of
I
. Since
I
is connected, one has
I_{\mathfrak{S}}=I
. Hence the curve
x
stays on
\mathfrak{S}
and the covector field
\widecheck{\beta}
remains tangent to
\mathfrak{S}
.

Finally, the tangential components of the
\widecheck{\nabla}
-conformal geodesic equations, in the gauge constructed by step 2, reduce to the
\widecheck{\mathfrak{D}}
-conformal geodesic equations. ∎

Similarly, one can define the conformal boundary of a solution
(\mathcal{S},h,E,M,\Psi,\kappa,K,z,T,Q)\in\mathscr{D}
to the (CVC) as the gauge-invariant subset

\not{\mathscr{I}}:=\{p\in\mathcal{S}\mid\Psi(p)=0\}\,.

However, contrary to the (CVE), the conformal boundary can cover the whole of
\mathcal{S}
, just as considered above in
4.17
.

Lemma 4.19
.

Let
(\mathcal{S},h,E,M,\Psi,\kappa,K,z,T,Q)\in\mathscr{D}
be a solution to the (CVC) with
\not{\mathscr{I}}\neq\varnothing
. Then

h^{-1}(d\Psi,d\Psi)=-\varepsilon z^{2}-\frac{\Lambda}{3}\quad\text{on }\not{\mathscr{I}}\,.

(4.19)

Proof.

The result is straightforward from (
4.10a
) and (
CVC.11
).
∎

4.1.6
Relation with the vacuum Einstein equations

The (CVE) are closely related to the Vacuum Einstein equations (VE) away from the conformal boundary as shown in the following proposition.

Proposition 4.20
.

i)

If
(\mathcal{M},\widetilde{g})
is a solution to the (
VE
) then for any smooth nowhere vanishing function
\Theta\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M},\mathbb{R}^{\star})
and any smooth covector field
\kappa
on
\mathcal{M}
, the quintuple
(\mathcal{M},\Theta^{2}\widetilde{g},\Theta^{-1}\widetilde{W},\Theta,\kappa)\in\mathscr{E}
, where
\widetilde{W}
is the Weyl tensor of
\widetilde{g}
, is a solution to the (
4.1
).

ii)

Let
(\mathcal{M},g,V,\Theta,\kappa)\in\mathscr{E}
be a solution to the (
4.1
). Then, on any open set
\mathcal{U}
where
\Theta
nowhere vanishes,
(\mathcal{U},|\Theta|^{-2}g)
is a solution to the (
VE
).

Proof.

i)
Assume that
(\mathcal{M},\widetilde{g})
is a 4-dimensional smooth Lorentzian manifold solution to the (VE), that is

\widetilde{L}_{\alpha\beta}=\frac{\Lambda}{6}\widetilde{g}_{\alpha\beta}\,.

(4.20)

Let
\Theta
be a smooth nowhere vanishing function and
\kappa
be a smooth covector field on
\mathcal{M}
. Take
\widehat{\nabla}
the Weyl connection associated to
\kappa+d\ln|\Theta|
with respect to
\widetilde{g}
by
2.24
. It follows that it is associated to
\kappa
with respect to
g:=\Theta^{2}\widetilde{g}
.

Equation (
CVE.2
) is the decomposition (
2.22
) of the Riemann tensor of a Weyl connection with
V:=\Theta^{-1}\widehat{W}
. Since
\widehat{W}=\widetilde{W}
by (
2.28c
), one has
V=\Theta^{-1}\widetilde{W}
. Equation (
4.20
) implies that the Cotton tensor
\widetilde{C}_{\alpha\beta\gamma}
of
\widetilde{g}
vanishes identically. Then the transformation law (
2.28b
) of the Cotton tensor gives equation (
CVE.3
). Moreover, equation (
CVE.1
) comes from the once contracted second Bianchi identity (
2.26a
). Finally, equation (
CVE.4
) is derived from the transformation law (
2.28a
) of the Schouten tensor and (
4.20
).

ii)
Let
(\mathcal{M},g,V,\Theta,\kappa)\in\mathscr{E}
be a solution to the (CVE) and
\mathcal{U}
be an open set where
\Theta
nowhere vanishes. Then
\widetilde{g}:=\Theta^{-2}g|_{\mathcal{U}}
is a well-defined Lorentzian metric on
\mathcal{U}
in the conformal class
[g]
on
\mathcal{U}
. Using (
CVE.4
) and (
4.2
), one finds

\widehat{L}_{\alpha\beta}=-\widehat{\nabla}_{\alpha}(\widehat{\kappa}_{\widetilde{g}})_{\beta}-\frac{1}{2}S_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\mu\nu}(\widehat{\kappa}_{\widetilde{g}})_{\mu}(\widehat{\kappa}_{\widetilde{g}})_{\nu}+\frac{\Lambda}{6}\frac{g_{\alpha\beta}}{\Theta^{2}}\,,

where
\widehat{\kappa}_{\widetilde{g}}=\kappa+d\ln|\Theta|
. Thus, the transformation law (
2.28a
) of the Schouten tensor gives that the Schouten tensor of
\widetilde{g}
verifies

\widetilde{L}_{\alpha\beta}=\frac{\Lambda}{6}\widetilde{g}_{\alpha\beta}\,.

Consequently,
(\mathcal{U},\widetilde{g})
is a solution to the (VE).
∎

A similar situation occurs for the constraint equations. Recall that a triple
(\widetilde{\mathcal{S}},\widetilde{h},\widetilde{K})
, where
(\widetilde{\mathcal{S}},\widetilde{h})
is a 3-dimensional Riemannian or Lorentzian manifold and
\widetilde{K}
is a symmetric 2-tensor on
\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}
, is said to be a solution to the Vacuum Constraint equations (VC) if

\displaystyle\widetilde{r}-\varepsilon\left((\widetilde{K}^{i}{}_{i})^{2}-\widetilde{K}_{ij}\widetilde{K}^{ij}\right)

\displaystyle=2\Lambda\,,

(VC.1)

\displaystyle\widetilde{D}_{i}\widetilde{K}^{i}{}_{j}-\widetilde{D}_{j}\widetilde{K}^{i}{}_{i}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(VC.2)

where
\widetilde{D}
is the Levi-Civita connection of
\widetilde{h}
,
\widetilde{r}
is its scalar curvature,
\Lambda
is the cosmological constant and
\varepsilon=\pm 1
according to the signature. The counterpart of
4.20
is the following proposition.

Proposition 4.21
.

i)

If
(\widetilde{\mathcal{S}},\widetilde{h},\widetilde{K})
is a solution to the (
4.21
) then, for any smooth nowhere vanishing scalar function
\Psi\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\widetilde{\mathcal{S}},\mathbb{R}^{\star})
, any smooth covector field
\kappa
on
\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}
and any smooth scalar field
z
on
\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}
, the 10-tuple
(\widetilde{\mathcal{S}},h:=\Psi^{2}\widetilde{h},E,M,\Psi,\kappa,K,z,T,Q)\in\mathscr{D}
is a solution to the (
4.9
) where one defines in this order

\displaystyle K_{ij}

\displaystyle:=|\Psi|\widetilde{K}_{ij}-\varepsilon z\Psi^{-1}h_{ij}\,,

(4.22a)

\displaystyle Q_{ij}

\displaystyle:=\Psi^{-1}\left(-\widehat{D}_{i}(\widehat{\zeta}_{h})_{j}-\kappa_{i}(\widehat{\zeta}_{h})_{j}+zK_{ij}+\widehat{\sigma}h_{ij}\right)\,,

(4.22b)

\displaystyle E_{ij}

\displaystyle:=\Psi^{-1}\left(Q_{ij}-\widehat{l}_{ij}+\varepsilon F_{ij}(K)\right)\,,

(4.22c)

\displaystyle T_{i}

\displaystyle:=\Psi^{-1}\left(-(dz)_{i}-\varepsilon K_{i}{}^{j}(\widehat{\zeta}_{h})_{j}\right)\,,

(4.22d)

\displaystyle M_{klj}

\displaystyle:=\Psi^{-1}\left(G_{klj}(K,\kappa)+h_{j[k}h^{ip}G_{l]ip}(K,\kappa)\right)\,,

(4.22e)

\widehat{D}
being the Weyl connection associated to
\kappa
with respect to
h
,
\widehat{l}_{ij}
being its Schouten tensor,
\widehat{\sigma}
and
\widehat{\zeta}_{h}
being defined by (
4.10
) and
F_{ij}(K)
,
G_{ijk}(K,\kappa)
being defined by (
2.54
).

ii)

Let
(\mathcal{S},h,E,M,\Psi,\kappa,K,z,T,Q)\in\mathscr{D}
be a solution to the (
4.9
). Then, on any open set
\mathcal{U}
where
\Psi
nowhere vanishes,
(\mathcal{U},\Psi^{-2}h,|\Psi|^{-1}(K+\varepsilon z\Psi^{-1}h))
is a solution to the (
4.21
).

Remark
.
