on a neighbourhood of
\mathcal{B}_{\star}
in
\mathfrak{U}
.

ii)

Conversely, let
d_{1}
,
d_{2}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{U},\mathbb{R})
be some analytic boundary data on a neighbourhood
\mathfrak{U}
of
\mathcal{B}_{\star}
in
\mathcal{B}
and let
\underline{u}_{\star}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\overline{\mathcal{U}_{\star}},\mathbb{R}^{m})
be some analytic initial data verifying all the conditions of
4.34
. The knowledge of the restriction on
\mathcal{B}_{\star}
of
\underline{u}_{\star}
and of the analytic boundary data is sufficient to compute, on a neighbourhood of
\mathcal{B}_{\star}
in
\mathfrak{U}
, the metric
\breve{\mathfrak{h}}
induced on
\mathscr{I}=\mathcal{W}\cap\mathcal{B}\subset\mathfrak{U}
by the metric
\breve{g}
given by
4.35
.

Remark
.

Even if the boundary conditions (
4.77
) are homogeneous, the Cotton tensor of the conformal class
[\breve{\mathfrak{h}}]
is not necessarily vanishing identically. Indeed, it vanishes identically if and only if, in addition, the initial data
\underline{u}_{\star}
is such that
\breve{\mathfrak{y}}_{\bf 00}
and
\breve{\mathfrak{y}}_{\bf 0A}
vanish on
\mathcal{B}_{\star}
, or equivalently such that
H_{\bf 33}
and
E_{\bf 3A}
vanish on
\mathcal{B}_{\star}
. In that case, the conformal class is locally conformally flat by
ii)
of
2.37
. Since this is the minimal geometric condition ensuring that (
4.77
) is homogeneous, imposing a conformal class which is locally conformally flat can naturally be interpreted as the geometric homogeneous Dirichlet boundary condition.
∎

Proof.

We provide details of the proof, first presented in
[
38
, Section 7.1]
, so that this paper is self-contained.

i)

The key idea is that the frame field
(e_{\bf i})=(e_{\bf 0},e_{\bf 1},e_{\bf 2})
on
\mathscr{I}=\mathcal{W}\cap\mathcal{B}
, a priori defined from the solution
\underline{u}
of the evolution system, does actually not depend on solving the whole evolution system (which would require a boundary data) and can be reconstructed solely with the hypotheses of
i)
.

Let
\mathfrak{h}
be a representative of the conformal class
[\mathfrak{h}]
on
\mathfrak{U}
such that, on
\mathcal{B}_{\star}
, the frame field
(e_{\bf i})=(\partial_{0},e_{\bf 1}{}^{A}\partial_{A},e_{\bf 2}{}^{A}\partial_{A})
, where
e_{\bf 1}{}^{A}
and
e_{\bf 2}{}^{A}
are given by the restriction on
\mathcal{B}_{\star}
of the initial data
\underline{u}_{\star}
, is
\mathfrak{h}
-orthonormal.

For each point
q\in\mathcal{B}_{\star}
, start a
\mathfrak{D}
-conformal geodesic
(x,\beta)
with initial data
x_{\star}=q
,
\dot{x}_{\star}=\partial_{0}
and
\beta_{\star}=\kappa
, where
\kappa
is given by the restriction on
\mathcal{B}_{\star}
of
\underline{u}_{\star}
. This forms a congruence of timelike conformal geodesics on a neighbourhood of
\mathcal{B}_{\star}
in
\mathfrak{U}
. Let
\widecheck{\mathfrak{D}}
be the Weyl connection associated to
\beta
with respect to
\mathfrak{h}
. Set
e_{\bf 0}:=\dot{x}
and extend the vector fields
e_{\bf 1}
and
e_{\bf 2}
from
\mathcal{B}_{\star}
by parallel propagation with respect to
\widecheck{\mathfrak{D}}
.

Now that the frame field
(e_{\bf i})
is reconstructed, one can compute the Cotton-York tensor
\mathfrak{y}_{ij}
of
\mathfrak{h}
, see
2.48
, and eventually derive
\mathfrak{y}^{\dagger}_{\bf AB}
, giving the value of
q^{\prime\prime}_{\bf AB}
and thus of the analytic boundary data.

ii)

The proof of
ii)
is much more involved since it relies on an auxiliary geometric problem on the conformal boundary constituted by certain structure equations. If
(\mathscr{I},\mathfrak{h})
is a 3-dimensional oriented smooth Lorentzian manifold and
\kappa
is a smooth covector field on
\mathscr{I}
then

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathfrak{r}}^{i}{}_{jkl}

\displaystyle=2S_{j[k}{}^{im}\widehat{\mathfrak{l}}_{l]m}\,,

(4.79a)

\displaystyle 2\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{[i}\widehat{\mathfrak{l}}_{j]k}

\displaystyle=-\epsilon_{ij}{}^{l}\mathfrak{y}_{lk}\,,

(4.79b)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i}\mathfrak{y}^{i}{}_{j}

\displaystyle=3\kappa_{i}\mathfrak{y}^{i}{}_{j}\,,

(4.79c)

where
\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}
is the Weyl connection associated to
\kappa
with respect to
\mathfrak{h}
,
\widehat{\mathfrak{r}}^{i}{}_{jkl}
and
\widehat{\mathfrak{l}}_{ij}
are respectively its Riemann and Schouten tensors,
\mathfrak{y}_{ij}
is the Cotton-York tensor of
\mathfrak{h}
,
\epsilon_{ijk}
is the volume form. Equation (
4.79a
) is just the decomposition of the Riemann tensor (
2.22
), equation (
4.79b
) comes from the definitions of the Cotton tensor (
2.23
) and the Cotton-York tensor (
2.48
), equation (
4.79c
) is the divergence equation on the Cotton-York tensor. The gauge invariances of this system are associated to isometries and Weyl connection changes.

Similarly to
Section

4.2.1
, one can introduce a set of zero quantities encoding this auxiliary system. For any 3-dimensional oriented smooth Lorentzian manifold
(\mathscr{I},\mathfrak{h})
, any smooth covector field
\kappa
on
\mathscr{I}
, any connection
\acute{\mathfrak{D}}
on
\mathscr{I}
, any smooth 2-tensor field
\mathfrak{U}_{ij}
on
\mathscr{I}
and any smooth frame field
(e_{\bf i})
on
\mathscr{I}
, define

\displaystyle\varphi_{\bf i}

\displaystyle:=\mathfrak{h}^{\bf jk}\left(\acute{\mathfrak{D}}_{\bf j}\mathfrak{y}_{\bf ki}-\kappa_{\bf j}\mathfrak{y}_{\bf ki}\right)\,,

\displaystyle\varpi^{\bf i}{}_{\bf jkl}

\displaystyle:=\acute{\mathfrak{r}}^{\bf i}{}_{\bf jkl}-2S_{\bf j[k}{}^{\bf im}\mathfrak{U}_{\bf l]m}\,,

\displaystyle\Pi_{\bf ij}

\displaystyle:=\acute{\mathfrak{D}}_{\bf i}\kappa_{\bf j}-\acute{\mathfrak{D}}_{\bf j}\kappa_{\bf i}+2\mathfrak{U}_{\bf[ij]}\,,

\displaystyle\varrho_{\bf ijk}

\displaystyle:=\acute{\mathfrak{D}}_{\bf i}\mathfrak{U}_{\bf jk}-\acute{\mathfrak{D}}_{\bf j}\mathfrak{U}_{\bf ik}+\epsilon_{\bf ij}{}^{\bf l}\mathfrak{y}_{\bf lk}\,,

\displaystyle\acute{\Sigma}_{\bf i}{}^{\bf k}{}_{\bf j}

\displaystyle:=\acute{\mathfrak{G}}_{\bf i}{}^{\bf k}{}_{\bf j}-\acute{\mathfrak{G}}_{\bf j}{}^{\bf k}{}_{\bf i}-\langle\omega^{\bf k},[e_{\bf i},e_{\bf j}]\rangle\,,

\displaystyle\acute{\vartheta}_{\bf ijk}

\displaystyle:=\acute{\mathfrak{D}}_{\bf i}\mathfrak{h}_{\bf jk}+2\kappa_{\bf i}\mathfrak{h}_{\bf jk}\,,

where
(\omega^{\bf i})
is the dual frame field of
(e_{\bf i})
,
\acute{\mathfrak{r}}^{\bf i}{}_{\bf jkl}
are the frame components of the Riemann tensor of
\acute{\mathfrak{D}}
and

\acute{\mathfrak{G}}_{\bf i}{}^{\bf k}{}_{\bf j}:=\langle\omega^{\bf k},\acute{\mathfrak{D}}_{e_{\bf i}}e_{\bf j}\rangle\,.

A gauge choice for the auxiliary system consists of choosing a Weyl connection
\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}
, an orthonormal frame field
(e_{\bf i})
and a coordinate system
(y^{i})
. Let
(\mathscr{I},\mathfrak{h},\kappa)
be a solution to this geometric auxiliary system and assume that it admits a compact spacelike hypersurface
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
. Similarly to
Section

4.2.2
, one can construct a gauge choice
(\widecheck{\mathfrak{D}},(e_{\bf i}),(y^{i})))
on a neighbourhood
\mathfrak{U}
of a point
p\in\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
based on a congruence of timelike conformal geodesics such that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}_{\star}\cap\mathfrak{U}

\displaystyle=\{q\in\mathfrak{U}\,|\,y^{0}(q)=0\}\,,

\displaystyle e_{\bf A}{}^{0}

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{on }\mathfrak{U}_{\star}\,,

\displaystyle e_{\bf 0}{}^{i}

\displaystyle=\delta_{\bf 0}{}^{i}\,,

\displaystyle\widecheck{\mathfrak{G}}_{\bf 0}{}^{\bf j}{}_{\bf i}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\widecheck{\mathfrak{l}}_{\bf 0i}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle(\widecheck{\kappa}_{\mathfrak{h}})_{\bf 0}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}_{\bf ij}

\displaystyle=\eta_{\bf ij}\,,

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\bf 012}

\displaystyle=-1\,.

In this gauge, one obtains a suitable quasilinear hyperbolic system of partial differential equation for the unknown

\underline{\mathfrak{u}}:=\left(\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{y}_{\bf 00},\mathfrak{y}_{\bf 0A},\acute{\mathfrak{G}}_{\bf A}{}^{\bf C}{}_{\bf B},\acute{\mathfrak{G}}_{\bf A}{}^{\bf B}{}_{\bf 0},\acute{\mathfrak{G}}_{\bf A}{}^{\bf 0}{}_{\bf B},\acute{\mathfrak{G}}_{\bf A}{}^{\bf 0}{}_{\bf 0},\kappa_{\bf A},\mathfrak{U}_{\bf AB},\mathfrak{U}_{\bf A0},e_{\bf A}{}^{i}\right)

from a certain part of the zero quantities. This system will be called the auxiliary evolution system. The initial data
\underline{\mathfrak{u}}_{\star}
for the auxiliary evolution system is derived by picking the adequate components of the restriction on
\mathcal{B}_{\star}
of the initial data
\underline{u}_{\star}
for the evolution system (
4.42
). By design of both

\bullet

the initial data
\underline{\mathfrak{u}}_{\star}
,

\bullet

the above gauge and the one for the (CVE), see
4.27
,

and by application of
4.18
, one deduces that the vector fields
e_{\bf 0}
,
e_{\bf 1}
,
e_{\bf 2}
of the two systems coincide (on
\mathscr{I}=\mathcal{W}\cap\mathcal{B}
). Hence
\mathfrak{y}^{\dagger}_{\bf AB}
in the above gauge is also given by
q^{\prime\prime}_{\bf AB}
expressed in the gauge for the (CVE) which is entirely determined by the analytic boundary data
d_{1}
,
d_{2}
.

Remark
.

Note that the boundary data for the evolution system (
4.42
) and its derivatives now play the role of source terms in the auxiliary evolution system.
∎

After solving the auxiliary evolution system, one can prove the propagation of the constraints, which are encoded by the rest of the zero quantities. Finally, one reconstructs the metric
\breve{\mathfrak{h}}
by
\breve{\mathfrak{h}}_{\bf ij}:=\eta_{\bf ij}\,\omega^{\bf i}\otimes\omega^{\bf j}
. ∎

4.3.5
The geometric homogeneous Robin boundary conditions

Now consider the reflective boundary conditions (
4.76
) with
\theta^{\prime}\neq\pi/2
and
q^{\prime\prime}_{\bf AB}=0
, that is

\mathfrak{t}^{\dagger}_{\bf AB}=\mu\,\mathfrak{y}^{\dagger}_{\bf AB}\,,

(4.81)

where
\mu:=\tan(\theta^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}
. Their corresponding analytic form is given by (
4.50
) with

B=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\chi&\sin\chi\\
\sin\chi&-\cos\chi\end{pmatrix}\in M_{2}(\mathbb{R})\,\qquad d_{1}=d_{2}=0\,,

(4.82)

for some
\chi\in(0,2\pi)
related to
\mu
by an invertible relation. From (
4.81
), which is gauge-dependent, we want to obtain our geometric reflective boundary conditions

\mathfrak{p}_{ij}:=\mathfrak{t}_{ij}-\mu\,\mathfrak{y}_{ij}=0\,.

(
1.1
)

Since (
4.81
) rewrite under the form

\mathfrak{p}^{\dagger}_{\bf AB}=0\,,

it remains to study the following quantities

\mathfrak{p}_{\bf A}:=\mathfrak{p}_{\bf 0A}=\mathfrak{t}_{\bf 0A}-\mu\,\mathfrak{y}_{\bf 0A}\,,\qquad\mathfrak{p}:=\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{p}_{\bf 00}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathfrak{t}_{\bf 00}-\mu\,\mathfrak{y}_{\bf 00}\right)\,.

To this end, we will also use an auxiliary evolution system on the conformal boundary. Recall that both the boundary stress-energy tensor
\mathfrak{t}
and the Cotton-York tensor
\mathfrak{y}
are divergence-free for the Levi-Civita connection
\mathfrak{D}
of any representative
\mathfrak{h}
of the boundary conformal class
[\mathfrak{h}]
. Thus

\mathfrak{D}_{i}\mathfrak{p}^{i}{}_{j}=0\,.

More generally, for any Weyl connection
\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}
and any representative
\mathfrak{h}\in[\mathfrak{h}]
, one has

\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i}\mathfrak{p}^{i}{}_{j}-3(\widehat{\kappa}_{\mathfrak{h}})_{i}\mathfrak{p}^{i}{}_{j}=0\,,

(4.83)

where
\widehat{\kappa}_{\mathfrak{h}}
is the covector field associated to
\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}
with respect to
\mathfrak{h}
.

Lemma 4.44
.

Consider the analytic boundary conditions (
4.50
) with the choices (
4.82
). Let
\underline{u}_{\star}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\overline{\mathcal{U}_{\star}},\mathbb{R}^{m})
be some analytic initial data satisfying all the conditions of
4.34
. The quantities
\mathfrak{p}
and
\mathfrak{p}_{\bf A}
, associated to the solution
(\mathcal{W},\breve{g},V,\Theta,\kappa)
and the gauge choice
(\Theta,\acute{\nabla},(e_{\bf a}),(y^{\mu}))
given by
4.35
, verify on the conformal boundary
\mathscr{I}=\mathcal{W}\cap\mathcal{B}
the following evolution system

\displaystyle\quad 2e_{\bf 0}(\mathfrak{p})-\delta^{\bf AB}e_{\bf A}(\mathfrak{p}_{\bf B})+3\acute{\mathfrak{G}}_{\bf A}{}^{\bf A}{}_{\bf 0}\,\mathfrak{p}-\left(\acute{\mathfrak{G}}_{\bf A}{}^{\bf A}{}_{\bf B}-4\kappa_{\bf B}\right)\delta^{\bf BC}\,\mathfrak{p}_{\bf C}

\displaystyle=-\acute{\mathfrak{G}}_{\bf A}{}^{\bf B}{}_{\bf 0}\delta^{\bf AC}\mathfrak{p}^{\dagger}_{\bf BC}\,,

(4.84a)

\displaystyle\quad e_{\bf 0}(\mathfrak{p}_{\bf A})-e_{\bf A}(\mathfrak{p})+3\kappa_{\bf A}\,\mathfrak{p}+\left(\acute{\mathfrak{G}}_{\bf C}{}^{\bf C}{}_{\bf 0}\delta_{\bf AB}+\acute{\mathfrak{G}}_{\bf B}{}^{\bf 0}{}_{\bf A}\right)\delta^{\bf BC}\mathfrak{p}_{\bf C}

\displaystyle=\delta^{\bf BC}e_{\bf B}(\mathfrak{p}^{\dagger}_{\bf AC})-\left(\acute{\mathfrak{G}}_{\bf B}{}^{\bf C}{}_{\bf A}-\acute{\mathfrak{G}}_{\bf E}{}^{\bf E}{}_{\bf B}\delta_{\bf A}{}^{\bf C}\right)\delta^{\bf BD}\mathfrak{p}^{\dagger}_{\bf CD}\,,

(4.84b)

where
\breve{\mathfrak{h}}
is the metric induced by
\breve{g}
on
\mathscr{I}
and
\acute{\mathfrak{G}}_{\bf i}{}^{\bf j}{}_{\bf k}
are the connection coefficients of the Weyl connection
\acute{\mathfrak{D}}
, induced by
\acute{\nabla}
on
\mathscr{I}
, in the frame field
(e_{\bf 0},e_{\bf 1},e_{\bf 2})
.

Proof.

The system (
4.84
) is just an expression of (
4.83
) using the gauge properties (
4.32
)-(
4.33
) and the decomposition

\mathfrak{p}_{\bf AB}=\mathfrak{p}^{\dagger}_{\bf AB}+\mathfrak{p}\,\delta_{\bf AB}\,.\qed

The analogue of
4.43
in this case is the following proposition.

Proposition 4.45
.

Consider the analytic boundary conditions (
4.50
) with the choices (
4.82
). Let
\underline{u}_{\star}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\overline{\mathcal{U}_{\star}},\mathbb{R}^{m})
be some analytic initial data satisfying all the conditions of
4.34
.

i)

If the 4-dimensional aAdS space
(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}\cap\mathcal{B},\Theta^{-2}\breve{g})
of
4.35
verifies the geometric boundary condition (
1.1
), then
\underline{u}_{\star}
satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{2}{3}E_{33}-\mu H_{33}

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{on }\mathcal{B}_{\star}\,,

(4.85a)

\displaystyle\frac{2}{3}H_{3A}+\mu E_{3A}

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{on }\mathcal{B}_{\star}\,,

(4.85b)

where the indices refer to the coordinates
(y^{1},y^{2},y^{3})
on
\mathcal{U}_{\star}
.

ii)

Conversely, if
\underline{u}_{\star}
satisfy (
4.85
) then the 4-dimensional aAdS space
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{X}\cap\mathcal{B},\Theta^{-2}\breve{g})
, where
\mathcal{X}
is a neighbourhood of
\mathcal{U}_{\star}
in
\mathcal{W}
, verifies the geometric boundary condition (
1.1
).

Remarks
.

•

The conditions (
4.85
) will be interpreted as geometric compatibility conditions in
4.47
.

•

The restriction to boundary conditions with constant coefficients does not, in fact, restrict the generality of (
1.1
) since it appears to be impossible to propagate it for a non-constant
\mu
. In that regard, see also the remark following
3.19
. ∎

Proof.

i)

If the geometric boundary condition (
1.1
) holds then
\mathfrak{p}=0
,
\mathfrak{p}_{\bf A}=0
on
\mathscr{I}
. Moreover,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{t}_{\bf 00}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{3}\mathfrak{E}_{\bf 00}=-\frac{2}{3}V^{\bf 3}{}_{\bf 030}=\frac{2}{3}E_{\bf 33}\,,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{y}_{\bf 00}

\displaystyle=-\mathfrak{H}_{\bf 00}=-(\star V)^{\bf 3}{}_{\bf 030}=H_{\bf 33}\,,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{t}_{\bf 0A}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{3}\mathfrak{E}_{\bf 0A}=-\frac{2}{3}V^{\bf 3}{}_{\bf 03A}=-\frac{1}{3}\not{\epsilon}_{\bf A}{}^{\bf B}H_{\bf 3B}\,,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{y}_{\bf 0A}

\displaystyle=-\mathfrak{H}_{\bf 0A}=-(\star V)^{\bf 3}{}_{\bf 03A}=\frac{1}{2}\not{\epsilon}_{\bf A}{}^{\bf B}E_{\bf 3B}\,.

One deduces the result using the properties of the frame field
(e_{\bf a})
.

ii)

Let us prove that
\mathfrak{p}
and
\mathfrak{p}_{\bf A}
vanish identically on a neighbourhood of
\mathcal{B}_{\star}
in
\mathscr{I}
. Note that (
4.84
) forms a homogeneous linear symmetric hyperbolic system on
(\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{p}_{\bf A})
. Indeed, for the following ordering of the unknowns
(\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{p}_{\bf 1},\mathfrak{p}_{\bf 2})
, the matrices with respect to the coordinates
(y^{i})=(y^{0},y^{1},y^{2})
are given by

D^{0}=D^{\bf 0}+e_{\bf 1}{}^{0}D^{\bf 1}+e_{\bf 2}{}^{0}D^{\bf 2}\,,\quad D^{A}=e_{\bf 1}{}^{A}D^{\bf 1}+e_{\bf 2}{}^{A}D^{\bf 2}\,,

where

D^{\bf 0}=\begin{pmatrix}2&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}\,,\qquad D^{\bf 1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1&0\\
-1&0&0\\
0&0&0\end{pmatrix}\,,\qquad D^{\bf 2}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&-1\\
0&0&0\\
-1&0&0\end{pmatrix}

Consequently, there exists a neighbourhood
\mathfrak{X}
of
\mathcal{B}_{\star}
in
\mathscr{I}
such that these quantities vanish identically on
\mathfrak{X}
if and only if they vanish initially on
\mathcal{B}_{\star}
, which is equivalent to (
4.85
). It suffices to choose a neighbourhood
\mathcal{X}
of
\mathcal{U}_{\star}
in
\mathcal{W}
such that
\mathcal{X}\cap\mathcal{B}=\mathfrak{X}
to obtain the result. ∎

4.4
The geometric initial data problem

Having established the geometric interpretation of certain analytic boundary conditions in
Section

4.3
, let us now discuss the geometric initial data. In
Section

4.4.1
, the analytic initial data and the conditions imposed on it are interpreted geometrically. This also shows how to derive the analytic initial data from the geometric one. A precise formulation of the geometric initial data problem is given in
Section

4.4.2
.

The next sections are devoted to the (partial) resolution of the geometric initial data problem. In
Section

4.4.3
, we cite some results using an adapted version of the conformal method.
Section

4.4.4
is dedicated to the study of the unphysical fields. In particular, we derive a novel result, see
4.51
and
4.52
, which gives necessary and sufficient conditions for the smoothness of the unphysical fields, which is part of the geometric initial data problem. This extends a previous result by Kánnár
[
62
]
.

4.4.1
Analytic and geometric initial data

In this section, we will use the notations of
4.34
. Recall that the 4-dimensional geometry was (re)introduced by
4.35
. The following lemma analyses all the conditions, except the compatibility conditions, imposed on the analytic initial data.

Lemma 4.46
.

Let
\underline{u}_{\star}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\overline{\mathcal{U}}_{\star},\mathbb{R}^{m})
be some initial data verifying all the conditions of
4.34
. Denote by
\breve{h}
the metric induced by
\breve{g}
on
\mathcal{U}_{\star}
. Then

i)

(e_{\bf i})
is a frame field on
\mathcal{U}_{\star}
such that, on
\mathcal{B}_{\star}
,
e_{\bf 1}
and
e_{\bf 2}
are tangent while
e_{\bf 3}
is the outward-pointing unit normal with respect to
\breve{h}
,

ii)

one has the following usual relations on the connection coefficients, see
2.58
and
2.57
,

\acute{\Gamma}_{\bf i}{}^{\bf 0}{}_{\bf 0}=\kappa_{\bf i}\,,\quad\acute{\Gamma}_{\bf i}{}^{\bf k}{}_{\bf 0}\delta_{\bf kj}=\acute{\Gamma}_{\bf i}{}^{\bf 0}{}_{\bf j}\,,\quad\acute{\Gamma}_{\bf i}{}^{\bf 0}{}_{\bf j}=\acute{\Gamma}_{\bf j}{}^{\bf 0}{}_{\bf i}\,,

Define the 10-tuple
(\mathcal{U}_{\star},\breve{h},E,\star H,\Theta_{\star},\kappa,K,0,T,Q)\in\mathscr{D}
where the fields
E
,
H
and
\kappa
are the ones defined in
4.34
and

K:=\acute{\Gamma}_{\bf i}{}^{\bf 0}{}_{\bf j}\,\omega^{\bf i}\otimes\omega^{\bf j}\,,\quad T:=U_{\bf i0}\,\omega^{\bf i}\,,\quad Q:=U_{\bf ij}\,\omega^{\bf i}\otimes\omega^{\bf j}\,,

(\omega^{\bf i})
denoting the dual frame field of
(e_{\bf i})
. Then

iii)

s_{\star}
is the field
\widehat{\sigma}
, defined by (
4.10c
), of this 10-tuple,

iv)

this 10-tuple satisfy the (
4.9
) with
\Lambda=-3
and

a)

the gauge-independent conditions

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{on }\not{\mathscr{I}}\,,

(4.86a)

\displaystyle K_{\perp i}+\varepsilon\frac{z}{\Psi}h_{\perp i}

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{on }\not{\mathscr{I}}\,,

(4.86b)

where a
\perp
index denotes the normal direction to
\not{\mathscr{I}}
,

b)

the gauge choices

\displaystyle\Psi

\displaystyle\geq 0\quad\text{on }\mathcal{S}\,,

(4.87a)

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{on }\mathcal{S}\setminus\not{\mathscr{I}}\,,

(4.87b)

\displaystyle\widehat{s}_{h}

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{on }\not{\mathscr{I}}\,.

(4.87c)

Remarks
.

•

Some of the conclusions are evident from the 4-dimensional geometry. However, it is useful to derive them solely from the analytic conditions to be sure to interpret all of these conditions.

•

The gauge choices (
4.87
) for the (CVC) are the transcription of the gauge choices (
4.34
) for the (CVE) made at the level of initial data in the gauge construction. Moreover, the condition (
4.86a
) corresponds to (
4.31b
). Note that the gauge choice (
4.87a
) (respectively (
4.87b
)) is only possible if
\not{\mathscr{I}}\subset\partial\mathcal{S}
(respectively (
4.86a
) holds).

•

Thanks to (
4.19
), the condition (
4.86a
) implies that
(\mathcal{U}_{\star},\mathcal{B}_{\star},\Theta_{\star}^{-2}\breve{h})
is asymptotically hyperbolic. ∎

Proof.

i)

The conditions (
4.46
) and (
4.56
) correspond to
(e_{\bf i})
being a frame field on
\mathcal{U}_{\star}
. Since
(e_{\bf a})
is
\breve{g}
-orthonormal, the conditions
e_{\bf A}{}^{3}=0
and
e_{\bf 3}{}^{3}<0
on
\mathcal{B}_{\star}
in (

) are equivalent to impose that, on
\mathcal{B}_{\star}
,
e_{\bf 1}
and
e_{\bf 2}
are tangent and
e_{\bf 3}
is the outward-pointing unit normal with respect to
\breve{h}
.

ii)

The conditions
\acute{\vartheta}_{\bf i0a}=0
and
\acute{\Sigma}_{\bf i}{}^{\bf 0}{}_{\bf j}=0
on
\mathcal{U}_{\star}
in (
4.57
) are equivalent to the relations on the connection coefficients.

iii)-iv)

The rest of the conditions in (
4.57
), that is to know

\displaystyle\varkappa_{\bf ia}

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{on }\mathcal{U}_{\star}\,,

\displaystyle\qquad\mho_{\bf i}

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{on }\mathcal{U}_{\star}\,,

\displaystyle\qquad\varsigma_{\bf i}

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{on }\mathcal{U}_{\star}\,,

\displaystyle\acute{\vartheta}_{\bf ijk}

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{on }\mathcal{U}_{\star}\,,

\displaystyle\qquad\aleph

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{on }\mathcal{U}_{\star}\,,

\displaystyle\qquad\varpi^{\bf a}{}_{\bf bij}

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{on }\mathcal{U}_{\star}\,,

\displaystyle\Pi_{\bf ij}

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{on }\mathcal{U}_{\star}\,,

\displaystyle\qquad\varrho_{\bf ija}

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{on }\mathcal{U}_{\star}\,,

\displaystyle\qquad\acute{\Sigma}_{\bf i}{}^{\bf k}{}_{\bf j}

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{on }\mathcal{U}_{\star}\,,

\displaystyle\varphi_{\bf ia0}

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{on }\mathcal{U}_{\star}\,,

are just the constraints implied by the (CVE) on
\mathcal{U}_{\star}
. This is equivalent to imposing that the 10-tuple defined in the lemma satisfies the (CVC) with
\Lambda=-3
and that
s_{\star}
is equal to the field
\widehat{\sigma}
of this 10-tuple.

Since
\Theta_{\star}
is a boundary defining function of
\mathcal{B}_{\star}
, (
4.87a
) is verified and
\not{\mathscr{I}}=\mathcal{B}_{\star}
. The fact that
z=0
splits into (
4.86a
) and (
4.87a
). Since
f_{\star}
is bounded from above, the field
\widehat{\sigma}
associated to the 10-tuple vanishes on
\not{\mathscr{I}}
. With (
4.86a
) and (
4.10c
), one deduces (
4.87c
).

Finally, let us look at the remaining conditions in (
4.51
). By definition of
K_{ij}
and
ii)
, the conditions
\acute{\Gamma}_{\bf A}{}^{\bf 3}{}_{\bf 0}=0
and
\acute{\Gamma}_{\bf 3}{}^{\bf 3}{}_{\bf 0}=0
on
\mathcal{B}_{\star}
are equivalent to
K_{\bf 3A}=0
and
K_{\bf 33}=0
on
\mathcal{B}_{\star}
. Thanks to
i)
, this is the condition (
4.86b
) expressed with the gauge choices (
4.87
). The condition
\zeta_{\bf A}=0
on
\mathcal{B}_{\star}
is automatically satisfied by
i)
and the fact that
\Theta_{\star}
is a boundary defining function of
\mathcal{B}_{\star}
. The conditions
U_{\bf A3}=0
and
U_{\bf 33}+f_{\star}=
on
\mathcal{B}_{\star}
are ensured by (
CVC.10
) and
iii)
. ∎

Let us now turn to the analytic compatibility conditions, see
iii)
of
4.32
, in the case where
\mathcal{B}_{\star}\neq\varnothing
. We will only detail the geometric compatibility conditions for our geometric boundary conditions (
1.1
). Then, recall that the analytic initial data also has to satisfy (
4.85
), see
ii)
of
4.45
.

Lemma 4.47
.

The analytic compatibility conditions and the conditions (
4.85
) can be expressed as conditions at
\mathcal{B}_{\star}
on the 10-tuple of
4.46
. These form the
enlarged set of compatibility conditions
for our geometric boundary conditions (
1.1
). Furthermore, the analytic compatibility conditions of order
0
and the conditions (
4.85
) are in fact geometric, meaning that they are left invariant by the gauge transformations of the (CVC) described in
4.12
.

Remarks
.

•

One can expect the higher order compatibility conditions to be also geometric although we do not address this problem in this article.

•

The conditions (
4.85
) are part of the geometric compatibility conditions of order
0
. These do not imply higher order compatibility conditions. Indeed, if the analytic boundary condition and the enlarged set of compatibility conditions hold then the time derivatives of all order of
\mathfrak{p}
and
\mathfrak{p}_{\bf A}
vanish on
\mathcal{B}_{\star}
thanks to (
4.84
). ∎

Proof.

The analytic compatibility conditions of order 0 are simply the analytic boundary conditions

0=\mathfrak{p}^{\dagger}_{\bf AB}:=\mathfrak{t}^{\dagger}_{\bf AB}-\mu\mathfrak{y}^{\dagger}_{\bf AB}=\frac{2}{3}E_{\bf AB}^{\dagger}-\mu H^{\dagger}_{\bf AB}\quad\text{on }\mathscr{I}\,.

These are conditions at
\mathcal{B}_{\star}
on the tensors
E_{ij}
and
M_{ijk}:=(\star H)_{ijk}
of the 10-tuple defined in
4.46
. Moreover, they are gauge-independent for the gauge transformations described in
4.12
. Similarly, the conditions
\mathfrak{p}=0
,
\mathfrak{p}_{\bf A}=0
are also gauge-independent conditions on the 10-tuple at
\mathcal{B}_{\star}
.

The higher order analytic compatibility conditions are obtained by combining the evolution equations and the analytic boundary conditions, see the last remark below
4.32
. Thanks to the gauge properties (
4.32
) and (
4.33
), all the 4-dimensional fields can be expressed with fields in the 10-tuple.
∎

4.4.2
Formulation of the geometric initial data problem

In light of
Section

4.4.1
as well as
4.21
, the geometric initial data problem is formulated as follows: find a smooth Riemannian metric
\widetilde{h}_{ij}
and a smooth symmetric 2-tensor
\widetilde{K}_{ij}
on the interior
\mathring{\mathcal{S}}
of a compact 3-dimensional smooth manifold
\mathcal{S}
with non-empty boundary
\partial\mathcal{S}
such that

i)

(\mathcal{S},\partial\mathcal{S},\widetilde{h}_{ij})
is an asymptotically hyperbolic space, in the sense of
3.2
, with smooth rescaled metrics,

ii)

(\mathring{\mathcal{S}},\widetilde{h}_{ij},\widetilde{K}_{ij})
is a solution to the (
4.21
) with
\Lambda=-3
,

iii)

for any boundary defining function
\Psi
of
\partial\mathcal{S}
, any smooth covector field
\kappa
on
\mathcal{S}
and any smooth scalar field
z
on
\mathcal{S}
which vanishes on
\partial\mathcal{S}
,

a)

the field
P_{ij}:=\Psi\widetilde{K}_{ij}
extends smoothly to
\partial\mathcal{S}
and verifies
P_{\perp i}=0
on
\partial\mathcal{S}
,

b)

the unphysical fields defined by (
4.22b
)-(
4.22e
) extend smoothly to
\partial\mathcal{S}
,

iv)

on each connected component
\mathcal{C}
of
\partial\mathcal{S}
, the corresponding geometric compatibility conditions are verified.

Remarks
.

•

Condition
iii)a)
is equivalent to both the smoothness of the unphysical field
K_{ij}
defined by (
4.22a
) and the geometric condition (
4.86b
). Note that (
4.86a
) is verified by the choice of
z
.

•

The smoothness of the unphysical fields is referred as the ‘asymptotically simple’ hypothesis by Friedrich in
[
38
]
. ∎

In
Section

4.4.3
, we cite certain results of Andersson and Chruściel addressing
i)
-
iii)a)
, when the trace
\widetilde{K}
is constant, by means of an adapted version of the conformal method. Under some hypotheses, Kánnár derived the conditions under which a solution given by the conformal method verifies
iii)b)
. These will be stated in
Section

4.4.4
, prior to the presentation of a new result extending Kánnár’s theorem, see
4.52
. This corollary provides the necessary and sufficient conditions for a solution to
i)
-
ii)
(regardless of the method used to solve this problem) to satisfy
iii)
.

4.4.3
The conformal method

Let us first recall the conformal method to solve the (VC) on a 3-dimensional closed smooth manifold
\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}
. The method consists in decomposing the fields
\widetilde{h}_{ij}
and
\widetilde{K}_{ij}
under the following form

\displaystyle\widetilde{h}_{ij}

\displaystyle=\theta^{4}h_{ij}\,,

(4.88a)

\displaystyle\widetilde{K}_{ij}

\displaystyle=\frac{\widetilde{K}}{3}\theta^{4}h_{ij}+\theta^{4}(\mathcal{K}_{h}X)_{ij}+\theta^{-2}\phi_{ij}\,,

(4.88b)

where

•

\theta
is a smooth positive function on
\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}
,

•

h_{ij}
is a smooth Riemannian metric on
\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}
,

•

\widetilde{K}
is a smooth scalar field on
\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}
,

•

\mathcal{K}_{h}
is the conformal Killing operator of
h
defined by

(\mathcal{K}_{h}X)_{ij}:=D_{i}X_{j}+D_{j}X_{i}-\frac{2}{3}D_{k}X^{k}h_{ij}\,,

•

X^{i}
is a smooth vector field on
\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}
defined up to conformal Killing fields,

•

\phi_{ij}
is a
TT-tensor
, that is a symmetric 2-tensor which is trace-free and divergence-free with respect to the metric
h
,

Note that (
4.88b
) is an orthogonal decomposition of
\widetilde{K}_{ij}
into its pure-trace part, its pure-divergence part and its TT part. The factors in powers of
\theta
, notably the unusual one in front of the conformal Killing operator, are designed so that the decomposition is conformally invariant, see later below. The (VC) are then equivalent to the following coupled elliptic equations on
(\theta,X^{i})

\displaystyle\Delta_{h}\theta-\frac{r}{8}\theta-\frac{1}{12}\theta^{5}\left(\widetilde{K}^{2}-3\Lambda\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{8}\theta^{-3}\left|\theta^{4}\mathcal{K}_{h}X+\theta^{-2}\phi\right|^{2}_{h}\,,

(4.89a)

\displaystyle D^{i}\left(\theta^{6}(\mathcal{K}_{h}X)_{ij}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{3}\theta^{6}(d\widetilde{K})_{j}\,.

(4.89b)

Both the decompositions (
4.88
) and the elliptic system (
4.89
) are left invariant under the following transformation

\left(\theta,h_{ij},\widetilde{K},X^{i},\phi_{ij}\right)\mapsto\left(\omega^{-1/2}\theta,\omega^{2}h_{ij},\widetilde{K},X^{i},\omega^{-1}\phi_{ij}\right)\,,

for any smooth positive function
\omega
. The free data is thus composed of the scalar field
\widetilde{K}
and of the class
[(h,\phi)]
for the same equivalence relation than in
3.11
. An important case is when
\widetilde{K}
is constant: from (
4.89b
) one deduces that
\mathcal{K}_{h}X=0
and it just remains to solve (
4.89a
).

Furthermore, the same operators can be used in order to construct TT-tensors. Indeed, for any symmetric smooth 2-tensor
A_{ij}
, one can define a TT-tensor
\phi_{ij}
under the form

\phi_{ij}:=(\mathcal{K}_{h}X)_{ij}+A_{ij}\,,

where
X^{i}
is the solution, defined up to conformal Killing fields of
h
, of the elliptic equation

D_{i}\left(\mathcal{K}_{h}\right)^{ij}=-D_{i}A^{ij}\,.

Let us now turn to the cases where the initial data set intersects the conformal boundary
\mathscr{I}
. This occurs when
\Lambda<0
and for hyperboloidal initial data sets when
\Lambda=0
. Consider a 3-dimensional compact smooth manifold
\mathcal{S}
with boundary
\partial\mathcal{S}\neq\varnothing
. There are a few problems when trying to apply the conformal method in this context. First, note that if
\Psi:=\theta^{-2}
is a boundary defining function of
\partial\mathcal{S}
then
\theta
blows up on the boundary. Thus equation (
4.89a
) is not suitable for setting a boundary condition. With that in mind, if one chooses the unknown
\Psi
instead then one has to deal with the following singular equation

\Psi\Delta_{h}\Psi+\frac{r}{4}\Psi^{2}-\frac{3}{2}|d\Psi|^{2}_{h}+\frac{1}{6}\left(\widetilde{K}^{2}-3\Lambda\right)=\frac{1}{4}\Psi^{4}\left|\tf_{\widetilde{h}}\widetilde{K}\right|_{h}^{2}\,.

(4.90)

Moreover, in our case, one expects from (
4.22a
) that on any compact neighbourhood of a boundary point
p\in\partial\mathcal{S}
, the field
\widetilde{K}_{ij}
verifies the following asymptotic behaviours

\left(\tf_{\widetilde{h}}\widetilde{K}\right)_{ij}=\mathcal{O}\left({\Psi^{-1}}\right)\,,\qquad\widetilde{K}=-3z+\mathcal{O}\left({\Psi}\right)\,.

(4.91)

Note that the first asymptotic behaviour does not match with the factors in powers of
\theta
in the decomposition (
4.88b
).

These problems require to modify the method. This was done by Andersson, Chruściel and Friedrich
[
5
]
in the case where
\Lambda\leq 0
and
\widetilde{K}_{ij}
is pure trace. In that case, note that the trace
\widetilde{K}
is then constant by the momentum constraint. Their results were later extended by Andersson and Chruściel
[
3
,
4
]
to the case where
\Lambda=0
and
\widetilde{K}
is a non-zero constant. Finally, Kánnár
[
62
]
transposed the theorems to
\widetilde{K}
constant and
\Lambda<\widetilde{K}^{2}/3
. For our purpose, a constant trace
\widetilde{K}
implies that it vanishes due to the geometric condition (
4.86a
). We summarise the relevant
7
7

7

Observe that our case
(\Lambda,\widetilde{K})=(-3,0)
is totally equivalent to
(\Lambda,\widetilde{K})=(0,\pm 3)
.
results in the following theorem.

Theorem 4.48

(Andersson and Chruściel
[
4
, Theorems 3.2, 4.7 and 2.8]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a 3-dimensional compact smooth manifold with non-empty boundary
\partial\mathcal{S}
.

i)

The adapted conformal method: Let
(h_{ij},\phi_{ij})
be a free data pair where

\bullet

h_{ij}
is a smooth Riemannian metric on
\mathcal{S}
,

\bullet

\phi_{ij}
is a smooth TT-tensor on the interior
\mathring{\mathcal{S}}
for the metric
h_{ij}
such that, for any (and thus all) boundary defining function
x
of
\partial\mathcal{S}
,
x^{2}\phi_{ij}
extends smoothly to
\partial\mathcal{S}
and verifies
x^{2}\phi_{\perp i}=0
on
\partial\mathcal{S}
.

Take a boundary defining function
x
of
\partial\mathcal{S}
and define the smooth Riemannian metric
\overline{h}_{ij}:=x^{-2}h_{ij}
on the interior
\mathring{\mathcal{S}}
. Then there exists a unique uniformly bounded, uniformly bounded away from zero, locally
\mathcal{C}^{2}
function
\theta
solution to

\displaystyle\Delta_{\overline{h}}\theta-\frac{\overline{r}}{8}\theta-\frac{3}{4}\theta^{5}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{8}\theta^{-7}\left|x^{3}\phi\right|_{h}^{2}\quad

\displaystyle\text{on }\mathring{\mathcal{S}}\,,

(4.92a)

\displaystyle\theta

\displaystyle=\left|dx\right|^{1/2}_{h}

\displaystyle\text{on }\partial\mathcal{S}\,.

(4.92b)

Even though
\Psi:=x\theta^{-2}
is polyhomogeneous and of class
\mathcal{C}^{3}
on
\mathcal{S}
for generic free data pairs, there exists large sets of non-generic free data pairs for which it is smooth up to the boundary. In that case, the fields

\displaystyle\widetilde{h}_{ij}

\displaystyle:=\theta^{4}\,\overline{h}_{ij}=\Psi^{-2}h_{ij}\,,

(4.93a)

\displaystyle\widetilde{K}_{ij}

\displaystyle:=\theta^{-2}\left(x\phi_{ij}\right)=\Psi\phi_{ij}\,,

(4.93b)

satisfy the conditions
i)
-
iii)a)
of the geometric initial data problem as defined in
Section

4.4.2
.

ii)

Construction of TT-tensors: Let
h_{ij}
be a smooth Riemannian metric on
\mathcal{S}
and let
x
a boundary defining function of
\partial\mathcal{S}
. For any smooth symmetric 2-tensor
A_{ij}
on
\mathcal{S}
such that

h^{ij}A_{ij}=0\quad\text{on }\mathcal{S}\,,\qquad A_{\perp i}=0\quad\text{on }\partial\mathcal{S}\,,

there exists a polyhomogeneous vector field
X^{i}
on
\mathcal{S}
solution to

D_{i}\left(\mathcal{K}_{h}X\right)^{ij}=-D_{i}\left(x^{-2}A^{ij}\right)\quad\text{on }\mathcal{S}\,.

The vector field
X^{i}
is unique up to smooth solutions of the homogeneous equation. Then the field

\phi_{ij}:=x^{-2}A_{ij}+\left(\mathcal{K}_{h}X\right)_{ij}

is a TT-tensor on
\mathring{\mathcal{S}}
for the metric
h_{ij}
such that
x^{2}\phi_{ij}
is polyhomogeneous, of class
\mathcal{C}^{1}
on
\mathcal{S}
and verifies
x^{2}\phi_{\perp i}=0
on
\partial\mathcal{S}
. Moreover, if
X^{i}
is smooth on
\mathcal{S}
then so is
x^{2}\phi_{ij}
. In particular, this is the case if
x^{-2}A_{ij}
is smooth on
\mathcal{S}
.

4.4.4
On the unphysical fields

Let us now focus on the condition
iii)
of the geometric initial data problem defined in
Section

4.4.2
. For fields arising from the conformal method as described in
4.48
, the following theorem ensures that
iii)
holds under some hypotheses.

Theorem 4.49

(Kánnár
[
62
, Theorem 2]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a (3-dimensional compact) smooth manifold (with non-empty boundary) diffeomorphic to the closed unit ball of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Let
h_{ij}
be a smooth Riemannian metric on
\mathcal{S}
and let
S_{ij}
be a smooth trace-free symmetric 2-tensor on
\mathcal{S}
. Take
x
a boundary defining function of
\partial\mathcal{S}
such that

•

\left|dx\right|_{h}=1
in a neighbourhood of
\partial\mathcal{S}
,

•

\overline{r}=-6+\mathcal{O}\left({x^{3}}\right)
where
\overline{r}
is the scalar curvature of
\overline{h}_{ij}:=x^{-2}h_{ij}
.

If the restriction of
S_{ij}
in the tangential directions to
\partial\mathcal{S}
is pure-trace, that is

S^{\dagger}_{AB}=0\quad\text{on }\partial\mathcal{S}\,,

(4.94)

and if
\partial\mathcal{S}
is totally geodesic for the Levi-Civita connection
D
of
h
, that is

\not{\mathfrak{K}}_{AB}=0\,,

(4.95)

then

i)

the TT-tensor
\phi_{ij}
, constructed by
ii)
of
4.48
out of
h_{ij}
and
x
and
A_{ij}:=xS_{ij}
, is such that
x\phi_{ij}
is smooth on
\mathcal{S}
,

ii)

the conformal factor
\Psi:=x\theta^{-2}
, constructed by
i)
of
4.48
out of
(h_{ij},\phi_{ij})
and
x
, is smooth on
\mathcal{S}
,

iii)

the consequent fields
\widetilde{h}_{ij}
,
\widetilde{K}_{ij}
defined by (
4.93
) satisfy
iii)b)
of the geometric initial data problem, see
Section

4.4.2
, in addition of
i)
-
iii)a)
already ensured by
4.48
.

Remark
.

The choice of
x
in
4.49
is more restrictive than the gauge in the original version of the theorem
[
62
, Theorem 2]
. However, this is actually the choice used in Kánnár’s proof. This makes the comparison with
4.51
easier.
∎

4.49
is quite restrictive for two reasons: the topology of the manifold is completely imposed and the field
\widetilde{K}_{ij}
is more regular than expected since it is smooth up to the boundary. In what follows, we derive the necessary and sufficient conditions in the general case for a solution to
i)
-
ii)
of the geometric initial data problem to satisfy
iii)
. Let us start with a technical lemma.

Lemma 4.50
.

Let
(\mathcal{S},\not{\mathfrak{I}},\widetilde{h})
be a 3-dimensional asymptotically hyperbolic space with smooth rescaled metrics and let
\widetilde{K}_{ij}
be a smooth symmetric 2-tensor field on
\mathcal{S}\setminus\not{\mathfrak{I}}
verifying the momentum constraint

\widetilde{D}_{i}\widetilde{K}^{i}{}_{j}-\widetilde{D}_{j}\widetilde{K}^{i}{}_{i}=0\quad\text{on }\mathcal{S}\setminus\not{\mathfrak{I}}\,.

For any boundary defining function
\Psi
of
\not{\mathfrak{I}}
, if the field
P_{ij}:=\Psi\widetilde{K}_{ij}
extends smoothly to
\not{\mathfrak{I}}
then it verifies

\displaystyle\Psi^{-1}P_{A\perp}

\displaystyle=\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{A}P_{B}{}^{B}-\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{B}P^{B}{}_{A}

\displaystyle\quad\text{on }\not{\mathfrak{I}}\,,

(4.96a)

\displaystyle\Psi^{-1}P_{\perp\perp}

\displaystyle=e_{\perp}\left(P\right)-\not{\mathfrak{K}}^{AB}P_{AB}

\displaystyle\quad\text{on }\not{\mathfrak{I}}\,,

(4.96b)

where

\bullet

\not{\mathfrak{h}}
is the metric induced on (a connected component of)
\not{\mathfrak{I}}
by the rescaled metric
h:=\Psi^{2}\widetilde{h}
,
\not{\mathfrak{D}}
is the Levi-Civita connection of
\not{\mathfrak{h}}
,
\not{\mathfrak{K}}
is the extrinsic curvature on (a connected component of)
\not{\mathfrak{I}}
of the Levi-Civita connection
D
of
h
,

\bullet

\perp
denotes the outward-pointing normal of
\not{\mathfrak{I}}
with respect to
h
while
A,B
are tangential indices,

\bullet

P:=h^{ij}P_{ij}
is the trace of
P_{ij}
with respect to
h
.

Remarks
.

•

If
P_{ij}:=\Psi\widetilde{K}_{ij}
extends smoothly to
\not{\mathfrak{I}}
then the trace
\widetilde{K}:=\widetilde{h}^{ij}\widetilde{K}_{ij}
can be smoothly rescaled by
\Psi^{-1}
. Indeed, one has
\Psi^{-1}\widetilde{K}=P\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{S},\mathbb{R})
.

•

Equations (
4.96a
)-(
4.96b
) imply that
P_{i\perp}=0
on
\not{\mathfrak{I}}
. Since
P_{ij}
is smooth up to the conformal boundary and by application of
2.11
,
\Psi^{-1}P_{i\perp}
are well defined on
\not{\mathfrak{I}}
, their value being given by the equations. ∎

Proof.

By definition of
P_{ij}
and
2.31
, one has

0=\widetilde{D}_{i}\widetilde{K}^{i}{}_{j}-\widetilde{D}_{j}\widetilde{K}^{i}{}_{i}=\Psi\left(D_{i}P^{i}{}_{j}-D_{j}P\right)-2P_{jk}h^{kl}(d\Psi)_{l}\quad\text{on }\mathcal{S}\setminus\not{\mathfrak{I}}\,.

Since
P_{ij}
and
h_{ij}
are smooth on the whole of
\mathcal{S}
, this equation extends up to
\not{\mathfrak{I}}
. Take a frame field
(e_{\bf i})=(e_{\perp},(e_{\bf A}))
adapted to the conformal boundary
\not{\mathfrak{I}}
(see
Section

2.6.1
) with
e_{\perp}
the outward-pointing
h
-unit normal. Evaluating the above equation on
\not{\mathfrak{I}}
yields

P_{\bf i\perp}=0\quad\text{on }\not{\mathfrak{I}}\,.

(4.97)

Now, one can first divide the equation by
\Psi
before evaluating on
\not{\mathfrak{I}}
. This gives

2\Psi^{-1}P_{\bf j\perp}=e_{\bf j}(P)-D_{\bf i}P^{\bf i}{}_{\bf j}\quad\text{on }\not{\mathfrak{I}}\,.

Using (
4.97
), one has

\displaystyle e_{\bf A}(P)

\displaystyle=e_{\bf A}(P_{\bf B}{}^{\bf B})=\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{\bf A}P_{\bf B}{}^{\bf B}\,,

\displaystyle D_{\bf i}P^{\bf i}{}_{\bf A}

\displaystyle=D_{\perp}P_{\perp\bf A}+D_{\bf B}P^{\bf B}{}_{\bf A}=-\Psi^{-1}P_{\bf A\perp}+\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{\bf B}P^{\bf B}{}_{\bf A}\,,

\displaystyle D_{\bf i}P^{\bf i}{}_{\perp}

\displaystyle=D_{\perp}P_{\perp\perp}+D_{\bf B}P^{\bf B}{}_{\perp}=-\Psi^{-1}P_{\perp\perp}-\not{\mathfrak{K}}_{\bf B}{}^{\bf A}P^{\bf B}{}_{\bf A}\,.

Hence equations (
4.96a
)-(
4.96b
).
∎

Theorem 4.51
.

Let
(\mathcal{S},\not{\mathfrak{I}},\widetilde{h})
be a 3-dimensional aH space with smooth rescaled metrics and let
\widetilde{K}_{ij}
be a smooth symmetric 2-tensor field on
\mathcal{S}\setminus\not{\mathfrak{I}}
such that
(\mathcal{S}\setminus\not{\mathfrak{I}},\widetilde{h},\widetilde{K})
is a solution of the (
4.21
) for
\Lambda=-3
. Then the following two statements are equivalent

i)

for any (and thus all) boundary defining function
\Psi
of
\not{\mathfrak{I}}

a)

the field
P_{ij}:=\Psi\widetilde{K}_{ij}
extends smoothly to
\not{\mathfrak{I}}
and verifies

\left(D_{\perp}P\right)^{\dagger}_{AB}=s_{h}P_{AB}^{\dagger}\quad\text{on }\not{\mathfrak{I}}\,,

(4.98)

b)

all connected components
\mathfrak{S}
of
\not{\mathfrak{I}}
are umbilical with

\not{\mathfrak{K}}_{AB}=s_{h}\;\not{\mathfrak{h}}_{AB}\,,

(4.99)

where

\bullet

D
is the Levi-Civita connection of the rescaled metric
h:=\Psi^{2}\widetilde{h}
,
s_{h}
is the non-extended 3-dimensional Friedrich scalar field of
h
defined by (
4.11
),

\bullet

\not{\mathfrak{h}}
is the metric induced by
h
on
\mathfrak{S}
,
\not{\mathfrak{D}}
is the Levi-Civita connection of
\not{\mathfrak{h}}
,
\not{\mathfrak{K}}
is the extrinsic curvature of
D
on
\mathfrak{S}
,

\bullet

\perp
denotes the outward-pointing normal of
\not{\mathfrak{I}}
with respect to
h
while
A,B
are tangential indices,

\bullet

X^{\dagger}_{AB}
is the trace-free part of the restriction of a 2-tensor
X_{ij}
on
\not{\mathfrak{I}}
,

ii)

for any boundary defining function
\Psi
of
\not{\mathfrak{I}}
, any smooth covector field
\kappa
on
\mathcal{S}
and any smooth scalar field
z
on
\mathcal{S}
which vanishes on
\not{\mathfrak{I}}
, one has

a)

the fields
K_{ij}
,
Q_{ij}
,
E_{ij}
,
T_{i}
,
M_{ijk}
defined by (
4.22
) extend smoothly to
\not{\mathfrak{I}}
,

b)

the 10-tuple
(\mathcal{S},h,E,M,\Psi,\kappa,K,z,T,Q)
verifies the geometric conditions (
4.86
).

Remark
.

From the transformation rules (
4.12
) and these of the extrinsic curvature described in
Section

2.6.3
, one deduces that if
(
4.99
)
holds for one rescaled metric then for all rescaled metric
h
and for all smooth covector field
\kappa

(\widehat{\not{\mathfrak{K}}}_{h})_{AB}=\widehat{s}_{h}\;\not{\mathfrak{h}}_{AB}\,,

where
\widehat{D}
is the Weyl connection associated to
\kappa
with respect to
h
,
\widehat{\not{\mathfrak{K}}}_{h}
is the extrinsic curvature of
\widehat{D}
with respect to
h
on
\mathcal{C}
,
\widehat{s}_{h}
is the extended 3-dimensional Friedrich scalar field defined by (
4.10b
) and
\not{\mathfrak{h}}
is the metric induced by
h
on
\mathcal{C}
.
∎

Corollary 4.52
.

Consider a 3-dimensional compact smooth manifold
\mathcal{S}
with non-empty boundary
\partial\mathcal{S}
. Let
\widetilde{h}_{ij}
be a smooth Riemannian metric on
\mathring{\mathcal{S}}
and let
\widetilde{K}_{ij}
be a smooth symmetric 2-tensor on
\mathring{\mathcal{S}}
such that
i)
and
ii)
of the geometric initial data problem are verified, see
Section

4.4.2
. Then
iii)
of the geometric initial data problem holds if and only if for any (and thus all) boundary defining function
\Psi
of
\partial\mathcal{S}
, the conditions described in
i)
of
4.51
hold.

Remarks
.

•

4.52
implies
4.49
. From
|dx|^{2}_{h}=1
on a neighbourhood of
\partial\mathcal{S}
and (
4.92b
), one has
\theta=1+\mathcal{O}\left({x^{k}}\right)
for some integer
k\geq 1
. Let us analyse the terms in (
4.92a
):

\displaystyle\Delta_{\overline{h}}\theta

\displaystyle=x^{2}\left(\Delta_{h}\theta-h^{-1}(dx,d\theta)x^{-1}\right)=\mathcal{O}\left({x^{k}}\right)\,,

\displaystyle-\overline{r}\theta-6\theta^{5}

\displaystyle=(6+\mathcal{O}\left({x^{3}}\right))(1+\mathcal{O}\left({x^{k}}\right))-6(1+\mathcal{O}\left({x^{k}}\right))=\mathcal{O}\left({x^{\min(3,k)}}\right)\,,

\displaystyle\theta^{-7}\left|x^{3}\phi\right|^{2}_{h}

\displaystyle=\theta^{-7}x^{4}\left|x\phi\right|^{2}_{h}=\mathcal{O}\left({x^{4}}\right)\,.

Hence
k=3
. It follows that the function
\Psi:=x\theta^{-2}
verifies
\left|d\Psi\right|^{2}_{h}-1=\mathcal{O}\left({x^{3}}\right)=\mathcal{O}\left({\Psi^{3}}\right)
. Then (
4.90
) gives

s_{h}=\frac{1}{2\Psi}\left(\left|d\Psi\right|^{2}_{h}-1\right)+\frac{\Psi^{3}}{12}\theta^{-4}\left|S\right|^{2}_{h}=\mathcal{O}\left({\Psi^{2}}\right)\,.

In particular, the gauge choice of
x
in
4.49
implies that
s_{h}=0
on
\partial\mathcal{S}
. Finally, one deduces that (
4.98
) and (
4.99
) reduce to (
4.94
) and (
4.95
) respectively.

•

The hypotheses in
i)
of
4.51
are not sufficient to prevent logarithmic terms in solutions constructed by the conformal method as described in
4.48
. A detailed analysis of the logarithmic terms is given in
[
3
, Section 4]
. In particular, the solutions constructed by the conformal method are smooth on
\mathcal{S}
if and only if certain of the first logarithmic terms, given by
[
3
, Equations (4.13), (4.14) and (4.28)]
for the choice of
x
as in
4.49
, vanish on the boundary
\partial\mathcal{S}
. From this, one can deduce the minimal hypotheses on the field
A_{ij}
so that the solutions are smooth up to the boundary under the hypotheses
i)
of
4.51
. ∎

Proof of
4.51
.

\Longrightarrow
Assume that
i)
is verified. Let
\Psi
be a boundary defining function of
\not{\mathfrak{I}}
, let
\kappa
be a smooth covector field on
\mathcal{S}
and let
z
be a smooth scalar field on
\mathcal{S}
which vanishes on
\not{\mathfrak{I}}
.

•

The field
K_{ij}
defined by (
4.22a
) and the geometric conditions (
4.86
): The condition (
4.86a
) holds by hypothesis on the field
z
. Moreover, the function
z/\Psi
extends smoothly on
\not{\mathfrak{I}}
by
2.11
. By assumption, the field
P_{ij}:=\Psi\widetilde{K}_{ij}
extends smoothly on
\not{\mathfrak{I}}
and the rescaled metric
h_{ij}:=\Psi^{2}\widetilde{h}_{ij}
is smooth on the whole of
\mathcal{S}
. Therefore, the field
K_{ij}
defined by (
4.22a
) extends smoothly on
\not{\mathfrak{I}}
. Furthermore, one has
P_{i\perp}=0
on
\not{\mathfrak{I}}
by
4.50
, which is equivalent to (
4.86b
) by (
4.22a
).

To prove the smoothness of the other fields in (
4.22
), we will work in a frame field
(e_{\bf i})=(e_{\perp},(e_{\bf A}))
adapted to the conformal boundary
\not{\mathfrak{I}}
(see
Section

2.6.1
) with
e_{\perp}
the outward-pointing
h
-unit normal. Since
(\mathcal{S},\not{\mathfrak{I}},\widetilde{h})
is an aH space and
\Psi
is a boundary defining function of
\not{\mathfrak{I}}
, one has
(d\Psi)_{\perp}=-1
on
\not{\mathfrak{I}}
. The idea is to apply
2.11
for each field in a specific order and to decompose the arising conditions in the above frame field. The number of such conditions can be reduced by exploiting the algebraic symmetries.

•

The field
Q_{ij}
defined by (
4.22b
): since
K_{ij}
,
h_{ij}
,
\kappa_{i}
,
z
and
\Psi
are all smooth on
\mathcal{S}
, the smoothness of
Q_{ij}
is equivalent by
2.11
to

-\widehat{D}_{i}(\widehat{\zeta}_{h})_{j}-\kappa_{i}(\widehat{\zeta}_{h})_{j}+zK_{ij}+\widehat{\sigma}h_{ij}=0\quad\text{on }\not{\mathfrak{I}}\,.

Note that the antisymmetric part of the tensor field on the left hand side is equal to
-\Psi\widehat{D}_{[i}\kappa_{j]}
. Thus, it vanishes on
\not{\mathfrak{I}}
. Therefore the above condition is equivalent to

\displaystyle-\widehat{D}_{\bf A}(\widehat{\zeta}_{h})_{\bf B}-\kappa_{\bf A}(\widehat{\zeta}_{h})_{\bf B}+\widehat{s}_{h}h_{\bf AB}

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{on }\not{\mathfrak{I}}

\displaystyle\;\iff\;

\displaystyle(\widehat{\not{\mathfrak{K}}}_{h})_{\bf AB}

\displaystyle=\widehat{s}_{h}\;\not{\mathfrak{h}}_{\bf AB}\,,

\displaystyle-\widehat{D}_{\bf A}(\widehat{\zeta}_{h})_{\perp}-\kappa_{\bf A}(\widehat{\zeta}_{h})_{\perp}+\widehat{s}_{h}h_{\bf A\perp}

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{on }\not{\mathfrak{I}}

\displaystyle\;\iff\;

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle-\widehat{D}_{\perp}(\widehat{\zeta}_{h})_{\perp}-\kappa_{\perp}(\widehat{\zeta}_{h})_{\perp}+\widehat{s}_{h}h_{\perp\perp}

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{on }\not{\mathfrak{I}}

\displaystyle\;\iff\;

\displaystyle e_{\perp}((d\Psi)_{\perp})

\displaystyle=\widehat{s}_{h}\quad\text{on }\not{\mathfrak{I}}\,.

The first condition is ensured by (
4.99
) thanks to the remark below the theorem. The second condition is trivial. Finally, note that equation (
CVC.11
) now holds on the whole of
\mathcal{S}
by continuity. Differentiating it with respect to
e_{\perp}
and evaluating it on
\not{\mathfrak{I}}
gives the third condition.

•

The field
T_{i}
defined by (
4.22d
): since
K_{ij}
,
h_{ij}
,
\kappa_{i}
,
z
and
\Psi
are all smooth on
\mathcal{S}
, the smoothness of
T_{i}
is equivalent by
2.11
to

-(dz)_{i}+K_{ij}h^{jk}(\widehat{\zeta}_{h})_{k}=0\quad\text{on }\not{\mathfrak{I}}\,.

Using the frame field, this can be rewritten under the form

K_{\bf i\perp}-\frac{(dz)_{\perp}}{(d\Psi)_{\perp}}h_{\bf i\perp}=0\quad\text{on }\not{\mathfrak{I}}\,,

which is verified by (
4.86b
) and L’Hôpital’s rule.

•

The field
M_{ijk}
defined by (
4.22e
): since
K_{ij}
,
h_{ij}
and
\kappa_{i}
are all smooth on
\mathcal{S}
, the smoothness of
M_{ijk}
is equivalent by
2.11
to

G_{ijk}(K,\kappa)+h_{k[i}h^{lm}G_{j]lm}(K,\kappa)=0\quad\text{on }\not{\mathfrak{I}}\,.

(4.100)

Note that (
CVC.4
) now holds on the whole of
\mathcal{S}
by continuity. Hence the condition rewrites as

G_{ijk}(K,\kappa)-2h_{k[i}T_{j]}=0\quad\text{on }\not{\mathfrak{I}}\,.

(4.101)

By (
4.22a
) and (
2.54
), one has on
\mathcal{S}

G_{ijk}(K,\kappa)=G_{ijk}(P,\kappa)-2h_{k[i}\left(\left(d\frac{z}{\Psi}\right)\vphantom{\kappa}_{j]}-\kappa_{j]}\frac{z}{\Psi}\right)\,.

Using
2.31
, one has on
\mathcal{S}

G_{ijk}(P,\kappa)=G_{ijk}(P,0)+2h_{k[i}P_{j]l}h^{lm}\kappa_{m}=2D_{[i}P_{j]k}+2h_{k[i}\widetilde{K}_{j]l}h^{lm}\Psi\kappa_{m}\,.

Moreover, from (
4.22d
) and (
4.22a
), one has on
\mathcal{S}

\displaystyle T_{i}

\displaystyle=-\left(d\frac{z}{\Psi}\right)_{i}+\kappa_{i}\frac{z}{\Psi}+\frac{1}{\Psi}\left(K_{i}{}^{j}-\frac{z}{\Psi}\delta_{i}{}^{j}\right)(\widehat{\zeta}_{h})_{j}

\displaystyle=-\left(d\frac{z}{\Psi}\right)_{i}+\kappa_{i}\frac{z}{\Psi}+\widetilde{K}_{ik}h^{jk}(\widehat{\zeta}_{h})_{j}\,.

Hence, (
4.101
) resumes to

2D_{[i}P_{j]k}-2h_{k[i}\widetilde{K}_{j]l}h^{lm}(d\Psi)_{m}=0\quad\text{on }\not{\mathfrak{I}}\,.

(4.102)

It is clear from (
4.100
) that the tensor field on the left hand side is a Cotton candidate on
(\mathcal{S},h)
, see
2.47
. Thus (
4.102
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle 2D_{\bf[A}P_{\bf B]C}+2h_{\bf C[A}\widetilde{K}_{\bf B]\perp}=0\quad\text{on }\not{\mathfrak{I}}\,,

(4.103a)

\displaystyle 2D_{\bf[\perp}P_{\bf A]B}+2h_{\bf B[\perp}\widetilde{K}_{\bf A]\perp}=0\quad\text{on }\not{\mathfrak{I}}\,.

(4.103b)

First consider (
4.103a
). By counting algebraic freedoms, it is equivalent to its trace. Furthermore, since
P_{\bf i\perp}=0
on
\not{\mathfrak{I}}
, one deduces that
D_{\bf A}P_{\bf BC}=\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{\bf A}P_{\bf BC}
. Hence, (
4.103a
) is equivalent to

\widetilde{K}_{\bf A\perp}=2h^{\bf BC}\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{\bf[A}P_{\bf B]C}=\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{\bf A}P_{\bf B}{}^{\bf B}-\not{\mathfrak{D}}_{\bf B}P^{\bf B}{}_{\bf A}\quad\text{on }\not{\mathfrak{I}}\,.

This is equation (
4.96a
) which holds by
4.50
.

Now consider (
4.103b
). Using
P_{\bf i\perp}=0
on
\not{\mathfrak{I}}
and (
4.99
), it is equivalent to

\widetilde{K}_{\perp\perp}h_{\bf AB}=D_{\perp}P_{\bf AB}-s_{h}P_{\bf AB}\quad\text{on }\not{\mathfrak{I}}\,.

Taking the trace gives

2\widetilde{K}_{\perp\perp}=e_{\perp}\left(h^{\bf AB}P_{\bf AB}\right)-s_{h}\,h^{\bf AB}P_{\bf AB}\,.

Using again
P_{\bf i\perp}=0
on
\not{\mathfrak{I}}
, one has

h^{\bf AB}P_{\bf AB}=P\quad\text{on }\not{\mathfrak{I}}\,,

and

e_{\perp}(h^{\bf AB}P_{\bf AB})=e_{\perp}(P)-h^{\bf A\perp}e_{\perp}(P_{\bf A\perp})-h^{\perp\perp}e_{\perp}(P_{\perp\perp})-P_{\bf i\perp}e_{\perp}(h^{\bf i\perp})=e_{\perp}(P)+\widetilde{K}_{\perp\perp}\quad\text{on }\not{\mathfrak{I}}\,.

Hence, one retrieves (
4.96b
) which is again verified by
4.50
. Taking the trace-free part of (
4.103b
) gives (
4.98
) ensured by
i)a)
.

•

The field
E_{ij}
defined by (
4.22c
): since
K_{ij}
,
h_{ij}
,
\kappa_{i}
and
Q_{ij}
are all smooth on
\mathcal{S}
, the smoothness of
E_{ij}
is equivalent by
2.11
to

X_{ij}:=Q_{ij}-\widehat{l}_{ij}-F_{ij}(K)=0\quad\text{on }\not{\mathfrak{I}}\,.

Note that the tensor field
X_{ij}
is symmetric and trace-free on
(\mathcal{S},h)
. By multiplying (
CVC.6
), which holds on
\mathring{\mathcal{S}}
, by the boundary defining function
\Psi
, one has

2(\widehat{\zeta}_{h})_{l}S_{k[i}{}^{lm}X_{j]m}=-\Psi\left(2\widehat{D}_{[i}Q_{j]k}-2K_{k[i}T_{j]}-zM_{ijk}\right)\quad\text{on }\mathring{\mathcal{S}}\,.

Since all the fields appearing in the above equation now extends smoothly, it also holds true on
\not{\mathfrak{I}}
giving

2(\widehat{\zeta}_{h})_{l}S_{k[i}{}^{lm}X_{j]m}=2(\widehat{\zeta}_{h})_{[i}X_{j]k}-2h_{k[i}X_{j]m}h^{lm}(\widehat{\zeta}_{h})_{l}=0\quad\text{on }\not{\mathfrak{I}}\,.

In particular,

\displaystyle 2(\widehat{\zeta}_{h})_{[\perp}X_{\bf A]B}-2h_{\bf B[\perp}X_{\bf A]m}h^{\bf lm}(\widehat{\zeta}_{h})_{\bf l}

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{on }\not{\mathfrak{I}}

\displaystyle\;\iff\;

\displaystyle X_{\bf AB}=0\quad\text{on }\not{\mathfrak{I}}\,,

\displaystyle 2(\widehat{\zeta}_{h})_{\bf[A}X_{\bf B]C}-2h_{\bf C[A}X_{\bf B]m}h^{\bf lm}(\widehat{\zeta}_{h})_{\bf l}

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{on }\not{\mathfrak{I}}

\displaystyle\;\iff\;

\displaystyle X_{\bf A\perp}=0\quad\text{on }\not{\mathfrak{I}}\,.

Therefore, one has
X_{ij}=0
on
\not{\mathfrak{I}}
.

\Longleftarrow
Assume that
ii)
is verified. Let
\Psi
be a boundary defining function of
\not{\mathfrak{I}}
and take
\kappa=0
,
z=0
. Equation (
4.22a
) gives that

P_{ij}:=\Psi\widetilde{K}_{ij}=K_{ij}\qquad\text{on }\mathcal{S}\setminus\not{\mathfrak{I}}\,.

Since the field
K_{ij}
extends smoothly on
\not{\mathfrak{I}}
by
ii)a)
, so does the field
P_{ij}
. The condition (
4.86b
), ensured by
ii)b)
, implies that
P_{i\perp}=0
on
\not{\mathfrak{I}}
.

Using
ii)a)
and
2.11
, one deduces from (
4.22b
) and (
4.22e
) that

\displaystyle D_{i}(d\Psi)_{j}-s_{h}h_{ij}

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{on }\not{\mathfrak{I}}\,,

\displaystyle G_{ijk}(K,0)+h_{k[i}h^{lm}G_{j]lm}(K,0)

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{on }\not{\mathfrak{I}}\,.

In the same manner than above, the first equation implies in particular (
4.99
) and then the second equation implies in particular (
4.98
). Hence the result.
∎

4.5
Main theorem

4.5.1
Precise version of the main theorem

The precise version of our main result on the geometric local existence and uniqueness for the vacuum Einstein equations (
VE
) with
\Lambda<0
,
n=3
and our new geometric boundary conditions (
1.1
) write as follows.

Theorem 4.53

(Main theorem, precise version)
.

Let

•

\mathcal{S}
be a 3-dimensional oriented and compact smooth manifold with non-empty boundary
\partial\mathcal{S}
,

•

\widetilde{h}
be a smooth Riemannian metric on the interior
\mathring{\mathcal{S}}
,

•

\widetilde{K}_{ij}
be a smooth symmetric 2-tensor on the interior
\mathring{\mathcal{S}}
,

•

for each connected component
\mathcal{C}
of
\partial\mathcal{S}
,
\mu_{\mathcal{C}}
be a real number,

such that

H1.

(\mathcal{S},\partial\mathcal{S},\widetilde{h})
is asymptotically hyperbolic, in the sense of
3.2
, with smooth rescaled metrics,

H2.

(\mathring{\mathcal{S}},\widetilde{h}_{ij},\widetilde{K}_{ij})
is a solution to the vacuum constraint equations for the normalised negative cosmological constant
\Lambda=-3
, that is

\displaystyle\widetilde{r}+(\widetilde{K}_{i}{}^{i})^{2}-\widetilde{K}_{ij}\widetilde{K}^{ij}

\displaystyle=-6

\displaystyle\quad\text{on }\mathring{\mathcal{S}}\,,

Remarks
.

•

The first equation in
H3
implies in particular that all connected components
\mathcal{C}
of
\partial\mathcal{S}
are umbilical in the conformal structure
(\mathcal{S},[h])
, where
[h]
is the class of rescaled metrics.

•

One can modify the above theorem to impose an inhomogeneous Dirichlet boundary condition as in Friedrich
[
38
]
on some of the connected components of the conformal boundary, provided that the compatibility conditions are modified accordingly.

•

Since the boundary conditions are reflective, one can construct a past development by changing the time orientation.

•

From the above theorem, one can construct a unique maximal development following Choquet-Bruhat and Geroch
[
19
]
and Sbierski
[
79
]
. ∎

Proof.

Equipped with all the results of
Sections

4.1
,
4.2.4
,
4.3
and
4.4
, the proof mainly follows standard lines. Let us detail the local existence part.

•

Step 1
: Construction of the 10-tuple

Let
\Psi
be a boundary defining function of
\partial\mathcal{S}
, let
\kappa_{i}
be a smooth covector field on
\mathcal{S}
and let
z=0\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{S},\mathbb{R})
. By
4.52
, the hypotheses H1-H3 imply that the 10-tuple
(\mathcal{S},h,E,M,\Psi,\kappa,K,0,\allowbreak T,Q)
, where the fields are defined by (
4.22
), is in
\mathscr{D}
, is solution to the (
4.9
) and verifies the geometric boundary conditions (
4.86
). By choice of
\Psi
and
z
, it also verifies (
4.87a
) and (
4.87b
). Using the gauge transformation
\Weylchg_{\omega}^{\parallel}
defined in
4.12
, one can assume that (
4.87c
) while maintaining (
4.87a
) and (
4.87b
).

•

Step 2
: Construction of the patches and of the analytic initial data
\underline{u}_{\star}

For any point
p\in\mathcal{S}
, one can construct a coordinate system
(y^{i})=(y^{1},y^{2},y^{3})
and an
h
-orthonormal frame field
(e_{\bf i})=(e_{\bf 1},e_{\bf 2},e_{\bf 3})
on a neighbourhood
\mathcal{U}_{\star}(p)
of
p
such that

✦

they are smooth on the closure
\overline{\mathcal{U}_{\star}(p)}
,

✦

y^{3}\geq 0
on
\mathcal{U}_{\star}(p)
and
\partial\mathcal{S}\cap\mathcal{U}_{\star}(p)=\{q\in\mathcal{U}_{\star}(p)\,|\,y^{3}(q)=0\}
,

✦

\partial\mathcal{S}\cap\mathcal{U}_{\star}(p)
is connected, that is
\mathcal{U}_{\star}(p)
intersects at most one connected component
\mathcal{C}
of the boundary
\partial\mathcal{S}
,

✦

if
\partial\mathcal{S}\cap\mathcal{U}_{\star}(p)\neq\varnothing
, the vector field
e_{\bf 3}
is its outward-pointing unit normal with respect to
h
(
e_{\bf 1}
and
e_{\bf 2}
are thus tangent).

By compactness of
\mathcal{S}
, the family
(\mathcal{U}_{\star}(p))_{p\in\mathcal{S}}
admits a finite subcover. Let
\mathcal{U}_{\star}
be a subset of this finite subcover. Construct the analytic initial data
\underline{u}_{\star}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\overline{\mathcal{U}_{\star}},\mathbb{R}^{m})
from the 10-tuple of step 1 as follows:

✦

E_{\bf AB}
,
E_{\bf 3B}
,
H_{\bf AB}
,
H_{\bf 3A}
,
\kappa_{\bf i}
are components of the already defined tensors with respect to the frame field
(e_{\bf i})
and
e_{\bf i}{}^{j}:=\langle dy^{j},e_{\bf i}\rangle
,

✦

\acute{\Gamma}_{\bf i}{}^{\bf j}{}_{\bf k}
are the frame connection coefficients of the Weyl connection associated to
\kappa
with respect to
h
,

✦

\acute{\Gamma}_{\bf i}{}^{\bf 0}{}_{\bf j}:=K_{\bf ij}
,
\acute{\Gamma}_{\bf i}{}^{\bf j}{}_{\bf 0}=\delta^{\bf jk}K_{\bf ik}
,
\acute{\Gamma}_{\bf i}{}^{\bf 0}{}_{\bf 0}:=\kappa_{\bf i}
,
U_{\bf i0}:=T_{\bf i}
,
U_{\bf ij}:=Q_{\bf ij}
,
e_{\bf i}{}^{0}:=0
and
\zeta_{\bf i}:=(d\Psi)_{\bf i}+\Psi\kappa_{\bf i}
.

Let
\Theta_{\star}
be the restriction on
\mathcal{U}_{\star}
of
\Psi
and
s_{\star}
be the restriction on
\mathcal{U}_{\star}
of the field
\widehat{\sigma}
, defined by (
4.10c
), of the 10-tuple of step 1. By (
4.87c
) and
2.11
,
f_{\star}:=s_{\star}/\Theta_{\star}
is smooth and bounded from above on
\mathcal{U}_{\star}
.

•

Step 3
: Evolution system and propagation of the constraints

If
\mathcal{B}_{\star}\neq\varnothing
, take the analytic boundary condition given by (
4.82
) with
\mu
equal to
\mu_{\mathcal{C}}
for the unique connected component
\mathcal{C}
of
\partial\mathcal{S}
intersecting
\mathcal{U}_{\star}
. In all cases, the analytic initial data
\underline{u}_{\star}
verifies all the conditions of
4.34
by step 2 and H4. Applying
4.31
/
4.32
and
4.34
and
4.35
yields a quintuple
(\mathcal{W},\breve{g},V,\Theta,\kappa)\in\mathscr{E}
solution to the (CVE) with
\Lambda=-3
and of conformal boundary
\mathscr{I}=\mathcal{W}\cap\mathcal{B}
. Moreover, if
\mathcal{B}_{\star}\neq\varnothing
,
(\mathcal{W},\mathscr{I},\Theta^{-2}\breve{g})
is an aAdS space.

•

Step 4
: Geometric boundary conditions and patching up

Assume
\mathcal{B}_{\star}\neq\varnothing
. By H4, the analytic initial data
\underline{u}_{\star}
satisfies (
4.85
). Using
ii)
of
4.45
, one can assume that
(\mathcal{W},\mathscr{I},\Theta^{-2}\breve{g})
satisfy the geometric boundary condition (
1.1
) for
\mu=\mu_{\mathcal{C}}
as in step 3, up to reducing
\mathcal{W}
.

Following standard procedures, one can patch up the local solutions on the different open subsets of the finite cover using local uniqueness of
4.31
/
4.32
. This yields a quintuple
(\mathcal{M},g,V,\Theta,\kappa)\in\mathscr{E}
solution to the (CVE) with
\Lambda=-3
. Then, one can verify that
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{S},\Theta^{-2}g)
satisfy
i)-vi)
for the good choices of time-orientation and orientation. ∎

4.5.2
Further comments on the main theorem

\bullet
Positive mass theorems for
\Lambda<0
and
n=3

Recall that the 3-dimensional hyperbolic space
(\mathbb{H}^{3},b)
can be defined by taking the open unit ball of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
equipped with the following Riemannian metric

b:=\frac{4}{(1-r^{2})^{2}}\delta\,,

where
\delta
is the Euclidian metric on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and
r
is the radius in spherical coordinates. Its conformal boundary is the unit circle. One can easily construct a FG gauge using the boundary defining function
\rho:=(1-r)/(1+r)
, giving

b=\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}\left(d\rho^{2}+\frac{(1-\rho^{2})^{2}}{4}d\sigma_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\right)\,.

There have been several proofs of the positive mass theorem in the case of
\Lambda<0
and
n=3
: see Wang
[
87
, Theorem 3.3]
,
Chruściel and Herzlich
[
25
, Theorem 1.5]
. In these theorems, they consider asymptotically hyperbolic spaces for which the least restrictive definition is as follows: an asymptotically hyperbolic space is a pair
(\mathcal{S},\widetilde{h})
, where
\mathcal{S}
is a 3-dimensional smooth manifold with boundary
\partial\mathcal{S}
and
\widetilde{h}
is a Riemannian metric on
\mathring{\mathcal{S}}
, such that there exists two compacts
K\Subset\mathring{\mathcal{S}}
,
K^{\prime}\Subset\mathbb{H}^{3}
and a diffeomorphism
\phi:\mathcal{S}\setminus K\to\mathbb{H}^{3}\setminus K^{\prime}
verifying
\phi_{\star}h-b=\mathcal{O}\left({\rho}\right)
. Hence
\phi_{\star}h
is a perturbation of the hyperbolic metric near the conformal boundary which is diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{S}^{2}
. Moreover, the required decay rate is quite restrictive since it is at the level of the boundary stress-energy tensor in the expansion of the initial metric induced by the FG expansion. This is thus a much stronger condition than our definition of aH spaces, see
3.2
. Consequently, the geometric initial data of
4.53
do not verify the hypotheses of the positive mass theorem, except the trivial case of the hyperbolic space.

\bullet
The orthogonality hypothesis between
\mathcal{S}_{\star}
and
\mathscr{I}

One restriction of
4.53
is that the embedded initial hypersurface
\mathcal{S}_{\star}=\phi(\mathcal{S})
has to be orthogonal to the conformal boundary for all rescaled metrics by
ii)
. If this is not the case, observe that it cannot be asymptotically hyperbolic in the sense of
3.2
because

h^{-1}(d\Psi,d\Psi)=1+z^{2}\neq 1\quad\text{on }\partial\mathcal{S}_{\star}\,,

by (
4.19
). With regard to (partially) solving the geometric initial data problem in this case, the conformal method appears to be sufficiently robust. Note that the asymptotic behaviour of the field
\widetilde{K}_{ij}
is still given by (
4.91
). Furthermore, we strongly expect that an analogue of
4.51
hold, generalising a result of Kánnár
[
62
, Theorem 1]
.

\mathscr{I}
\mathcal{S}
\partial\mathcal{S}
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}

Figure 4
:
Modification of the initial data

As for the geometric initial data problem with an initial hypersurface which is not orthogonal to the conformal boundary, one could either attempt to adapt the proof of
4.53
or to modify the initial data. For the former idea, one could modify the gauge constructed in
4.27
by taking
e_{\bf 0}
a unit transverse vector field to the hypersurface which is tangent to
\mathscr{I}
on
\partial\mathcal{S}_{\star}
. Observe that, from
\acute{\vartheta}_{\bf 00i}=0
, one has
e_{\bf 0}(\breve{g}_{\bf 0i})=0
. For the latter idea, one could:

✦

First solve the vacuum Einstein equations from the initial data set
(\mathring{\mathcal{S}},\widetilde{h}_{ij},\widetilde{K}_{ij})
by the classical results
[
34
,
19
]
giving a maximal globally hyperbolic development
(\widetilde{\mathcal{D}},\widetilde{g}_{\alpha\beta})
.

✦

Modify the hypersurface
\mathring{\mathcal{S}}
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
outside a compact set into a new spacelike hypersurface with mean curvature
\widetilde{K}^{\prime}
tending to
0
at spatial infinity. Attach
\partial\mathcal{S}
to it in order to construct a manifold with boundary denoted by
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.

✦

Apply
4.53
to the initial data set composed of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
and
\widetilde{h}^{\prime}_{ij}
,
\widetilde{K}^{\prime}_{ij}
the first and second fundamental forms of
\mathring{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
in
(\widetilde{\mathcal{D}},\widetilde{g}_{\alpha\beta})
.

The construction is illustrated in
Figure

4
, in the conformal picture and with embedded hypersurfaces for simplicity. The timelike conformal boundary
\mathscr{I}
resulting from
4.53
is represented for clarity. For this method to work, one has to prove a propagation result of both the smoothness of the unphysical fields and of the compatibility conditions from
\mathcal{S}
to
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.

\bullet
Link with the boundary conditions for the Teukolsky equations

The geometric homogeneous Dirichlet boundary condition translate into certain boundary conditions for the Teukolsky equations on aAdS black holes, see for instance
[
50
, Section 6.3]
for AdS and
[
42
, Proposition 2.2]
for SAdS. In what follows, we will determine the boundary conditions corresponding to the homogeneous Robin boundary conditions (
1.1
). Construct a null frame
(e^{\bullet}_{\bf a})
for the rescaled metric by setting

e^{\bullet}_{\bf A}:=e_{\bf A}\,,\quad e^{\bullet}_{\bf 3}:=e_{\bf 0}-e_{\bf 3}\,,\quad e^{\bullet}_{\bf 4}:=e_{\bf 0}+e_{\bf 3}\,.

In the null decomposition of the Weyl tensor, see
[
20
, Equations (4.8)]
, one has

\alpha_{\bf AB}:=W(e^{\bullet}_{\bf A},e^{\bullet}_{\bf 4},e^{\bullet}_{\bf B},e^{\bullet}_{\bf 4})\,,\qquad\underline{\alpha}_{\bf AB}:=W(e^{\bullet}_{\bf A},e^{\bullet}_{\bf 3},e^{\bullet}_{\bf B},e^{\bullet}_{\bf 3})\,.

These are symmetric trace-free smooth 2-tensors on the distribution
\mathcal{D}^{2}=\vectorspan(e_{\bf 1},e_{\bf 2})
, that is they are in
\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{D}^{2})
as defined in
Section

2.5.5
. By definition of the rescaled Weyl tensor and the electromagnetic decomposition, see
2.62
, one deduces that

\alpha_{\bf AB}=-2\Theta\left(E^{\dagger}_{\bf AB}+(\star H^{\dagger})_{\bf AB}\right)\,,\qquad\underline{\alpha}_{\bf AB}=-2\Theta\left(E^{\dagger}_{\bf AB}-(\star H^{\dagger})_{\bf AB}\right)\,.

Hence the boundary conditions (
4.81
) rewrite under the form

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}^{\dagger}_{\bf AB}=0\;\;\text{on }\mathscr{I}

\displaystyle\iff\frac{2}{3}E^{\dagger}_{\bf AB}-\mu H^{\dagger}_{\bf AB}=0\;\;\text{on }\mathscr{I}

\displaystyle\iff\lim_{\Theta\to 0}\Theta^{-1}\left(\frac{2}{3}(\alpha+\underline{\alpha})_{\bf AB}-\mu\left(\star(\alpha-\underline{\alpha})\right)_{\bf AB}\right)=0

\displaystyle\iff\lim_{\Theta\to 0}\Theta^{-1}\left(\frac{2}{3}\left(\star(\alpha+\underline{\alpha})\right)_{\bf AB}-\mu(\alpha-\underline{\alpha})_{\bf AB}\right)=0\,.

In the limit case
\mu\to+\infty
, one retrieves
[
42
, Equation (31)]
, that is
8
8

8

The discrepancy in powers of
\Theta
, with
-1
instead of
-3
, is easily explained by our use of a frame field for the rescaled metric instead of the physical metric.

\lim_{\Theta\to 0}\Theta^{-1}\left(\alpha-\underline{\alpha}\right)_{\bf AB}=0\,.

The second boundary condition follows from the second Bianchi identity and, more precisely, from the evolution equations (
4.40a
) and (
4.40c
). One has

\displaystyle e_{\bf 0}\left(\mathfrak{p}^{\dagger}_{\bf AB}\right)=0\;\;\text{on }\mathscr{I}

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle e_{\bf 0}\left(\frac{2}{3}E^{\dagger}_{\bf AB}-\mu H^{\dagger}_{\bf AB}\right)=0\;\;\text{on }\mathscr{I}

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle e_{\bf 3}\left(\frac{2}{3}(\star H^{\dagger})_{\bf AB}+\mu(\star E^{\dagger})_{\bf AB}\right)-\not{\epsilon}_{\bf(A|}{}^{\bf C}e_{\bf C}\left(\frac{2}{3}H_{\bf 3|B)}+\mu E_{\bf 3|B)}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{2}{3}N^{\dagger}_{\bf AB}(E,H)-\mu N^{\dagger}_{\bf AB}(H,-E)\;\;\text{on }\mathscr{I}

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle e_{\bf 3}\left(\frac{2}{3}(\star H^{\dagger})_{\bf AB}+\mu(\star E^{\dagger})_{\bf AB}\right)-\not{\epsilon}_{\bf(A|}{}^{\bf C}e_{\bf C}\left(\frac{2}{3}H_{\bf 3|B)}+\mu E_{\bf 3|B)}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=N^{\dagger}_{\bf AB}\left(\frac{2}{3}E-\mu H,\frac{2}{3}H+\mu E\right)\;\;\text{on }\mathscr{I}\,.

Note that the geometric homogeneous Robin boundary condition also implies that

\displaystyle\frac{2}{3}H_{\bf 3B}+\mu E_{\bf 3B}

\displaystyle=0\;\;\text{on }\mathscr{I}\,,

\displaystyle\frac{2}{3}E_{\bf 33}-\mu H_{\bf 33}

\displaystyle=0\;\;\text{on }\mathscr{I}\,.

Since
e_{\bf A}
are tangent to
\mathscr{I}
, one deduces that

e_{\bf 3}\left(\Theta^{-1}\left(\frac{2}{3}(\alpha-\underline{\alpha})_{\bf AB}+\mu(\star(\alpha+\underline{\alpha}))_{\bf AB}\right)\right)=L^{(\mu)}_{\bf AB}(\alpha,\underline{\alpha})\quad\text{on }\mathscr{I}\,,

for some linear function
L^{(\mu)}:\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{D}^{2})\times\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{D}^{2})\to\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{D}^{2})
. Finding the explicit expression of
L^{(\mu)}
would require a further study but one can expect simplifications using the boundary condition (
1.1
), the gauge properties (
4.86
)-(
4.87
) and (
4.99
).
